Homological Mirror Symmetry Course at SIMIS: Introduction and Applications
Veronica Pasquarella

Abstract
The present work consists of topics covered through a course currently taught by the author at SIMIS.



1
Lagrangian Floer cohomology

The Novikov field
\Lambda
over a basis field
\mathbb{k}
is

\Lambda\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left\{\ \sum_{{}_{i=0}}^{{}^{\infty}}a_{{}_{i}}T^{{}^{\lambda_{{}_{i}}}}\ \bigg|\ a_{{}_{i}}\in\mathbb{k},\lambda_{{}_{i}}\in\mathbb{R},\underset{i\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\ \lambda_{{}_{i}}=+\infty\ \right\}.

(1)

Denoting by

\chi(L_{{}_{o}},L_{{}_{1}})\ =\ L_{{}_{o}}\cap L_{{}_{1}}

(2)

the set of generators of the Floer complex, the latter can be defined as follows

CF(L_{{}_{o}},L_{{}_{1}})\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \bigoplus_{{}_{p\in\chi(L_{{}_{o}},L_{{}_{1}})}}\Lambda\circ p.

(3)

For this to be a chain complex, we need to define the Floer differential and the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading of the Floer complex.

2
Moduli space of holomorphic disks

2.1
Gromov-Witten invariants

A CohFT, then consists of a system

\Omega\ =\ (\Omega_{{}_{g,n}})_{{}_{2g-2+n>0}}

(4)

of tensors

\Omega_{{}_{g,n}}\ \in\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(\overline{\mathfrak{M}}_{{}_{g,n}},\mathbb{Q}\right)\otimes(V^{\bullet})^{\otimes n},

(5)

associating a cohomology in
H^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(\overline{\mathfrak{M}}_{{}_{g,n}},\mathbb{Q}\right)
to vectors
v_{{}_{1}},...,v_{{}_{n}}\in V
assigned to the
n
markings.

In the context of HMS, they are particularly useful for calculations involving the A-side, as explained in what follows.

To define Gromov-Witten invariants of a symplectic manifold,
{\cal M}
, consider moduli spaces of stable homolorphic maps,

\overline{\cal M}_{{}_{g,n,\beta}}(M)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left\{u:\Sigma_{{}_{g}}\rightarrow X\ \text{holomorphic},\ u[\Sigma_{{}_{g}}]=\beta\right\}\ /\sim,

(6)

there are maps

\overline{\cal M}_{{}_{g,n}}\ \longleftarrow\ \overline{\cal M}_{{}_{g,n,\beta}}\ \longrightarrow\ M^{{}^{n}}

(7)

\Rightarrow\ \ \ I_{{}_{g,n,\beta}}:\ \ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(M)^{{}^{\otimes n}}\ \rightarrow\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(\overline{\cal M}_{{}_{g,n,\beta}}\right).

(8)

Then, the maps

I_{{}_{g,n}}\ =\ \sum_{{}_{\beta\in H_{{}_{2}}(M)}}r^{{}^{\omega(\beta)}}\ I_{{}_{g,n,\beta}}

(9)

form a
\mathbb{C}[[r]]
-linear cohomological field theory.

3
Fukaya categories

3.1
Lagrangian submanifolds

A Lagrangian submanifold of a symplectic manifold is a maximally isotropic submanifold, hence one on which the symplectic form vanishes, and which is maximal with this property.

Example
For
X\equiv\mathbb{R}^{{}^{2n}}
equipped with a canonical symplectic form,
1
,

\omega\ =\ \sum_{{}_{i=1}^{{}^{n}}}dq_{{}_{i}}\wedge dp^{{}^{i}}

(10)

standard Lagrangian submanifolds are submanifolds
\mathbb{R}^{{}^{n}}\ \hookrightarrow\ \mathbb{R}^{{}^{2n}}
of fixed values of the
\left\{q_{{}_{i}}\right\}_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{n}}
coordinates. Locally, every Lagrangian submanifold looks like this.

def.
A Lagrangian submanifold of a symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
is a submanifold
L\hookrightarrow X
such that the following equivalent conditions hold:

1.

At each point
\ell\in L
, the tangent space

T_{{}_{\ell}}L\ \hookrightarrow\ T_{{}_{\ell}}X

(11)

is a Lagrangian subspace of
T_{{}_{\ell}}X
equipped with the symplectic form
\omega_{{}_{X}}
.

2.

L\hookrightarrow X
is a maximal isotropic submanifold.

Remark (more advanced):
More generally, one can consider Lagrangian subamnifolds of symplectic structures in higher geometry, such as symplectic Lie n-algebroids equipped with their canonical invariant polynomials, thought of as dg-manifolds via their CE-algebra, and equipped with graded symplectic forms. Such structures are also referred to as
\Lambda
-structures, and a foliation by such leaves constitutes a Lagrangian foliation of a Lie algebroid.

Types of Lagrangian submanifolds

Lagrangian submanifolds come into four main types, namely: minimal, calibrated, special, and graded.

•

Minimal:
A minimal submanifold is a submanifold such that small deformations of its embedding do not affect its volume.

•

Calibrated:
Let
M
be a Riemann manifold, and vol
{}_{{}_{V}}
denote the induced volume for any given subspace
V\subset T_{{}_{z}}M
. A
k
-form is said to be calibrated on
M
if it is closed and for all oriented
k
-dimensional subspaces
V\subset T_{{}_{z}}M,\eta|_{{}_{V}}=\lambda\text{vol}_{{}_{V}}
for some
\lambda\leq 1
. A submanifold
N\hookrightarrow M
is
\eta
-calibrated if
\forall z\in N
,
\eta|_{{}_{T_{{}_{z}}N}}=\text{vol}_{{}_{V}}
.

Proposition
Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold,
\eta
a calibration on
M
, and
N
a compact
\eta
-calibrated submanifold of
M
. Then
N
is volume minimising in its homology class.
This tells us that the volume of calibrated submanifolds is unchanged by small variations of the embedding, thereby implying that calibrated submanifolds are minimal.

•

Special:
Let
M
be a complex
n
-dimensional CY manifold with CY form
\Omega
and K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler form
\omega
. The volume form induced by the K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler metric is then

\frac{\omega^{{}^{n}}}{n!}\ =\ \frac{1}{n!}\sum dz

(12)

with
\omega|_{{}_{L}}=0
(from the condition of a submanifold to be Lagrangian).

CYs are compact by definition. Therefore, all holomorphic volume forms on them are locally constant. We can certainly scale
\Omega
(uniquely up to a phase), such that

\frac{\omega^{{}^{n}}}{n!}\ =\ (-1)^{{}^{n(n-2)/2}}\left(\frac{i}{2}\right)^{{}^{n}}\Omega\wedge\overline{\Omega}.

(13)

Then, the scaled
\Omega
is the unique CY form which is a tivial calibration on
M
. For any Lagrangian submanifold
L\hookrightarrow M
,

\text{vol}_{{}_{L}}\ =\ e^{{}^{i\pi\theta}}\Omega_{{}_{L}}

(14)

for some
\theta:L\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, referred to as the phase of
L
. If
\theta
=const., then the given Lagrangian submanifold is termed
special
.

Likewise, we could define them as the submanifolds of
M
calibrated by Re
(\Omega)
under some choice of a phase (i.e. the phase of
L
).

Example
All special Lagrangian submanidfolds of a complex 1D CY manifold (i.e. an elliptic curve) are geodesics. However, the converse is also true, since geodesics are submanifolds of constant phase.

•

Graded:

\theta
is unique up to an integer shift. Given a fixed
\Omega
, if
\theta
=const. implies
L
is special, this choice is called grading of
L
.

A graded special Lagrangian submanifold therefore corresponds to a pair
(L,\theta)
. This corresponds to the concept of grading used later on in the context of HMS for elliptic curves.

The shift

(L,\theta)[n]\ \overset{def.}{=}\ (L,\theta+n)

(15)

mirrors the
n^{{}^{th}}
-shift functor on complexes of coherent sheaves.

The shift

(L,\theta)\ \mapsto\ (L,\theta+1)

(16)

corresponds to reversing the orientation of
L
induced by vol
{}_{{}_{L}}
, since

\text{vol}_{{}_{L}}\ \mapsto\ -\text{vol}_{{}_{L}}\ \ \ \ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \ \ e^{{}^{i\pi\theta}}\ \mapsto\ e^{{}^{-i\pi\theta}}.

(17)

The notion of grading can be generalised to other Lagrangian submanifolds by defining the
average phase
of
L
,
\phi(L)
, given modulo
\mathbb{Z}
by the holomorphic ratio

\frac{\int_{{}_{L}}\Omega}{\int_{{}_{L}}\text{vol}_{{}_{L}}}\ =\ e^{{}^{i\pi\phi(L)}},

(18)

which is invariant under Hamiltonian deformations of
L
. Assuming
L
is homologous to a special Lagrangian submanifold, matches to the aforementioned restricted concept of grading.

3.2
Lagrangian Floer cohomology

3.2.1
Floer differential

The Floer differential is defined by the pseudo-holomorphic curves bounded by
L_{{}_{0}},L_{{}_{1}}
, representing the worldsheet instantons in Physics. Specifically, let
J
be an
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure on
M
, namely, an almost complex structure such that

g_{{}_{J}}(.,.)\ \equiv\ \omega(.,J.)

(19)

is a Riemannian metric.

Consider the space of pseudoholomorphic curves

u:\ \Sigma=\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\ \rightarrow\ M

(20)

meaning the curve
u
satisfies the complex linear condition

J\circ du\ =\ du\ \circ J_{{}_{o}},

(21)

with
J_{{}_{o}}
denoting the almost complex structure of
\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{C}
. The complex linear condition is equivalent to the Cauchy-Riemann equation

\overline{\partial}_{{}_{J}}\ =\ 0\ \ \ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \ \ \frac{\partial u}{\partial s}+J(u)\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}\ =\ 0

(22)

with
(s,t)
denoting real coordinates of
\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{C}
.

def.
Let
\hat{\cal M}(p,q;[u],J)
denote the
moduli space
of pseudoholomorphic strips

u:\ \mathbb{R}\times[0,1]\ \rightarrow\ (M,J)

(23)

in the homotopy class
[u]\in\pi_{{}_{2}}(M,L_{{}_{0}}\cup L_{{}_{1}})
with Lagrangian boundary conditions

\begin{cases}&u(s,0)\ \in\ L_{{}_{0}},\ u(s,1)\ \in\ L_{{}_{1}}\ \forall s\ \in\ \mathbb{R}\\
&\underset{s\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\ u(s,t)\ =\ p,\ \underset{s\rightarrow-\infty}{\lim}\ u(s,t)\ =\ q\end{cases}

(24)

and finite energy condition

E(u)\ =\ \omega([u])\ =\ \int\ u^{{}^{*}}\omega\ =\ \int\left|\frac{\partial u}{\partial s}\right|^{{}^{2}}dt\ ds\ <\ \infty.

(25)

We can further simplify the moduli space by quotienting the action of
\mathbb{R}
-translations on it. This can be readily checked by taking
a\in\mathbb{R}
and noticing that

(a\cdot u)(s,t)\ =\ u(s-a,t).

(26)

Then, the orbit action of
\mathbb{R}
-translations provide the quotiented moduli space, that we will be denoting as
{\cal M}(p,q;[u],J)
.

Remark:

Let
D_{{}_{\overline{\partial}_{{}_{J}},u}}
be the linearisation of
\overline{\partial}_{{}_{J}}
at a given strip
u
. The linearised operator
D_{{}_{\overline{\partial}_{{}_{J}},u}}
is a 1
{}^{{}^{st}}
-order differential operator, meaning that it maps to a section of
u^{{}^{*}}TM
satisfying the Lagrangian boundary conditions specified above to a
u^{{}^{*}}TM
-valued
(0,1)
-form on
\Sigma
, namely a section of the bundle
u^{{}^{*}}TM\otimes\Lambda^{{}^{(0,1)}}T\Sigma
.

The lienarised operator is also a Fredholm operator, whose index is defined by

\text{ind}_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}D_{{}_{\overline{\partial}_{{}_{J}},u}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \text{dim\ Ker}D_{{}_{\overline{\partial}_{{}_{J}},u}}-\text{dim\ coKer}D_{{}_{\overline{\partial}_{{}_{J}},u}}.

(27)

If its action on
\hat{\cal M}
is surjective, then,

\text{dim}\hat{\cal M}\ =\ \text{ind}_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}D_{{}_{\overline{\partial}_{{}_{J}},u}}

(28)

as a Fredholm operator
1
1

1

This condition is ensured by performing Hamiltonian perturbation, if not transversally-intersecting.
.

The Floer differential

\partial:\ CF(L_{{}_{o}},L_{{}_{1}})\ \rightarrow\ CF(L_{{}_{o}},L_{{}_{1}})

(29)

is the
\Lambda
-linear map defined by

\partial(p)\ =\ \underset{[u]:\text{ind}([u])=1}{\sum_{{}_{q\in\chi(L_{{}_{o}},L_{{}_{1}})}}}\left(n.{\cal M}(p,q;[u],J)\right)T^{{}^{\omega([u])}}q,

(30)

where
n.{\cal M}(p,q;[u],J)
denotes the number of points in
{\cal M}(p,q;[u],J)
. The physical meaning of implementing the Floer differential is the tunnelling of an open string at one point to another, as shown in the Figure.

The most important theorem in Floer cohomology is
\partial^{2}=0
. The proof of this identity relies on Gromov’s compactness theorem.

3.2.2
Relation to Morse Theory

Let
X
be a complete Riemannian manifold, not necessarily of finite dimension. Consider a smooth function
f
on
X
, i.e.
f\in C^{{}^{\infty}}(X,\mathbb{R})
. The essential feature of Morse Theory and its generalisations is the relationship between the structure of the critical set of
f
,

C(f)\ \equiv\ \{x\in X:df(x)=0\}

(31)

and the topology of
X
. Morse theory yields very interesting results for functions on finite-dimensional Riemann manifolds.

However, it can also be extended to infinite-dimensional situations.

Examples

Given a Riemannian manifold,
X
, diffeomorphic to the 2-sphere,
S^{{}^{2}}
, and studying smooth functions on
X
, more specifically, taking two functions
f_{{}_{1}},f_{{}_{2}}
whose leve; set graphs are exhibited in the following figure, with the vertical axis describing the value of the functions.

The idea of Morse Theory is that of extracting information about the global topology of
X
from the critical points of
f
, i.e. from the points
p\in X
with
df(p)=0
. This is not invariant: for
f_{{}_{1}}
, there are two critical points, for
f_{{}_{2}}
there are four. The local geometry of the function in the vicinity of a critical point requires assigning a Morse index
\mu(p)
to each critical point
p
as the number of linearly-independent directions on which the second derivative
d^{{}^{2}}f(p)
is negative-definite. Equivalently, this is the dimension of the
unstable manifold
,
W^{{}^{u}}(p)
.

Such unstable manifold is defined as follows: consider the solutions of

x:\ \mathbb{R}\ \longrightarrow\ M\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \dot{x}(t)\ =\ -\text{grad}\ f(x(t))\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \forall t\in\mathbb{R}.

(32)

At this point, define the gradient of
f
as the vector field dual to the 1-form
df
. The flow lines
x(t)
are curves of steepest descent for
f
. For
t\rightarrow\pm\infty
, each flow line
x(t)
converges to some critical points
p=x(-\infty),q=x(\infty)
of
f
. The unstable manifold
W^{{}^{u}}(p)
of a critical point
p
then simply consists of all flow lines
x(t)
with
x(-\infty)=p
, i.e. those flow lines that emanate from
p
.

In these examples,

\mu_{{}_{f_{{}_{1}}}}\left(p_{{}_{1}}\right)\ =\ 2\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \mu_{{}_{f_{{}_{1}}}}\left(p_{{}_{2}}\right)\ =\ 0,

(33)

and for
f_{{}_{2}}

\mu_{{}_{f_{{}_{2}}}}\left(p_{{}_{1}}\right)\ =\ 2\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \mu_{{}_{f_{{}_{2}}}}\left(p_{{}_{2}}\right)\ =\ 2\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \mu_{{}_{f_{{}_{2}}}}\left(p_{{}_{3}}\right)\ =\ 1\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \mu_{{}_{f_{{}_{2}}}}\left(p_{{}_{4}}\right)\ =\ 0.

(34)

This follows from the fact that
f_{{}_{1}}
has a maximum point,
p_{{}_{1}}
, and a minimum,
p_{{}_{2}}
, as its only critical points, whereas
f_{{}_{2}}
has two local maxima,
p_{{}_{1}},p_{{}_{2}}
, a saddle point,
p_{{}_{3}}
, and a minimum,
p_{{}_{4}}
.

The unstable manifold is therefore topologically a cell (i.e. homeomorphic to an open ball),
W^{{}^{u}}(p)
, of dimensions
\mu(p)
, and the manifold
X
is the union of the unstable manifolds of the critical points of the function. Thus, we get a decomposition of
X
into cells.

To see the local effects of critical points, we need to intersect
W^{{}^{u}}(p)
with a small ball around
p
, and contract the boundary of that intersection to a point. We then obtain a pointed sphere
\left(S^{{}^{\mu(p)}},\text{pt.}\right)
of dimension
\mu(p)
. These local constructions already yield an important topological invariant, namely the Euler characteristic
\chi(X)
, as the alternating sum of these dimensions

\chi(X)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{p}}(-1)^{{}^{\mu(p)}}\mu(p),

(35)

where
p
are the critical points of
f
. The signs
(-1)^{{}^{\mu(p)}}
are introduced to get some cancellations between the contributions from the individual critical points. This issue is handled in more generality by introducing the boundary operator
\partial
. From the point of view of Floer theory, consider pairs
(p,q)
of critical points with
\mu(q)=\mu(p)-1
, namely of index difference 1. We then count the number of trajectories from
p
to
q
mod 2

\partial p\ =\ \sum_{{}_{q}}\ \left(\text{number of lines from}\ p\ \text{to}\ q\ \text{mod 2}\right)\ q.

(36)

This way, we can get an automorphism from the vector space over
\mathbb{Z}_{{}_{2}}
generated by the critical points of
f
to itself,
C_{{}_{*}}\left(f,\mathbb{Z}_{{}_{2}}\right)
. The crucial point is then to show that

\partial\ \circ\ \partial\ =\ 0.

(37)

On this basis, one can define the homology groups

H_{{}_{k}}(X,f,\mathbb{Z})\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \text{ker}(\partial)_{{}_{C_{{}_{k}}(f,\mathbb{Z}_{{}_{2}})}}\bigg/\text{im}(\partial)_{{}_{C_{{}_{k+1}}(f,\mathbb{Z}_{{}_{2}})}},

(38)

where
C_{{}_{k}}(f,\mathbb{Z}_{{}_{2}})
is generated by the critical points of Morse index
k
.

3.3
Gromov’s compactness theorem

Any sequence of pseudo holomorphic curves with uniformly bounded energy has a subsequence that converges to nodal configurations up to reparametrisation.

For pseudoholomorphic strips satisfying Lagrangian boundary conditions, there are three types of admissible configurations:

1.

Broken strips.

Figure 1:

Energy concentrates at either end,
s\rightarrow\pm\infty
, meaning that there is a sequence
a_{{}_{n}}\rightarrow\pm\infty
such that the translated strips
u_{{}_{n}}(s-a_{{}_{n}},t)
converge to a non-constant limit strip.

2.

The disk bubble.

Figure 2:

Energy concentrates at a point on the boundary of the strips, where suitable rescalings of
u_{{}_{n}}
converge to a pseudoholomorphic disk in
{\cal M}
with boundary entirely contained in, either,
L_{{}_{o}}
or
L_{{}_{1}}
.

3.

The sphere bubble.

Energy concentrates at an interior point of the strip, where suitable rescalings of
u_{{}_{n}}
converge to a pseudoholomorphic sphere in
M
.

Figure 3:

The broken strip constitutes the nontrivial contribution to the proof of the identity
\partial^{{}^{2}}=0
. This is because, both, the disk bubbles and the sphere bubbles can be regarded as elements in
\pi_{{}_{2}}(M,L_{{}_{i}})
. As such, the symplectic area of all bubbles vanishes.

Considering only homotopy class
[u]
of Maslov index 2, according to Gromov’s compactness theorem, the boundaries of the compactified moduli space
\overline{\cal M}(p,q;[u],J)
consists of broken strips connecting
p
to
q
in the class
[u]
. The components of the broken strips correspond to the moduli spaces
{\cal M}(p,r;[u^{{}^{\prime}}],J)
and
{\cal M}(r,q;[u^{{}^{\prime\prime}}],J)
where
r
denotes any generator of the Floer complex, and the relevant homotopy classes satisfy the following relation

\left[u^{{}^{\prime}}\right]+\left[u^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\right]\ =\ \left[u\right].

(39)

Since the Maslov index is additive under the addition of homotopy classes,

\text{dim}{\cal M}(p,q;[u],J)\ =\ \text{ind}([u])-1

(40)

the Maslov index of a non-compact strip must be at least 1. Thus, the only possibility is a broken strip consisting of two components of Maslov index 1. Furthermore, the gluing theorem states that every broken strip is locally the limit of a unique family of index 2 strips.

Hence, the boundaries of the compactified moduli space is precisely the case of broken strips, and each broken strip consists of two strips of Maslov index 1.

Overall, we get

\partial\overline{\cal M}(p,q;[u],J)\ =\ \underset{\left[u^{{}^{\prime}}\right]+\left[u^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\right]\ =\ \left[u\right]}{\bigsqcup_{{}_{r\in\chi(L_{{}_{o}},L_{{}_{1}})}}}\left({\cal M}(p,r;[u^{{}^{\prime}}],J)\ \times\ {\cal M}(p,q;[u^{{}^{\prime\prime}}],J)\right),

(41)

with unit indices

\text{ind}\left(\left[u^{{}^{\prime}}\right]\right)\ =\ \text{ind}\left(\left[u^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\right]\right)\ =\ 1.

(42)

The total number of boundary points of a compact 1-manifold is always zero mod 2, hence,

\displaystyle\partial^{{}^{2}}p

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{{}_{...}}\left(n.{\cal M}\left(p,r;\left[u^{{}^{\prime}}\right],J\right)\right)T^{{}^{\omega\left(\left[u^{{}^{\prime}}\right]\right)}}\partial r

(43)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{}_{r\in\chi\left(L_{{}_{o}},\chi_{{}_{1}}\right)}}\sum_{{}_{q\in\chi\left(L_{{}_{o}},\chi_{{}_{1}}\right)}}\left(n.{\cal M}\left(p,r;\left[u^{{}^{\prime}}\right],J\right)\right)\left(n.{\cal M}\left(p,r;\left[u^{{}^{\prime}}\right],J\right)\right)T^{{}^{\omega\left(\left[u^{{}^{\prime}}\right]\right)+\omega\left(\left[u^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\right]\right)}}q

\displaystyle=0.

Figure 4:

3.4
Products

Multiproducts of Floer complexes are required to achieve an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-structure on the Floer cohomology. Consider intersections

p_{{}_{i}}\ \in\ L_{{}_{i-1}}\cap L_{{}_{i}}\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ q\ \in\ L_{{}_{o}}\ \cap\ L_{{}_{k}}\ \ \ \ ,i=1,...,k,

(44)

and choose
k+1
fixed points on the boundary of
D^{{}^{2}}
. Let
\hat{\cal M}\left(p_{{}_{1}},...,p_{{}_{k}},q;[u],J\right)
be the moduli space of pseudoholomorphic curves

u:\ D^{{}^{2}}\ \longrightarrow\ M

(45)

of finite energy is the homotopy class

[u]\ \in\ \pi_{{}_{2}}\left(M,\bigcup_{{}_{i=0}}^{{}^{k}}L_{{}_{i}}\right).

(46)

For example, for
k=2
, there are 3 Lagrangians and 3 intesection points. The Maslov index, in this case, reads

\text{Ind}([u])\ =\ \text{deg}(q)-\sum_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{k}}\text{deg}\left(p_{{}_{i}}\right)

(47)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ \ \ \text{dim}{\cal M}

\displaystyle=\ \text{dim}\hat{\cal M}\ +\ (k+1)-3

(48)

\displaystyle=\ \text{Ind}([u])+k-2.

Multi-product operation

\mu_{{}_{k}}:\ CF\left(L_{{}_{k-1}},L_{{}_{k}}\right)\ \otimes\ ...\ \otimes\ CF\left(L_{{}_{0}},L_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ CF\left(L_{{}_{0}},L_{{}_{k}}\right)

(49)

are
\Lambda
-linear maps defined as follows

\mu_{{}_{k}}\left(p_{{}_{k}},...,p_{{}_{1}}\right)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{q\in\ \chi(L_{{}_{o}},L_{{}_{k}})}}\left(n.{\cal M}\left(p_{{}_{1}},...,p_{{}_{k}},q;[u],J\right)\right)\ T^{{}^{\omega([u])}}\ q.

(50)

3.5
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-categories

Multilinear maps
\mu_{{}_{k}}
of degree
2-k

\mu_{{}_{k}}:\ \text{Hom}_{{}_{\cal A}}\left(A_{{}_{k-1}},A_{{}_{k}}\right)\ \otimes\ ...\ \otimes\ \text{Hom}_{{}_{\cal A}}\left(A_{{}_{0}},A_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Hom}_{{}_{\cal A}}\left(A_{{}_{0}},A_{{}_{k}}\right)\ [2-k],

(51)

satisfying the
A_{{}_{\infty}}
relations

\sum_{{}_{l=1}}^{{}^{k}}\sum_{{}_{j=0}}^{{}^{k-l}}\ (-1)^{{}^{\sigma}}\mu_{{}_{k+1-l}}\left(f_{{}_{1}},...f_{{}_{j+l+1}},\mu_{{}_{l}}\left(f_{{}_{j+l}},...,f_{{}_{j+1}}\right),f_{{}_{j}},...,f_{{}_{1}}\right)\ =\ 0,

(52)

where

\sigma\ =\ j+\text{deg}\left(f_{{}_{1}}\right)+...+\text{deg}\left(f_{{}_{j}}\right)\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ f_{{}_{i}}\ \in\ \text{Hom}_{{}_{\cal A}}\left(A_{{}_{i-1}},A_{{}_{i}}\right)\ \ \ \ \text{for}\ \ i=1,...,k.

(53)

3.6
Graded Lagrangian submanifolds

\mathbb{Z}
-grading of Floer complex.

In order to give a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading of the generators of teh floer complex, we need some extra data of the Lagrangian manifolds
L_{{}_{o}},L_{{}_{1}}
: a choice of the graded lift of the canonical section of the Lagrangian submanifold defines a
graded Lagrangian submanifold
.

Two conditions need to be met to ensure the existence of such lifts:

1.

The 1
{}^{{}^{st}}
Chern class of
M
must be 2-torsion, namely

2c_{{}_{1}}(TM)\ =\ 0.

(54)

2.

The Maslov class of
L_{{}_{i}}
,

\mu_{{}_{L_{{}_{i}}}}\ \in\ H^{{}^{1}}\left(L_{{}_{i}},\mathbb{Z}\right)

(55)

must be 0 for
i=0,1
.

Some preliminary material

Denoting by LGr
(TM)
the Lagrangian Grassmann of
M
, i.e. the fiber of LGr
(TM)
is the Lagrangian Grassmannian of
T_{{}_{p}}M
, to construct the
grading structure
of Lagrangian submanifolds, we need to construct a map

\Omega:\ \text{LGr}(TM)\ \rightarrow\ S^{{}^{1}},

(56)

corresponding to the existence of the following commutative diagram

LGr
(n)
denotes the
Lagrangian Grassmannian
, consisting of all Lagrangian subspaces of
\left(\mathbb{R}^{{}^{2n}},\omega_{{}_{o}}\right)
, where
\omega_{{}_{o}}
is the standard symplecting form of
\mathbb{R}^{{}^{2}n}
.

Let
\Theta
denote a nowhere vanishing section of
\left(\Lambda^{{}^{n}}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}T^{{}^{*}}M\right)^{{}^{\otimes 2}}
. For any Lagrangian subspace

L_{{}_{p}}\ \in\ \text{LGr}\left(T_{{}_{p}}M\right)

(57)

define

\Omega\left(L_{{}_{p}}\right)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \frac{\Theta\left(v_{{}_{1}},...,v_{{}_{n}},v_{{}_{n}}\right)}{\bigg|\Theta\left(v_{{}_{1}},...,v_{{}_{n}},v_{{}_{n}}\right)\bigg|},

(58)

with
\{v_{{}_{i}}\}
denoting a real basis of
L_{{}_{p}}
.
\Omega
is independent w.r.t.
\{v_{{}_{i}}\}
, and is a fiberwise det
{}^{{}^{2}}
-map, also referred to as the
Calabi-Yau form
.

Defining the phase square map as

\varphi_{{}_{L}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \Omega\circ s_{{}_{L}}:\ L\ \rightarrow\ S^{{}^{1}}

(59)

where
s_{{}_{L}}(p)\ T_{{}_{p}}L\ \in\ \text{LGr}(T_{{}_{p}}M)
, a
grading
of the Lagrangian submanifold
L
is defined by a choice of the lifting of the phase square map through the standard covering

e^{{}^{2\pi it}}:\ \mathbb{R}\ \longrightarrow\ S^{{}^{1}}\

(60)

namely a choice of
\varphi_{{}_{L}}
in the following commutative diagram

A graded Lagrangian submanifold is therefore defined by a pair

\tilde{L}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left(L,\tilde{\varphi}_{{}_{L}}\right).

(61)

Let

\tilde{\text{LGr}}(TM)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{LGr}(TM)

(62)

be the pullback covering space of
\mathbb{R}\rightarrow S^{{}^{1}}
through
\Omega
. By the isomorphism

\text{det}^{{}^{2}}_{{}_{*}}:\ \pi_{{}_{1}}\left(U(n)/O(n)\right)\ \rightarrow\ \pi_{{}_{1}}(S^{{}^{1}})

(63)

and that
\Omega
is fiberwisely equivalent to det
{}^{{}^{2}}
, the pullback bundle
\tilde{\text{LGr}}(TM)
is a fiberwise universal covering of LGr
(TM)
.
Let

\tilde{\Omega}:\ \tilde{\text{LGr}}(TM)\ \rightarrow\ \mathbb{R}

(64)

be the unique lifting of
\Omega
via the covering maps.

There is a 1-to-1 correspondence between the lifting sections
\tilde{\varphi}_{{}_{L}}\equiv\tilde{\Omega}\circ\tilde{s}_{{}_{L}}
, implying a grading of
L
can be equivalently defined as a choice of the lifting conical section
\tilde{\varphi}_{{}_{L}}
. To tell whether such a lifting exists, we need to introduce the notion of the Maslov class.

The Maslov class,
\mu_{{}_{L}}
, of a Lagrangian submanifold
L
is defined to be the first
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficient cohomology class of
L
corresponding to the homotopy class of the phase square map
\varphi_{{}_{L}}
, namely

\mu_{{}_{L}}\ =\ \left[\varphi_{{}_{L}}\right]\ \in\ \left[L,S^{{}^{1}}\right]\ \simeq\ \text{Hom}\left(\pi_{{}_{1}}(L),\mathbb{Z}\right)\ \simeq\ H^{{}^{1}}(L,\mathbb{Z}).

(65)

The Maslov class is the obstruction to lifting
\varphi_{{}_{L}}
through the standard covering
\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
,meaning the existence of such a lifting is equivalent to
\left[\varphi_{{}_{L}}\right]=0
.

Hence, under the conditions

2c_{{}_{1}}(TM)\ =\ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mu_{{}_{L}}\ =\ 0,

(66)

we can equip
L
with a grading. For every connected component of
L
, the choices of the grading are in 1-to-1 correspondence to
\mathbb{Z}
.

The
\mathbb{Z}
-grading of the Floer complex between two graded Lagrangians,
\tilde{L}_{{}_{0}},\tilde{L}_{{}_{1}}
, is defined as follows. Given
p\in\tilde{L}_{{}_{0}}\cap\tilde{L}_{{}_{1}}
,

p\ \in\ CF\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{0}},\tilde{L}_{{}_{1}}\right),

(67)

choose a path from
\tilde{s}_{{}_{L_{{}_{0}}}}
to
\tilde{s}_{{}_{L_{{}_{1}}}}
in
\tilde{\text{LGr}}\left(T_{{}_{p}}M\right)
, and then projecting to
\text{LGr}\left(T_{{}_{p}}M\right)
.

The homotopy class of the projected path does not rely on the choice of the path in the fiberwise universal covering space
\tilde{\text{LGr}}\left(T_{{}_{p}}M\right)
.
Concatenating this projected path with the canonical short path from
T_{{}_{p}}L_{{}_{1}}
to
T_{{}_{p}}L_{{}_{0}}
to get a loop in LGr
\left(T_{{}_{p}}M\right)
. Then, the degree of
p
is defined to be the Maslov index of the loop. The Maslov index is therefore obtained as the change in degree of two intersections

\text{ind}([u])\ =\ \text{deg}(q)\ -\ \text{deg}(p).

(68)

In particular, the Floer differential,
\partial
, is of degree 1. Altogether, this provides the definition of the chain complex of the Floer complex
CF\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{0}},\tilde{L}_{{}_{1}}\right)
, leading to the Floer cohomology

HF\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{0}},\tilde{L}_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(CF\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{0}},\tilde{L}_{{}_{1}}\right),\partial\right).

(69)

3.6.1
Fukaya category (the complete version)

Lagrangian branes

Let
\left(M,\phi_{{}_{M}}\right)
be a symplectic manifold with
2c_{{}_{1}}(M)=0
, equipped with a compatible almost complex structure
I_{{}_{M}}
. Choose a quadratic complex volume form,
\eta_{{}_{M}}^{{}^{2}}
, i.e. a nowhere vanishing zero section of the bicanonical bundle

K_{{}_{M}}^{{}^{2}}\ =\ \lambda_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}^{{}^{\text{top}}}(TM)^{{}^{\otimes-2}}.

(70)

Let

\alpha_{{}_{M}}:\ \text{Gr}(TM)\ \rightarrow\ S^{{}^{1}}

(71)

be the associated squared phase map. A Lagrangian brane is a triple

\tilde{L}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left(L,\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{P}\right)

(72)

consisting of a Lagrangian submanifold
L\subset M
and a brane structure
\left(\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{P}\right)
on the sub-bundle

TL\ \subset\ TM|_{{}_{L}}.

(73)

Concretely, this consists of a function

\tilde{\alpha}:\ L\ \rightarrow\ \mathbb{R}

(74)

such that

\exp\left(2\pi i\tilde{\alpha}(x)\right)\ =\ \alpha_{{}_{M}}\left(TL_{{}_{x}}\right)

(75)

defines the grading of
L
, together with a Pin structure,
\tilde{P}
for
TL
. The Maslov class, namely the obstruction to the existence of a grading,
\mu_{{}_{L}}\in H^{{}^{1}}(L)
, maps to the relative 1
st
Chern class
2c_{{}_{1}}(M,L)
under the boundary map

H^{{}^{1}}(L)\ \rightarrow\ H^{{}^{2}}(M,L)

(76)

4
Categories of coherent sheaves

Finite generation, finite presentation and coherence

Prior to getting to sheaves, it is instructive to discuss some finiteness properties for modules over a commutative ring
R
. An
R
-module
2
2

2

R
is
Noetherian
if every ideal is finitely-generated, implying a submodule of a finitely-generated module is finitely-generated. If
R
is not Noetherian, then finite-generation is too weak, in which case we need to introduce a stronger notion.
,
M
, is finitely-generated, or of finite type, if there is a finite set
\{m_{{}_{1}},...,m_{{}_{n}}\}\ \subset\ M
, such that every element of
M
is an
R
-linear combination of the
m_{{}_{i}}
’s. This is equivalent to having a surjective
R
-module homomorphism,
R^{{}^{n}}\ \rightarrow\ M
, where the
i^{{}^{th}}
-standard basis vector maps to
m_{{}_{i}}
.

Lemma

The following are equivalent:

1.

There exists an exact sequence

\mathbb{R^{{}^{a}}}\ \rightarrow\ R^{{}^{b}}\ \rightarrow\ M\ \rightarrow\ 0

(77)

2.

M
is finitely-generated and the kernel of any surjection
R^{{}^{n}}\rightarrow M
is finitely-generated.

M
is said to be
coherent
if it is finitely-generated and all its finitely-generated modules are finitely-presented. In particular,
M
is finitely presented. Over a Noetherian ring, coherence, finite presentation and finite generation are equivalent, however, this is not true in general. Furthermore, coherence is
stable
when going to submodules.

Theorem

Let

0\ \longrightarrow\ M_{{}_{1}}\ \longrightarrow\ M_{{}_{2}}\ \longrightarrow\ M_{{}_{3}}\ \longrightarrow\ 0

(78)

be an exact sequence. If any two of the
M_{{}_{i}}
’s are coherent, then so too is the third.

4.1
Coherent sheaves

Let
(X,R)
be a ringed space. An
R
-module,
M
, is said to be
coherent
if:

•

For any point
x\in X
there exists an open neighbourhood,
U
, such that
M(U)
is finitely generated. In such instance,
M
is said to be of
finite type
.

•

If for any open set
U\subset X
and morphism

R\bigg|_{{}_{U}}^{{}^{n}}\ \longrightarrow\ M\bigg|_{{}_{U}},

(79)

with finite-type kernel.

We now turn to some basic examples. Let
X\subset\mathbb{A}_{{}_{k}}^{{}^{n}}
be an affine variety with coordinate ring

R\ =\ {\cal O}(X)\ \simeq\ \mathbb{k}\left[x_{{}_{1}},...,x_{{}_{n}}\right]\bigg/I(X).

(80)

Given an
R
-module,
M
, let

\overline{M}(U)\ =\ M\ \otimes_{{}_{R}}\ {\cal O}_{{}_{X}}(R).

(81)

This defines an
{\cal O}_{{}_{X}}
-module. We have

\overline{M}(D(f))\ =\ M\left[1/f\right].

(82)

Since the rings

R\left[1/f\right]\ =\ {\cal O}(D(f))

(83)

are Noetherian, if
M
is finitely-generated, then
\overline{M}
is coherent.

The operation

M\ \mapsto\ \overline{M}

(84)

induces an equivalence of all
R
-modules and the category of quasicoherent
{\cal O}_{{}_{X}}
-modules.

The equivalence is exact, namely it takes exact sequences to exact sequences. Let
Y\subset X
be a subvariety. Then, we have an exact sequence of finitely-generated modules

0\ \longrightarrow\ I\ \longrightarrow\ {\cal O}(X)\ =\ R\ \longrightarrow\ {\cal O}(Y)\ \longrightarrow\ 0,

(85)

where
I
is the image of
I(Y)
in
R
.

Passing to sheaves gives an exact sequence of coherent sheaves,

0\ \longrightarrow\ {\cal I}_{{}_{Y}}\ \longrightarrow\ {\cal O}_{{}_{X}}\ \longrightarrow\ {\cal O}_{{}_{Y}}\ \longrightarrow\ 0,

(86)

with
{\cal I}_{{}_{Y}}
denoting the ideal sheaf of
Y
.
{\cal I}_{{}_{Y}}(U)
are regular functions on
U
vanishing on
Y\cap U
. The sheaf
{\cal O}_{{}_{Y}}
is the sheaf of regular functions on
Y
extended to
X
. So,
{\cal O}_{{}_{Y}}(U)
are the ring regular functions on
Y\cap U
.

Theorem

Let
X
be a complex manifold, then, the sheaf of holomorphic functions
{\cal O}_{{}_{X}}
is coherent as an
{\cal O}_{{}_{X}}
-module.

For example, if
Y\subset X
is a complex submanifold, define the ideal sheaf,
{\cal I}_{{}_{Y}}
, as above, namely as the collection of holomorphic functions vanishing on
Y
. Since
Y
is locally defined by finitely-many equations,
{\cal I}_{{}_{Y}}
is of finite type.

Locally-free sheaves

An
R
-module,
M
, is said to be locally-free (of rank
3
3

3

If
X
is disconnected, in principle, the ranks can be variable.

n
) if there exists an open cover
\{U_{{}_{i}}\}
such that

M\bigg|_{{}_{U_{{}_{i}}}}\ \simeq\ R\bigg|_{{}_{U_{{}_{i}}}}^{{}^{n_{{}_{i}}}},

(87)

with
n\equiv n_{{}_{i}}
.

Complex manifolds

A complex manifold is a manifold holomorphically modelled on polydisks
D
in
\mathbb{C}^{{}^{n}}
. It is a smooth manifold locally isomorphic to
D\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{{}^{n}}
whose transition functions are holomorphic functions.

A smooth manifold equipped with an integrable almost complex structure. A smooth complex analytic space.

For example, a 1-dimensional manifold, is a Riemann surface. In
\mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}
, this is a K3-surface.

4.2
Skyscraper sheaves

A skyscraper sheaf is a sheaf supported at a single point. For
X
a topological space,
x\in X
a point in
X
, and a set
S\ \in\ \text{Set}
, the skyscraper sheaf

\text{skyscr}_{{}_{x}}(S)\ \in\text{Sh}(X)

(88)

in the category of sheaves,
\text{Sh}(X)
on the category of open subsets
\text{Op}(X)
of
X
supported at
x
with value
S
is teh sheaf of sets given by the assignment

\text{skyscr}_{{}_{x}}(S):\ (U\subset x)\ \mapsto\ \begin{cases}&S\ \ \ \ \text{if}\ x\in U\\
&*\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(89)

4.3
Ext-groups

Consider an abelian category
{\cal A}
(with enough projections). Let
A\in{\cal A}
be any object. Consider the contravariant hom function

\text{Hom}_{{}_{\cal A}}(-,A):\ {\cal A}^{{}^{op}}\ \rightarrow\ \text{Ab}

(90)

(notice that this is a left-exact functor). To derive it by resolutions, we need to consider injective resolutions in the opposite category,
{\cal A}^{{}^{op}}
.

For any object
X\in{\cal A}
, and projective resolution

((QX)\ \rightarrow\ X)\ \in\ \text{Ch}_{{}_{\bullet\geq 0}}({\cal A}),

(91)

the n
{}^{{}^{th}}
Ext-group of
X
with coefficients in
A
is the degree-n cochain cohomology

\text{Ext}^{{}^{n}}(X,A)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ H^{{}^{n}}\left(\text{Hom}_{{}_{\cal A}}((QH)_{{}_{\bullet}},A)\right)

(92)

of the cochain complex Hom
((QH)_{{}_{\bullet}},A)
.

Denoting with

[-,-]\ :\ \text{Ch}_{{}_{\bullet}}({\cal A})^{{}^{op}}\times\text{Ch}_{{}_{\bullet}}({\cal A})\ \rightarrow\ \text{Ch}_{{}_{\bullet}}(\text{Ab})

(93)

the enriched Hom of chain complexes, the
n^{{}^{th}}
-Ext-group is canonically identified with the 0
{}^{{}^{th}}
homology of
[,]
as follows:

\text{Ext}^{{}^{n}}(X,A)\ \simeq\ H_{{}_{o}}\left[(QX),A[n]\right]

(94)

from the resolution
QX
of
X
to the
n
-fold delooping/suspensions chain complex of
A

B^{{}^{n}}A\ \overset{def.}{=}\ A[n].

(95)

Ext
{}^{{}^{n}}(X,A)
is identified with the group of extensions of
X
by

B^{{}^{n-1}}A\ =\ A[n-1]

(96)

In particular, for
n=1
, and
{\cal A}
an abstract category, Ext
{}^{{}^{1}}(X,A)
corresponds to the ordinary group extension of
X
by
A
.

Examples of calculations of Ext groups

Given two
R
-modules
A
and
C
, an extension of
C
by
A
is a short exact sequence

0\ \longrightarrow\ A\ \longrightarrow\ B\ \longrightarrow\ C\ \longrightarrow\ 0.

(97)

Another extension is

0\ \longrightarrow\ A\ \longrightarrow\ B^{{}^{\prime}}\ \longrightarrow\ C\ \longrightarrow\ 0.

(98)

Example

Let
R\equiv\mathbb{Z}
. Consider the exact sequence

0\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{Z}\ \xrightarrow{n}\ \mathbb{Z}\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}\ \longrightarrow\ 0.

(99)

Then,

\text{Ext}(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z},A)\ \simeq\ A/nA

(100)

This calculation can be upgraded to calculate Ext
(B,A)
for any finitely-generated abelian group, writing

B\ \equiv\ \bigoplus\ \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}\oplus\ \mathbb{Z}^{{}^{N}}

(101)

\text{Ext}(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z},A)\ \simeq\ \bigoplus_{{}_{i}}A/n_{{}_{i}}A

(102)

5
\mathbb{C}^{*}

5.1
A-side

X=\mathbb{R}\times S^{{}^{1}}\ \simeq\ \mathbb{C}^{*}
, with symplectic structure,
2
,

\omega\ =\ dr\wedge\ d\theta\ =\ d(rd\theta)

(103)

If
\omega=d\alpha
,
\alpha
a 1-form, then
(X,\omega)
is exact. If
L\ \subset\ X
is Lagrangian,
\omega|_{{}_{L}}=0
, and dim
L
=
\frac{1}{2}
dim
X
, then

d\left(\alpha|_{{}_{L}}\right)\ =\ 0

(104)

If

\alpha|_{{}_{L}}\ =\ dh|_{{}_{L}},

(105)

then
L
is said to be exact. For the purpose of this example, the setting is that of exact Lagrangian submanifolds.

The Fukaya category associated to
(X,\omega)
, Fuk
(X\omega)
, is an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-category with objects given by Lagrangian submanifolds
L\subset X
equipped with an additional brane structure.

Morphisms are given by

\text{Hom}(L_{{}_{i}},L_{{}_{j}})\ =\ \mathbb{k}<L_{{}_{i}}\cap L_{{}_{j}}>\ =\ \bigoplus_{{}_{p\in L_{{}_{i}}\cap L_{{}_{j}}}}\mathbb{k}\cdot p,

(106)

with
\mathbb{k}
a suitable field.

In turn, the composition of these morphisms reads as follows

\mu_{{}_{k}}\ :\ \text{Hom}(L_{{}_{o}},L_{{}_{1}})\ \otimes\ \text{Hom}(L_{{}_{1}},L_{{}_{2}})\ \otimes\ ...\otimes\ \text{Hom}(L_{{}_{k-1}},L_{{}_{k}})\ \rightarrow\ \text{Hom}(L_{{}_{o}},L_{{}_{k}})

(107)

\mu_{{}_{k}}(p_{{}_{1}},...,p_{{}_{k}})\ =\ \sum_{{}_{p_{{}_{o}},u\in{\cal M}_{{}_{o}}(p_{{}_{o}},...,p_{{}_{k}})}}\ e^{{}^{-2\pi\omega(u)}}p_{{}_{o}},

(108)

where
{\cal M}_{{}_{o}}
is the 0-dimensional component of
{\cal M}

{\cal M}(p_{{}_{o}},...,p_{{}_{k}})\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left\{\ \color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bigg]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u:\Sigma\ \rightarrow\ X\ \text{holom.}\ \right\}\bigg/\sim

(109)

and
\Sigma
the disk with
k+1
marked boundary points
z_{{}_{i}}
, and
u(z_{{}_{i}})=p_{{}_{i}},u(z)\in L_{{}_{i}}
for
z\in\partial\Sigma
between
z_{{}_{i}},z_{{}_{i+1}}
.

Figure 5:

However, in principle,
{\cal M}_{{}_{o}}
could be infinite. To avoid divergence issues, we need to use Gromov’s compactness theorem, and introduce the Novikov field

\Lambda\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left\{\sum_{{}_{i}=0}^{{}^{\infty}}a_{{}_{i}}T^{{}^{i}}\ |\ a_{{}_{i}}\in\mathbb{C},\lambda_{-i}\in\mathbb{R},\underset{i\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\ \lambda_{{}_{i}}=+\infty\ \right\}

(110)

to serve as the coefficient field of Fuk
(X,\omega)
instead of
\mathbb{C}
, and set

\text{Hom}(L_{{}_{i}},L_{{}_{j}})\ =\ \Lambda<L_{{}_{i}}\cap L_{{}_{j}}>,

(111)

with

\mu_{{}_{k}}(p_{{}_{1}},...,p_{{}_{k}})\ =\ \sum_{{}_{p_{{}_{o}},u\in{\cal M}_{{}_{o}}(p_{{}_{o}},...,p_{{}_{k}})}}\ T^{{}^{\omega(u)}}p_{{}_{o}}.

(112)

5.2
The exact Fukaya category

The same as the definition provided above, but with the following additional requirements:

1.

Objects are exact Lagrangians.

2.

\text{Hom}(L_{{}_{i}},L_{{}_{j}})\ =\ \mathbb{C}<L_{{}_{i}}\cap L_{{}_{j}}>.

(113)

3.

The composition of morphisms are as before, (
112
), except that now
T=1
.

The reason why we can replace
\Lambda\mapsto\mathbb{C}
and
T\mapsto 1
is that the Lagrangian submanifolds are exact.

Given that
p\in L_{{}_{o}}\cap L_{{}_{1}}
and
h_{{}_{L_{{}_{i}}}}\in C^{{}^{\infty}}(L_{{}_{i}},\mathbb{R})
, let us define

A(p)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ h_{{}_{L_{{}_{o}}}}-h_{{}_{L_{{}_{1}}}}.

(114)

Lemma

If
u\in{\cal M}(p_{{}_{o}},...,p_{{}_{k}})
, then

\omega(u)\ =\ A(p_{{}_{o}})-\sum_{{}_{i}=1}^{k}A(p_{{}_{i}})

(115)

Proof

Recall that
u:\Sigma\rightarrow X
, so the pullback
u^{*}
brings
\omega
to the setting of the marked disk. Using Stokes’s Theorem

\int_{{}_{\partial X}}\omega=\int_{{}_{X}}d\omega

(116)

\displaystyle\omega(u)

\displaystyle\equiv\ \int_{{}_{\Sigma}}u^{*}\omega

(117)

\displaystyle=\int_{{}_{\Sigma}}u^{*}(d\alpha)

\displaystyle=\int_{{}_{\Sigma}}d(u^{*}\alpha)

\displaystyle=\int_{{}_{\partial\Sigma}}u^{*}\alpha

\displaystyle=\int_{{}_{L_{o}}}u^{*}\alpha\bigg|_{{}_{L_{o}}}+...+\int_{{}_{L_{k}}}u^{*}\alpha\bigg|_{{}_{L_{k}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{{}_{L_{o}}}u^{*}dh_{{}_{L_{o}}}+...+\int_{{}_{L_{k}}}u^{*}dh_{{}_{L_{k}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{{}_{\partial L_{o}}}u^{*}h_{{}_{L_{o}}}+...+\int_{{}_{\partial L_{k}}}u^{*}h_{{}_{L_{k}}}

\displaystyle=h_{{}_{L_{o}}}(p_{{}_{1}})-h_{{}_{L_{o}}}(p_{{}_{o}})+h_{{}_{L_{1}}}(p_{{}_{2}})-h_{{}_{L_{1}}}(p_{{}_{1}})+...+h_{{}_{L_{k}}}(p_{{}_{o}})-h_{{}_{L_{k}}}(p_{{}_{k}})

\displaystyle=A(p_{{}_{o}})-\sum_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{k}}A(p_{{}_{i}}).

Upshot: in the exact case,
\omega(u)
is independent w.r.t.
u
, hence, only one power of
T
appears in the definition of
\mu_{{}_{k}}
, and we can set
T=1
.

Given that we know the exact Fukaya category to be
\mathbb{C}
-linear, this means we can compare it with

D^{{}^{b}}(Y)\ =\ D^{{}^{b}}(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}),

(118)

since
D^{{}^{b}}(Y)
is in turn a
\mathbb{C}
-linear category.

5.3
Compact Lagrangian case

The only compact exact Lagrangian in
X
up to Hamiltonian isotopy is the circle
S^{{}^{1}}
(which we write as the set
\{r=0\}
on the cylinder). It is important to note that we must consider
S^{{}^{1}}
at 0 on the cylinder, otherwise, it will not be exact.

5.3.1
Local systems

Objects in a compact Fukaya category consist of pairs

(L,\xi)\ \in\ \text{Obj}(\text{Fuk}(X)),

(119)

where
\xi
is a
\mathbb{C}
-local system, specifically, flat vector bundles
\xi\rightarrow L
with unitary holonomy over
\mathbb{C}
.
\xi
is also referred to as the
brane structure
of the Lagrangian in question. Adding it to the objects of Fuk
(X)
in turn, leads to a modification of the product formula, (
112
), which we are about to describe.

Consider the following isomorphisms

\gamma_{{}_{i}}:\ \xi_{{}_{i}}\bigg|_{{}_{p_{{}_{i}}}}\ \rightarrow\ \xi_{{}_{i}}\bigg|_{{}_{p_{{}_{i+1}}}}

(120)

\rho_{{}_{i}}:\ \xi_{{}_{i-1}}\bigg|_{{}_{p_{{}_{i}}}}\ \rightarrow\ \xi_{{}_{i}}\bigg|_{{}_{p_{{}_{i}}}}.

(121)

Then, defining the composition

\eta_{{}_{u,\rho_{{}_{1}},...,\rho_{{}_{k}}}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \gamma_{{}_{k}}\circ\rho_{{}_{k}}\circ...\circ\gamma_{{}_{1}}\circ\rho_{{}_{1}}\circ\gamma_{{}_{o}}

(122)

(
112
) now becomes

\mu_{{}_{k}}(p_{{}_{1}},...,p_{{}_{k}})\ =\ \sum_{{}_{p_{{}_{o}},u\in{\cal M}_{{}_{o}}(p_{{}_{o}},...,p_{{}_{k}})}}\ T^{{}^{\omega(u)}}p_{{}_{o}}\eta_{{}_{u,\rho_{{}_{1}},...,\rho_{{}_{k}}}}

(123)

with
\eta
adding a monodromy contribution while moving on the boundary of a holomorphic disk.

Figure 6:
A and B denote two holomorphic disks resulting from Lagrangian intersection.

\mu_{{}_{1}}(p)\ =\ q\left(1-ba^{{}^{-1}}\right)

(124)

whereas

\mu_{{}_{1}}(q)\ =\ 0.

(125)

We know that the map

\mu_{{}_{1}}:\ \text{Hom}\left(\left(S^{{}^{1}},\xi_{{}_{a}}\right),\left(S^{{}^{1}},\xi_{{}_{b}}\right)\right)\ =\ \mathbb{C}\cdot p\ \oplus\ \mathbb{C}\cdot q\ \rightarrow\ \text{Hom}\left(\left(S^{{}^{1}},\xi_{{}_{a}}\right),\left(S^{{}^{1}},\xi_{{}_{b}}\right)\right)\ =\ \mathbb{C}\cdot p\ \oplus\ \mathbb{C}\cdot q

(126)

is given by

p\ \mapsto\ q\cdot(1-b^{{}^{-1}}a).

(127)

If
a\neq b
, then this is an isomorphism such that the cohomology is zero. On the other hand, if
a=b
, we get the zero map, thus the cohomology defines a 2D vector space. What we have just said can be succinct;y expressed as follows

\text{Hom}((S^{{}^{1}},\xi_{{}_{a}}),(S^{{}^{1}},\xi_{{}_{b}}))\ \simeq\ \begin{cases}&0\ \ \ ,\ \text{if}\ \ a\neq b\\
&\mathbb{C}[\theta]\bigg/\theta^{{}^{2}}\ \ \ ,\ \text{if}\ \ a=b\\
\end{cases},

(128)

which agrees with what can be found on the B-side with skyscraper sheaves,
{\cal O}_{{}_{a}}
, as mirror duals to the Fukaya object
S_{{}_{a}}^{{}^{1}}\ =\ (S^{{}^{1}},\xi_{{}_{a}})
.

5.4
The wrapped Fukaya category

5.4.1
Hamiltonian perturbation

As already mentioned when introducing Floer cohomology, Hamiltonian perturbation is required when one wishes to calculate the Floer complex
CF\left(L_{{}_{0}},L_{{}_{1}}\right)
for the case of non-transversally intersecting Lagrangians, especially for the case
L_{{}_{0}}\ \equiv\ L_{{}_{1}}
. We now turn to briefly overview how this affects the calculation of the Floer differential.

Let
H_{{}_{t}}\ \in\ C^{{}^{\infty}}(M\times[0,1])
be a time-dependent Hamiltonian,
X_{{}_{H_{{}_{t}}}}
be the Hamiltonian vector field generated by
H_{{}_{t}}
and the 1-parameter flow
4
4

4

Also referred to as Hamiltonian isotopy.
,
\phi_{{}_{t}}
, generated by
X_{{}_{H_{{}_{t}}}}
.

Let

J\ \in\ C^{{}^{\infty}}\left([0,1],{\cal J}(M,\omega)\right)

(129)

be a generic smooth family of
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures.
The Floer data,
\left(H_{{}_{t}},J\right)
, should ensure the regularity condition
5
5

5

Namely
D_{{}_{\overline{\partial}_{{}_{J,u}}}}
is surjective at any strip
u
.
and
L_{{}_{0}}
and
\phi_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{-1}}L_{{}_{1}}
intersect transversally. Then, the Cauchy-Riemann equation becomes

\frac{\partial\tilde{u}}{\partial s}\ +\ \tilde{J}(\tilde{u})\frac{\partial\tilde{u}}{\partial t}\ =\ 0,

(130)

where

\tilde{J}=(\phi_{{}_{t}})^{{}^{-1}}{{}_{{}_{*}}}\ \ \ J\ \ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \tilde{u}(s,t)\ =\ \left(\phi_{{}_{t}}\right)^{{}^{-1}}\left(u(s,t)\right).

(131)

From such data, the Floer complex in presence of a Hamiltoninan perturbation can be redefined as follows

CF\left(L_{{}_{0}},L_{{}_{1}};H_{{}_{t}},J\right)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ CF\left(L_{{}_{o}},\left(\phi_{{}_{t}}\right)^{{}^{-1}}(L_{{}_{1}});J_{{}_{t}}\right).

(132)

Theorem
[D. Auroux]

Given
L_{{}_{0}},L_{{}_{1}}
, then the Floer complex
(CF(L_{{}_{0}},L_{{}_{1}}),\partial)
does not depend on the choice of the time-dependent almost-complex structure, and the Hamiltonian isotopy, up to equivalence.

5.4.2
The wrapped Fukaya category

The last thing to find is the mirror of
{\cal O}
. Note that

\text{Hom}^{{}^{\bullet}}({\cal O},{\cal O})\ =\ \mathbb{C}[z^{{}^{\pm 1}}]

(133)

is infinite dimensional. Therefore, we need
\mathbb{C}<L\cap\varphi(L)>
to be infinite dimensional as well. For this to occur,
L
must be non-compact. Require these non-compact objects to be well-behaved at infinity. By this we mean that the Lagrangians will radially wrap the cylinder as approaching infinity.

The wrapped Fukaya category,
{\cal W}Fuk(X)
, is therefore characterised as follows

•

Objects are exact Lagrangian submanifolds such that they are invariant under Liouville flow.

•

Morphisms (invariance of intersection at infinity)

\text{Hom}(L,\varphi(L))\ =\ \bigoplus_{{}_{p\in L\cap\varphi(L)}}\mathbb{C}\cdot p.

(134)

•

Product: the composition

\text{Hom}(L_{{}_{o}},L_{{}_{1}})\ \otimes\ \text{Hom}(L_{{}_{1}},L_{{}_{2}})\ \rightarrow\ \text{Hom}(L_{{}_{o}},L_{{}_{2}})\

(135)

is given by

(p,q)\ \mapsto\ \sum_{{}_{r}}\eta_{{}_{pqr}}\cdot r,

(136)

where
\eta_{{}_{pqr}}
is the count of holomorphic disks with boundary.

Figure 7:
A holomorphic triangle.

Figure
7
shows a holomorphic triangle as would arise in
C^{*}
when acting with a Hamiltonian perturbation to evaluate cohomology of noncompact Lagrangian self-intersections. In particular, in the figure reproduced above we have that
\varphi^{2}_{{}_{H}}
is a perturbation of
L
by double the amount.
\varphi_{{}_{H}}(p)\equiv\varphi_{{}_{H}}(L\cap\varphi_{{}_{H}}L)
, and
r^{{}^{\prime}}\in L\cap\varphi(L)
.

For the case of the cylinder, the noncompact Lagrangian in question is

L\ \equiv\ \mathbb{R}\ \simeq\ \mathbb{R}\times\{\text{pt}\}\ =\ \{\theta=0\}.

(137)

We need to calculate
End(L)
. In order to do so, we need
L
to wrap the cylinder. Note that

\text{Hom}(L,L)\ =\ \bigoplus_{{}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\mathbb{C}\cdot x_{{}_{n}}.

(138)

This has infinite rank, but, to get a true correspondence, we need to compute the multiplication structure
x_{{}_{n}}\cdot x_{{}_{n}}
, and this, in turn, requires introducing a second perturbation, so that we can get a third Lagrangian, resulting in the holographic triangle pictured above.

Remark:
We would like

x_{{}_{n}}\ \in\ \text{Hom}(L,L)

(139)

to correspond to the power

z^{{}^{n}}\ \in\mathbb{C}\left[z^{{}^{\pm 1}}\right].

(140)

The way to define the product equation is as follows: take
L\cap\varphi_{{}_{H}}(L)
and an intersection point of
\varphi_{{}_{H}}(L)\cap\varphi^{2}(L)
. These points determine a third point
L\cap\varphi^{2}_{{}_{H}}(L)
which is the product we want to define.

Figure 8:
A holomorphic triangle on
\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

By taking the universal cover of
X
¡ in this case
\mathbb{R}^{{}^{2}}
, we see that

x_{{}_{i}}\ \cdot\ x_{{}_{j}}\ =\ x_{{}_{i+j}}

(141)

thereby signalling that the structure of Hom
{{}^{\bullet}}(L,L)
is the same as that of
\mathbb{C}\left[z^{{}^{\pm 1}}\right]
, where

z^{{}^{i}}\cdot\ z^{{}^{j}}\ =\ z^{{}^{i+j}}\ \ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ x_{{}_{i}}\ \mapsto\ z^{{}^{i}}.

(142)

Hence,

\text{Hom}^{{}^{\bullet}}(L,L)\ \simeq\ \mathbb{C}\left[z^{{}^{\pm 1}}\right]

(143)

thereby implying
L=\mathbb{R}
is mirror to
{\cal O}
.

A further relation to consider in the HMS construction is the following

\text{Hom}^{{}^{\bullet}}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})\ \otimes\ \text{Hom}^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(\mathbb{R},S_{{}_{a}}^{{}^{1}}\right)\ \simeq\ \text{Hom}^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(\mathbb{R},S_{{}_{a}}^{{}^{1}}\right).

(144)

Once more, working on the universal cover of
X
, (
144
) reduces to the following map

x_{{}_{i}}\ \cdot\ a\ =\ a.

(145)

This argument extends straightforwardly to the case in which

X\ =\ \left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)^{{}^{n}}\ \simeq\ T^{{}^{*}}T^{{}^{n}}

(146)

whose wrapped Fukaya category is generated by

L_{{}_{0}}\ =\ \left(\mathbb{R}_{{}_{+}}\right)^{{}^{n}}.

(147)

Upon introducing a Hamiltonian perturbation of the kind

H\ =\ \frac{1}{2}\ \sum_{{}_{i}}r_{{}_{i}}^{{}^{2}}\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ r_{{}_{i}}\ =\ \log|z_{{}_{i}}|

(148)

the resulting wrapped cohomology reads as follows

HW^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(L_{{}_{0}},L_{{}_{0}}\right)\ =\ CW^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(L_{{}_{0}},L_{{}_{0}}\right)\ \simeq\ \mathbb{C}\left[x_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{\pm}},...,x_{{}_{n}}^{{}^{\pm}}\right]

(149)

which agrees with the ring of functions of the mirror
X^{{}^{\text{V}}}=\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)^{{}^{n}}
.

5.5
B-side

Notation:

Y\ \equiv\ \text{Spec}\left[z^{{}^{\pm 1}}\right]\ =\ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}},

(150)

with structure sheaf
{\cal O}_{{}_{Y}}
and skyscraper sheaves
{\cal O}_{{}_{a}}
.

On the B-side, objects are skyscraper sheaves,
{\cal O}_{{}_{a}}
, corresponding to Lagrangian submanifolds equipped with local brane structure. Homoeomorphisms are

\text{Hom}^{{}^{\bullet}}_{{}_{D(R)}}\left({\cal O}_{{}_{a}},{\cal O}_{{}_{b}}\right)\ \simeq\ \text{Ext}^{{}^{\bullet}}_{{}_{R}}\left({\cal O}_{{}_{a}},{\cal O}_{{}_{b}}\right)\ \simeq\ \begin{cases}&0\ \ \ ,\ \ \text{if}\ \ a\neq b\\
&\mathbb{C}[\theta]/\theta^{{}^{2}}\ \ ,\ \ \text{if}\ \ a=b\end{cases}

(151)

Proposition

For
{\cal F}
a coherent sheaf on a variety,
X
, we have

\text{Ext}^{{}^{i}}\left({\cal O}_{{}_{X}},{\cal F}\right)\ \simeq\ H^{{}^{i}}(X,{\cal F})\ \ \ \ \ \forall i\geq 0.

(152)

Theorem [Serre]
(Part of a cohomology-vanishing theorem)

\text{Hom}^{{}^{\bullet}}({\cal O},{\cal O})\ \simeq\ \text{Ext}^{{}^{\bullet}}({\cal O},{\cal O})\ \simeq\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(Y,{\cal O})\ =\ \mathbb{C}\left[z^{{}^{\pm 1}}\right]

(153)

\text{Hom}^{{}^{\bullet}}({\cal O},{\cal O}_{{}_{a}})\ \simeq\ \text{Ext}^{{}^{\bullet}}({\cal O},{\cal O}_{{}_{a}})\ \simeq\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(Y,{\cal O}_{{}_{a}})\ =\ \mathbb{C}\left[0\right]

(154)

\text{Hom}^{{}^{\bullet}}({\cal O}_{{}_{a}},{\cal O})\ \simeq\ \text{Ext}^{{}^{\bullet}}({\cal O}_{{}_{a}},{\cal O})\ =\ \mathbb{C}\left[1\right]

(155)

where [0], [1] denote the degree that
\mathbb{C}
is in. (
153
) follows from the general fact that

{\cal O}_{{}_{\text{Spec}R}}(\text{Spec}R)\ \simeq\ R

(156)

and cohomology vanishes for
\bullet>0
.

(
154
) follows from skyscraper sheaves are flasque, namely have vanishing cohomology for
\bullet>1
. In such case,
\mathbb{C}
is degree-0 since
{\cal O}
has a stalk only at
a
.

For (
155
), let’s calculate the Ext group by taking a resolution. First note that, under the equivalence of categories, we have

\text{Ext}^{{}^{\bullet}}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}}}\left({\cal O}_{{}_{a}},{\cal O}\right)\ \simeq\ \text{Ext}^{{}^{\bullet}}_{{}_{R}}\left(\frac{R}{z-a},R\right)

(157)

where
R=\mathbb{C}\left[z^{{}^{\pm 1}}\right]
. Computing a projective resolution of
\frac{R}{z-a}
, we have

0\ \longrightarrow\ R\ \xrightarrow{\cdot(z-a)}\ R\ \longrightarrow\ R/(z-a)\ \longrightarrow\ 0.

(158)

Taking Hom
{}_{{}_{R}}(-,R)
, we get

Remark
Under the equivalence of categories

\text{QCoh}\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)\ \simeq\ \text{Mod}-\mathbb{C}\left[z^{{}^{\pm 1}}\right]

(159)

the structure sheaf
{\cal O}
corresponds to the ring
\mathbb{C}\left[z^{{}^{\pm 1}}\right]
, and the skyscraper
{\cal O}_{{}_{a}}
to the ring

\mathbb{C}\left[z^{{}^{\pm 1}}\right]/(z-a)\ \simeq\ \mathbb{C}.

(160)

Hence,

\text{QCoh}\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)\ \xrightarrow{\sim}\ \text{Mod}-\mathbb{C}\left[z^{{}^{\pm 1}}\right]

(161)

means taking a global section.
We then have

{\cal O}\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)\ \simeq\ \mathbb{C}\left[z^{{}^{\pm 1}}\right]

(162)

and

{\cal O}_{{}_{a}}\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)\ \simeq\ \mathbb{C}

(163)

because the skyscraper sheaf
{\cal O}_{{}_{a}}
has stalk
\mathbb{C}
when evaluated on opens containing
a
, and zero stalks otherwise.

Remark

D^{{}^{b}}(Y)\ =\ D^{{}^{b}}\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)
is generated by the structure sheaves
{\cal O}
. Therefore, the proof of HMS for the cylinder only requires to find mirror objects and Hom sets for
{\cal O}
, as well as the mirrors of
{\cal O}_{{}_{a}}
.

Viewing
{\cal W}\left(\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)\right)
and
D^{{}^{b}}\text{Coh}\left(\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)\right)
in terms of perfect complexes of modules over
\mathbb{C}\left[x_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{\pm}},...,x_{{}_{n}}^{{}^{\pm}}\right]
, HMS follows.

6
Orientation of moduli space

Let
(\tilde{L}_{{}_{o}},\tilde{L}_{{}_{1}})
such that
L_{{}_{o}},L_{{}_{1}}
intersect transversally. In the tangent space to
(M,\phi_{{}_{M}},\theta_{{}_{M}},I_{{}_{M}})
, namely

\left(TM_{{}_{y}},\phi_{{}_{M,y}},I_{{}_{M,y}},\eta^{{}^{2}}_{{}_{M,y}}\right)

(164)

at every internal point
y\in L_{{}_{o}}\cap L_{{}_{1}}
, there is an obvious pair of linear Lagrangian branes

\tilde{\Lambda}_{{}_{K,y}}\ =\ \left(\Lambda,\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{P}_{{}_{K,y}}\right)

(165)

with index and orientation spaces

i(y)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ i\left(\tilde{\Lambda}_{{}_{K,y}},\tilde{\Lambda}_{{}_{K,y}}\right)\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ o(y)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ o\left(\tilde{\Lambda}_{{}_{K,y}},\tilde{\Lambda}_{{}_{K,y}}\right).

(166)

(
166
) are defined as follows: choose a path
\tilde{\lambda}
in
\tilde{\text{Gr}}(V)
connecting the two points, whose projection
\lambda
lies in
{\cal P}^{{}^{-}}\text{Gr}(V)
, and set

i\left(\tilde{\Lambda}_{{}_{K,y}},\tilde{\Lambda}_{{}_{K,y}}\right)\ =\ I_{{}_{H}}(\lambda)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ o\left(\tilde{\Lambda}_{{}_{K,y}},\tilde{\Lambda}_{{}_{K,y}}\right)\ =\ \left(\delta_{{}_{H}}\right)_{{}_{\lambda}}.

(167)

In principle, one should prove that these are independent of the choice of
\tilde{\lambda}
(up to canonical isomorphism). Denoting by

\Omega^{{}^{-}}\left(\text{Gr}(V),\Lambda_{{}_{o}},\Lambda_{1}\right)

(168)

the subspace of
{\cal P}^{{}^{-}}\text{Gr}(V)
consisting of all paths from
\Lambda_{{}_{o}}
to
\Lambda_{{}_{1}}
, and by

\Omega^{{}^{-}}\left(\tilde{\text{Gr}}(V),\tilde{\Lambda}_{{}_{o}},\tilde{\Lambda}_{1}\right)

(169)

the corresponding space of paths
\tilde{\lambda}
in
\tilde{\text{Gr}}(V)
, these spaces are homotopy equivalent to the ordinary loop spaces
\Omega\text{Gr}(V)
and
\tilde{\Omega\text{Gr}}(V)
, respectively.

For
n\geq 2
,
\tilde{\Omega\text{Gr}}(V)
is simply-connected, thereby implying uniqueness. However, for
n=1
, there are two distinct homotopy classes, both of which lead to the same
i
, but, for the orientation space, there are two different possibilities, corresponding to the automorphism responsible for change of orientation.

We declare that there is a change in homotopy class of
\tilde{\lambda}
giving rise to an orientation-reversing automorphism of
\left(\delta_{{}_{H}}\right)_{{}_{\lambda}}
.

Suppose
S
is a
(d+1)
-dimensional disk equipped with strip-like ends and brane labels
\tilde{L}_{{}_{0}},...,\tilde{L}_{{}_{d}}
. Choose Floer data for the pairs
\left(L_{{}_{o}},L_{{}_{d}}\right)
, and
\left(L_{{}_{k-1}},L_{{}_{k}}\right)
,
k=1,...,d
, and a compatible perturbation datum on
S
. At a regular point
u\in{\cal M}_{{}_{S}}\left(y_{{}_{o}},...,y_{{}_{d}}\right)
one has

\text{dim}_{{}_{\cal U}}\left({\cal M}_{{}_{s}}\left(y_{{}_{o}},...,y_{{}_{d}}\right)\right)\ =\ i\left(y_{{}_{o}}\right)-i\left(y_{{}_{1}}\right)-...-i\left(y_{{}_{d}}\right)

(170)

\displaystyle\left(\lambda^{{}^{top}}T{\cal M}_{{}_{S}}\left(y_{{}_{o}},...,y_{{}_{d}}\right)\right)_{{}_{n}}

\displaystyle\simeq\ \lambda^{{}^{top}}\text{Ker}\left(D_{{}_{S,u}}\right)

(171)

\displaystyle\simeq\ o\left(y_{{}_{o}}\right)\ \otimes\ o\left(y_{{}_{1}}\right)^{{}^{\text{V}}}\ \otimes\ ...\ \otimes\ o\left(y_{{}_{d}}\right)^{{}^{\text{V}}},

with
{\cal M}_{{}_{S}}
canonically oriented relative to the orientation space at the ends.

6.1
More general Riemann surfaces

Enlarging the class of Riemann surfaces
S
. Assuming the compactification
\hat{S}
has the property that each boundary circle contains exactly one incoming point at infinity. Then, for
u
a regular point of a suitable moduli space
{\cal M}_{{}_{S}}\left(\{y_{{}_{\xi}}\}\right)
,

\text{dim}_{{}_{u}}\left({\cal M}_{{}_{S}}\left(\{y_{{}_{\xi}}\}\right)\right)\ =\ u\left(\chi(S)-|\Sigma^{{}^{-}}|\right)+\sum_{{}_{\xi\in\Sigma^{{}^{\pm}}}}\mp i\left(y_{{}_{\xi}}\right)

(172)

\left(\lambda^{{}^{top}}T{\cal M}_{{}_{S}}\left(y_{{}_{o}},...,y_{{}_{d}}\right)\right)_{{}_{n}}\ \simeq\ \bigoplus_{{}_{\xi\in\Sigma^{{}^{\pm}}}}o\left(y_{{}_{\xi}}\right)^{{}^{\mp 1}}.

(173)

6.2
Floer cohomology of branes

Fix a coefficient field,
\mathbb{k}
. If
\delta
is any 1D real vector space,
|\delta|_{{}_{\mathbb{k}}}
is the 1D
\mathbb{k}
-vector space generated by the two orientations of
\delta
, such that their sum is 0.

The upgraded version of Floer cohomology associated to any pair
\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{o}},\tilde{L}_{{}_{1}}\right)
of exact Lagrangian branes in
M
,
HF^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{o}},\tilde{L}_{{}_{1}}\right)
with the following properties:

1.

Triangle product

HF^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{1}}\tilde{L}_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \otimes\ HF^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{o}},\tilde{L}_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ HF^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{o}}\tilde{L}_{{}_{2}}\right).

(174)

2.

Change of brane structure. Applying the same operation to all the branes involved, the Floer cohomology group remains unchanged. Operations could be, either, a change of grading by a constant in
\mathbb{Z}
, or to tensor the Pin structures by the restriction of a common line bundle
\beta\rightarrow M
.

3.

Duality.
\forall\tilde{L}
, Poincare’ duality and the PSS isomorphism give rise to a map

\int_{{}_{\tilde{L}}}:\ HF^{{}^{n}}\left(\tilde{L},\tilde{\otimes}\lambda^{{}^{top}}(TL)\ \simeq\ H^{{}^{n}}\left(L;|\lambda^{{}^{top}}TL|_{{}_{\mathbb{k}}}\right)\ \simeq\ H_{{}_{{}_{o}}}(L;\mathbb{k}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{k}.

(175)

6.3
Determining the grading and signs

Choose a regular Floer datum and define the graded Floer cochain group by setting,
\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

CF^{{}^{k}}\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{o}},\tilde{L}_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \bigoplus_{{}_{i(y)=k}}\left|o(y)\right|_{{}_{\mathbb{k}}}

(176)

Let
u\in{\cal M}_{{}_{z}}\left(y_{{}_{o}},y_{{}_{1}}\right)
be a non-stationary solution of Floer’s equation. Then, there is a short-exact sequence

\mathbb{R}\ \rightarrow\ T{\cal M}_{{}_{z}}\left(y_{{}_{o}},y_{{}_{1}}\right)_{{}_{u}}\ \rightarrow\ T{\cal M}_{{}_{z}}^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(y_{{}_{o}},y_{{}_{1}}\right)_{{}_{u}}

(177)

where
\mathbb{R}
corresponds to the translation direction. This allows one to identify the top exterior powers of
T_{{}_{u}}{\cal M}_{{}_{z}}\left(y_{{}_{o}},y_{{}_{1}}\right)_{{}_{u}}
and
T_{{}_{u}}{\cal M}_{{}_{z}}^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(y_{{}_{o}},y_{{}_{1}}\right)_{{}_{u}}
, hence, by

\left(\lambda^{{}^{top}}T{\cal M}_{{}_{S}}\left(y_{{}_{o}},...,y_{{}_{d}}\right)\right)_{{}_{u}}\ \simeq\ \lambda^{{}^{top}}\text{Ker}\left(D_{{}_{S,u}}\right)\ \simeq\ o\left(y_{{}_{o}}\right)\otimes o\left(y_{{}_{1}}\right)^{{}^{\text{V}}}\otimes...\otimes o\left(y_{{}_{d}}\right)^{{}^{\text{V}}}

(178)

yields a preferred orientation of
{\cal M}_{{}_{z}}\left(y_{{}_{o}},y_{{}_{1}}\right)
relative to
o\left(y_{{}_{o}}\right)\otimes o\left(y_{{}_{1}}\right)^{{}^{\text{V}}}
. In particular, if
u
is an isolated point of
{\cal M}_{{}_{z}}^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(y_{{}_{o}},y_{{}_{1}}\right)
, we get an isomorphism

c_{{}_{u}}:\ o\left(y_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ o\left(y_{{}_{o}}\right).

(179)

The
\left(y_{{}_{o}},y_{{}_{1}}\right)
-coefficient of the boundary operator
\partial
is defined as the sum over
\mathbb{k}
-normalisations of all such
c_{{}_{u}}
,

\partial^{{}^{y_{{}_{o}},y_{{}_{1}}}}\ =\ \sum_{{}_{u}}|c_{{}_{n}}|_{{}_{\mathbb{k}}}:\left|o\left(y_{{}_{1}}\right)\right|_{{}_{\mathbb{k}}}\ \rightarrow\ \left|o\left(y_{{}_{o}}\right)\right|_{{}_{\mathbb{k}}}.

(180)

(
180
) corresponds to saying that each
u
contributes with
\pm 1
. The Floer cohomology is therefore redefined as follows

HF^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{o}},\tilde{L}_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(CF^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{o}},\tilde{L}_{{}_{1}}\right),\partial\right).

(181)

Then, take a
(d+1)
-pointed disk
S
, with brane labels
\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{o}},...,\tilde{L}_{-d}\right)
; choose regular Floer data and perturbation data. At every isolated point
u\in{\cal M}_{{}_{S}}\left(y_{{}_{o}},...,y_{{}_{d}}\right)
, the relative orientation (
178
) gives rise to an isomorphism

c_{{}_{n}}:\ o\left(y_{{}_{d}}\right)\ \otimes...\otimes\ o\left(y_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ o\left(y_{{}_{o}}\right).

(182)

We use these to define a chain map

C\Phi_{{}_{S}}:\ CF^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{d-1}},\tilde{L}_{{}_{d}}\right)\ \otimes\ ...\ \otimes\ CF^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{0}},\tilde{L}_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ CF^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(\tilde{L}_{{}_{0}},\tilde{L}_{{}_{d}}\right).

(183)

Some generalities

Given an arbitrary symplectic manifold
(M,\phi)
with an almost complex structure
I_{{}_{M}}
and quadratic complex volume form
\eta_{{}_{M}}^{{}^{2}}
, a graded automorphism of
M
is a pair
(\phi,\tilde{\gamma})
consisting of a symplectic automorphism
\phi
and a map
\tilde{\gamma}:\text{Gr}(TM)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\text{exp}\left(2\pi i\tilde{\gamma}(\Lambda)\right)\ =\ \frac{\alpha_{{}_{M}}\left(D\phi(\Lambda)\right)}{\alpha_{{}_{M}}(\Lambda)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \Lambda\in\text{Gr}(TM).

(184)

Composition of such automorphisms is defined by

\tilde{\phi}_{{}_{2}}\circ\tilde{\phi}_{{}_{1}}\ =\ \left(\phi_{{}_{2}}\circ\phi_{{}_{1}},\tilde{\gamma}_{{}_{2}}\circ D\phi_{{}_{1}}+\tilde{\gamma}_{{}_{1}}\right).

(185)

Graded automorphisms act naturally on the set of Lagrangian branes: if
\left(L,\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{P}\right)
is a brane, the image
\phi(L)
inherits the grading

x\ \mapsto\ \tilde{\gamma}\left(TL_{{}_{\phi^{{}^{-1}}(x)}}\right)+\tilde{\alpha}\left(\phi^{{}^{-1}}(x)\right)

(186)

as well as the pushforward Pin structure
\left(\phi^{{}^{-1}}\right)^{{}^{*}}\tilde{P}
. This can be further generalised as follows: define a brane automorphism to be a triple
\phi=(\phi,\tilde{\gamma},\beta)
, where
(\phi,\tilde{\gamma})
is as before, and
\beta\rightarrow M
is a real line bundle. The action of such automorphisms on branes includes a twist of the Pin-structure,

\tilde{P}\ \mapsto\ \left(\phi^{{}^{-1}}\right)^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(\tilde{P}\otimes\beta|_{{}_{L}}\right).

(187)

7
HMS for Fano varieties

7.1
LG model

LG model are used as models for 2D QFT in String Theory. They are defined from the action

S_{{}_{LG}}\ :\ \left(\phi:\ \Sigma\rightarrow\ X\right)\ \mapsto\int_{{}_{\Sigma}}\left(\left|\nabla\phi\right|^{{}^{2}}+\left|g_{{}_{ij}}\frac{\partial{\cal W}(\phi)}{\partial\phi_{{}_{i}}}\frac{\partial{\cal W}(\phi)}{\partial\phi_{{}_{j}}}\right|^{{}^{2}}+\text{ferm. d.o.f.}\right)\ d\mu

(188)

where
\Sigma
is the 2D worldsheet, and a holomorphic function

{\cal W}:\ X\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{C}

(189)

referred to as the
superpotential
. Kontsevich understood that such a theory provides a type of QFTs that can be used on one side of the HMS correspondence. Specifically, if
X
is a Fano variety, the usual B-model does not exist, since the corresponding SUSY sigma model is not conformally-invariant. The LG B-branes are the HMS duals to the A-model on a Fano variety. Kontsevich provided the mathematical formulation of this by noticing that, the B-branes are not given by chain complexes of coherent sheaves as in the usual B-model encounered in the first instances of the HMS correspondence. But, rather, by
twisted complexes
. The latter essentially means that the differential is nonvanishing

\text{d}^{{}^{2}}\ =\ a{\cal W},

(190)

with
a
a constant. (
190
) being nonvanishing supplies a way of twisting
D^{{}^{b}}
Coh
(X)
.

7.2
The case of Fano varieties

Given an algebraic hypersurface,

H\ =\ {\cal W}^{{}^{-1}}(0)\ \ \ \ \subset\ \ \ \ \left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)^{{}^{n}},

(191)

HMS relates the wrapped Fukaya category of
H
to the derived category of singularities of the mirror Landau-Ginzburg (LG) model.

Furthermore, one can enrich this by using wrapped Fukaya categories of the complement

\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)^{{}^{n}}\ \backslash\ H

(192)

and the Fukaya-Seidel category of the LG model

\left(\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)^{{}^{n}},{\cal W}\right).

(193)

Let
H
be a smooth algebraic hypersurface, whose defining equation is a Laurent polynomial

f\left(x_{{}_{1}},...,x_{{}_{n}}\right)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{\alpha\in A}}c_{{}_{\alpha}}\tau^{{}^{\rho(\alpha)}}x_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{\alpha_{{}_{1}}}}...x_{{}_{n}}^{{}^{\alpha_{{}_{n}}}}.

(194)

Take
A
to be a finite subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{{}^{n}},c_{{}_{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}},\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{{}_{+}}
sufficiently small, then

\rho:\ A\ \rightarrow\ \mathbb{R}

(195)

is a convex function.
Requiring
\rho
to be the restriction to
A
of a convex piecewise linear function
\hat{\rho}
, define the convex hull to be

\text{Conv}(\Lambda)\ \subset\ \mathbb{R}^{{}^{n}}.

(196)

The maximal domains of linearity of
\hat{\rho}
define a polyhedral decomposition
{\cal P}
of
\text{Conv}(A)
whose set of vertices is required to be exactly
A
. Hori and Vafa conjectured that
H
should arise as a mirror to a toric CY manifold,
Y
, specifically, a toric LG model
(Y,{\cal W})
. The outcome of this is as follows:
consider the piecewise linear function

\varphi:\ \mathbb{R}^{{}^{n}}\ \rightarrow\ \mathbb{R}

(197)

obtained by tropicalising
{\cal W}
, and the noncompact polytope

\Delta_{{}_{Y}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left\{\left(\xi,\eta\right)\ \in\ \mathbb{R}^{{}^{n}}\oplus\ \mathbb{R}\ \bigg|\ \eta\geq\varphi(\xi)\right\}\ \ \ \subseteq\ \ \ \mathbb{R}^{{}^{n+1}}.

(198)

Let
Y
be the noncompact
(n+1)
-dimensional toric variety defined by the moment polytope
\Delta_{{}_{Y}}
.
Y
is also described by the vectors

(-\alpha,1)\ ,\ \alpha\in A

(199)

and in which the vectors

(-\alpha_{{}_{1}},1),...,(-\alpha_{{}_{k}},1)

(200)

span a cone iff
\alpha_{{}_{1}},...,\alpha_{{}_{k}}
span a cell of
{\cal P}
.

Also, defining

{\cal W}\ =\ -z^{{}^{(0,...,0,1)}}\ \in\ {\cal O}(Y).

(201)

The irreducible toric divisors of
Y
are indexed by the elements of
A
; denoting by
Z_{{}_{\alpha}}
the divisor corresponding to the ray
(\alpha,1)
of
\Sigma_{{}_{Y}}
, i.e. the facet of
\Delta_{{}_{Y}}
given by the graph of
\varphi
over the region where the maximum in
\varphi(\xi)
is achieved by
\alpha
. The superpotential,
{\cal W}
, is then the toric monomial that vanishes to order 1 on each toric divisor
Z_{{}_{\alpha}}
, namely

{\cal W}^{{}^{-1}}(0)\ =\ \bigcup_{{}_{\alpha\in A}}Z_{{}_{\alpha}}

(202)

as the union of all toric strata.

The HMS direction being used here predicts an equivalence between the derived wrapped Fukaya category of
H
and the derived category of singularities of the LG model
(Y,{\cal W})
,

WFuk(H)\ \simeq\ D_{{}_{sg}}^{{}^{b}}(Z),

(203)

where

D_{{}_{sg}}^{{}^{b}}(Z)\ \equiv\ D^{{}^{b}}\text{Coh}(Z)/\text{Perf}(Z)

(204)

on the regular fiber

Z\ \equiv\ {\cal W}^{{}^{-1}}(0)\ =\ \bigcup_{{}_{\alpha\in A}}Z_{{}_{A}}.

(205)

WFuk(H)
only depends of the set
A\subset\mathbb{Z}^{{}^{n}}
, not on the coefficients
c_{{}_{\alpha}}
, nor on
\rho
, since hypersurfaces corresponding to different choices are deformation equivalent Liouville (or Stein) submanifolds of
\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)^{{}^{n}}
. On the other hand,
Y
depends on the polyhedral decomposition
{\cal P}
of Conv
(A)
, hence, different choices of
\rho
can yield different mirrors. However, these mirrors are birational to each other (related by flops), and therefore, the resulting derived categories of singularities are expected to be equivalent.

Conjecture

Z\ =\ \bigcup_{{}_{\alpha}}Z_{{}_{\alpha}}\ \subset\ Y

(206)

is mirror to the complement

\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)^{{}^{n}}\ \backslash\ H,

(207)

and there is a commutative diagram

where
\rho
and
p
denote the restriction and quotient projection functor, respectively.

\rho
singles-out the ends of a Lagrangian submanifold of
\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)^{{}^{n}}\backslash H
which lie on the missing divisor
H
.
\rho
is the composition of restriction to a neighbourhood of
H
isomorphic to the product of
H
with a punctured disk
\mathbb{D}^{{}^{*}}
, and projection from
H\times\mathbb{D}^{{}^{*}}
to
H
.

From the description of the commutative diagram we have just provided, some additional remarks are in order:

•

The top row in the diagram above fits with the general philosophy that removing a divisor from a symplectic manifold
\left(\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)^{{}^{n}}\right)
should correspond to a degeneartion of its mirror to a singular space (namely
Z
). The level set of
{\cal W}
provides exactly such degeneration.

•

\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)^{{}^{n}}\backslash H
can itself be viewed as a hypersurface in
\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}\right)^{{}^{n+1}}
, defined by

\hat{f}\left(x_{{}_{1}},...,x_{{}_{n+1}}\right)\ =\ f\left(x_{{}_{1}},...,x_{{}_{n+1}}\right)+x_{{}_{n+1}}\ =\ 0.

(208)

The mirror to the hypersurface (
208
) is conjectured to be the toric LG model

\left(\hat{Y},\hat{\cal W}\right)\ =\ \left(\mathbb{C}\times Y,y{\cal W}\right)

(209)

where
y
is the coordinate on the
\mathbb{C}
-factor. Orlov proved that the category of singularities of the LG model
\left(\hat{Y},\hat{\cal W}\right)
is equivalent to
D
Coh
\left({\cal W}^{{}^{-1}}(0)\ =\ Z\ \subset\ Y\right)
.

8
\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}

We now switch gears to a slightly more general manifestation of HMS, namely
\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}
. In such case, HMS ceases to be marely a correspondence among manifolds, since one side involves a Fano variety. The idea that quantum cohomology ring is equivalent to the Jacobian ring begins from one of Givental’s Theorem, stating that the D-module defined by an oscillatory integral with the superpotential as its kernel is isomorphic to
S^{{}^{1}}
-equivariant Floer cohomology pf periodic Hamiltonian systems.
The main idea in this example is that of combining such information to extract information on Lagrangian submanifolds.

8.1
Equivariant cohomology

Equivariant cohomology, a.k.a. Borel cohomology, is a cohomology theory from algebraic topology that applies to topological spaces equipped with a group action. It can be viewed as a common generalisation of group cohomology and an ordinary cohomology theory.

The equivariant cohomology ring of a space
X
with actionn of a topological group
G
is defined as the ordinary cohomology wing with coefficient ring
\Lambda
of the homotopy quotient

H^{{}^{\bullet}}_{{}_{G}}\ (X;\Lambda)\ =\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(EG\times_{{}_{G}}X;\Lambda\right).

(210)

If
G
is the trivial group, then (
210
) reduces to the ordinary idempotent ring of
X
, wheras, if
X
is contractible, it reduces to the cohomology ring of the classifying space
BG
. If
G
acts freely on
X
, then the canonical map

EG\times_{{}_{G}}X\ \rightarrow\ X/G

(211)

is a homotopy equivalence, and (
210
) reduces to

H^{{}^{\bullet}}_{{}_{G}}\ (X;\Lambda)\ =\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(X/G;\Lambda).

(212)

8.2
A-side on
\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}

Given a symplectic manifold,
M
, and an
S^{{}^{1}}
-invariant symplectic form,
\omega
, we have

\omega(\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}})\ =\ 2\pi.

(213)

Let

\mu:\ \mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}\rightarrow[0,1]

(214)

be the associated moment map, and let
L_{{}_{u}}\overset{def.}{=}\mu^{{}^{-1}}(u)\subset\mathbb{CP^{{}^{1}}}
be the moment fiber Lagrangian over an interior point
u\in(0,1)
. By identifying
L_{{}_{u}}
with
S^{{}^{1}}
via the
S^{{}^{1}}
-action, we choose an orientation of
L_{{}_{u}}
such that it is compatible with that of
S^{{}^{1}}
.

Let

\mathfrak{g}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ lie(S^{{}^{1}})\ \otimes\ \mathbb{C}.

(215)

Take a basis
e_{{}_{1}}
of
\mathfrak{g}
consistent with the orientation of
S^{{}^{1}}
and an integral basis

e^{{}^{1}}\ \in\ \Omega^{{}^{1}}(L_{{}_{u}})^{{}^{\mathfrak{g}}}\ \simeq\ H^{{}^{1}}(L_{{}_{u}}),

(216)

such that
e^{{}^{1}}
coincides with the dual basis
e^{{}^{1}}\ \in\ \mathfrak{g}^{{}^{\text{V}}}
via the identification
S^{{}^{1}}\rightarrow L_{{}_{u}}
given by the
S^{{}^{1}}
-action.

This time, the holomorphic disks contributing to the equivariant Floer
A_{{}_{\infty}}
algebras of
L_{{}_{u}}
. Those projecting on
[0,u]\ \subset\ [0,1]
via
\mu
, and those projecting on
[u,1]\ \subset\ [0,1]
, with respective relative cohomology classes
\beta_{{}_{1}},\beta_{{}_{2}}\ \in\ H_{{}_{2}}(\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}},L_{{}_{u}};\mathbb{Z})
.

Figure 9:

Then,

<e^{{}^{1}},\partial\beta_{{}_{1}}>\ =\ 1\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \omega(\beta_{{}_{2}})\ =\ 2\pi\ (1-u)\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \partial\beta_{{}_{2}}\ =\ -\partial\beta_{{}_{1}}.

(217)

Take a closed
S^{{}^{1}}
-invariant 1-form

\mathfrak{b}\ =\ \mathfrak{b}_{{}_{o}}\ +\ \mathfrak{b}_{{}_{+}}

(218)

where

\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{o}}\ \in\ \Omega^{{}^{1}}(L_{{}_{u}})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mathfrak{b}_{{}_{+}}\ \in\ \Omega^{{}^{1}}(L_{{}_{u}})\hat{\otimes}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}\ \Lambda_{{}_{+}}

(219)

and define

c_{{}_{o}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ e^{{}^{<}\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{o}},\partial\beta_{{}_{1}}>}\ \in\ \mathbb{C}\ \ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ 1+c_{{}_{+}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ e^{{}^{<}\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{+}},\partial\beta_{{}_{1}}>}\ \in\ \Lambda_{{}_{+}}.

(220)

Upon calculating the index in terms of the equivariant Floer
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-algebra, we get

\mu_{{}_{o}}^{{}^{\mathfrak{b}}}(1)\ =\ T^{{}^{\omega(\beta_{{}_{1}})/2\pi}}e^{{}^{<\mathfrak{b},\partial\beta_{{}_{1}}>}}+T^{{}^{\omega(\beta_{{}_{2}})/2\pi}}e^{{}^{<\mathfrak{b},\partial\beta_{{}_{2}}>}}.

(221)

Making use of (
220
), (
221
) becomes

\mu_{{}_{o}}^{{}^{\mathfrak{b}}}(1)\ =\ T^{{}^{u}}c_{{}_{o}}(1+c_{{}_{+}})\ +\ T^{{}^{1-u}}c_{{}_{o}}^{{}^{-1}}(1+c_{{}_{+}})^{{}^{-1}}.

(222)

Next, the differential is calculated as

\displaystyle\mu_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{\mathfrak{b}}}(e^{{}^{1}})

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{{}_{\beta}}T^{{}^{\omega(\beta)/2\pi}}e^{{}^{<\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{o}},\partial\beta>}}\sum_{{}_{l_{{}_{o}}+l_{{}_{1}}=l}}\mu_{{}_{l+1,\beta}}\left(\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{+}},...,\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{+}},e^{{}^{1}},\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{+}},...,\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{+}}\right)\ +\ <\lambda,e^{{}^{1}}>

(223)

\displaystyle=\ T^{{}^{u}}c_{{}_{o}}(1+c_{{}_{+}})\ -\ T^{{}^{1-u}}c_{{}_{o}}^{{}^{-1}}(1+c_{{}_{+}})^{{}^{-1}}\ +\ <\lambda,e^{{}^{1}}>.

Givental’s potential function for
\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}

{\cal F}\ =\ X+\frac{T}{X}\ +\ \lambda\log{X}

(224)

is recovered from (
221
) by introducing the variable

X\ \overset{def.}{=}\ T^{{}^{u}}c_{{}_{o}}(1+c_{{}_{+}}).

(225)

In this reformulation, (
223
) can thus be redefined in the following way

\partial{\cal F}\ =\ X-\frac{T}{X}\ -\ \lambda\ =\ 0

(226)

corresponding to the equation for the critical points of the potential (
224
), where
\partial\overset{def.}{=}\partial/\partial X
. We associate an equivariant Lagrangian brane to a solution
X
of
\partial F=0
satisfying

0\ <\ \text{val}(X)\ <\ 1

(227)

as follows: take the moment fibre
L\equiv L_{{}_{u}}
, where
u\equiv\text{val}(X)
, take
c_{{}_{o}}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, and
c_{{}_{+}}\in\Lambda_{{}_{+}}
such that (
225
) is satisfied. The positive valuation part of the bounding cochain,
\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{+}}
is given by

\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{+}}\ =\ \log\ (1+c_{{}_{+}})e^{{}^{1}}.

(228)

The leading term

\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{o}}\ =\ b_{{}_{o}}e^{{}^{1}}

(229)

is taken such that

e^{{}^{b_{{}_{o}}}}\ =\ c_{{}_{o}},

(230)

and gives a local system on
L_{{}_{u}}
with monodromy
c_{{}_{o}}
.

Then,
\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{o}}+\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{+}}
gives a bounding cochain since
e^{{}^{1}}
is an invariant form, and the resulting equivariant Lagrangian brane therefore consists of the triple

{\cal L}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ (L_{{}_{u}},\rho,\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{+}}).

(231)

Solutions to (
226
) can be classified in 2 types according to the valuation of
\lambda
:

1.

If
\text{val}(\lambda)\geq\frac{1}{2}
, both solutions have valuation in
u_{{}_{1}}=u_{{}_{2}}=\frac{1}{2}
, i.e. consider two Lagrangian branes with the same underlying Lagrangian submanifold
L_{{}_{\frac{1}{2}}}
, but different bounding cochains.

2.

If
0\leq\text{val}(\lambda)<\frac{1}{2}
, then the solutions have
u_{{}_{1}}=\text{val}(\lambda)
,
u_{{}_{2}}=1-\text{val}(\lambda)
, corresponding to two disjoint underlying Lagrangians
L_{{}_{u_{{}_{1}}}},L_{{}_{u_{{}_{2}}}}
.

Note that, for
\text{val}(\lambda)=0
, the Lagrangians collapse to points. We thereby restrict to the
\text{val}(\lambda)>0
case.

Denote the corresponding maximal ideals by
\mathfrak{m}_{{}_{1}},\mathfrak{m}_{{}_{2}}\in\text{Crit}{\cal F}
, and the corresponding bounding cochains by

\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{i}}\ =\ \mathfrak{b}_{{}_{o,i}}+\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{+,i}}.

(232)

Since
\mu_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{i}},\lambda}}=0
, the cohomology algebra reads as follows

H\left(\Omega^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(L_{{}_{u_{{}_{i}}}}\right)^{{}^{\mathfrak{g}}}\underset{\mathbb{C}}{\hat{\otimes}}\ \Lambda,\mu_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{i}},\lambda}}\right)\ \simeq\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(L_{{}_{u_{{}_{i}}}}\right)\otimes_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}\Lambda.

(233)

as a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\Lambda
-vector spaces.

The multiplicative structure

For the case of the equivariant Floer
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-algebra,

\displaystyle 2\mu_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{b_{{}_{i}},\lambda}}\left(e^{{}^{1}},e^{{}^{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\ \mu_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{b_{{}_{i}},\lambda}}\left(e^{{}^{1}},e^{{}^{1}}\right)+\mu_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{b_{{}_{i}},\lambda}}\left(e^{{}^{1}},e^{{}^{1}}\right)

(234)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{}_{\beta}}T^{{}^{\omega(\beta)}/2\pi}e^{{}^{<b_{{}_{o,i}},\partial\beta>}}<b_{{}_{+,i}},\partial\beta>^{{}^{2}}\sum_{{}_{\ell}}\mu_{{}_{\ell}}\left(b_{{}_{+,i}},...,b_{{}_{+,i}}\right)

\displaystyle=T^{{}^{u}}c_{{}_{o}}\left(1+c_{{}_{+}}\right)+T^{{}^{1-u}}c_{{}_{o}}^{{}^{-1}}\left(1-c_{{}_{+}}\right)^{{}^{-1}}

\displaystyle=X+TX^{{}^{-1}}

\Rightarrow\ \ \ \boxed{\ \ \ 2\mu_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{b_{{}_{i}},\lambda}}\left(e^{{}^{1}},e^{{}^{1}}\right)\ =\ \partial^{{}^{2}}F\left(m_{{}_{i}}\right)\ \color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bigg]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

(235)

which is nontrivial since, by assumption,

\lambda\ \neq\ \pm 2\sqrt{-1}T^{{}^{1/2}}.

(236)

Let
