H\left(\Omega^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(L_{{}_{u_{{}_{i}}}}\right)^{{}^{\mathfrak{g}}}\underset{\mathbb{C}}{\hat{\otimes}}\ \Lambda,\mu_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{b_{{}_{i}},\lambda}},\left\{\mu_{{}_{k}}^{{}^{b_{{}_{i}},\lambda}}\right\}\right)

(237)

with the product defined as follows

[x]\cdot[y]\ (-1)^{{}^{|x|}}\left[\mu_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{b_{{}_{i}},\lambda}}(x,y)\right].

(238)

Then, we have an isomorphism of
\Lambda
-algebras

H\left(\Omega^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(L_{{}_{u_{{}_{i}}}}\right)^{{}^{\mathfrak{g}}}\underset{\mathbb{C}}{\hat{\otimes}}\ \Lambda,\mu_{{}_{k}}^{{}^{b_{{}_{i}},\lambda}}\right)\ \rightarrow\ Cl\left(-\hat{F}_{{}_{m_{{}_{i}}}}\right),

(239)

with
Cl\left(-\hat{F}_{{}_{m_{{}_{i}}}}\right)
denoting a generic element of the Clifford algebra. The latter can be lifted to a quasi-isomorphism of
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-algebras

\left(\Omega^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(L_{{}_{u_{{}_{i}}}}\right)^{{}^{\mathfrak{g}}}\underset{\mathbb{C}}{\hat{\otimes}}\ \Lambda,\left\{\mu_{{}_{k}}^{{}^{b_{{}_{i}},\lambda}}\right\}\right)\ \rightarrow\ Cl\left(-\hat{F}_{{}_{m_{{}_{i}}}}\right)_{{}_{\infty}},

(240)

8.3
B-side on (
\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
{\cal W}
)

8.3.1
Adic Topology

A linear topology of a ring
R
in which the fundamental system of neighbourhoods of zero consists of powers
\mathfrak{m}^{{}^{n}}
of some 2-sided ideal
\mathfrak{m}
. The topology is then said to be
\mathfrak{m}
-adic, and the ideal
\mathfrak{m}
is said to be the defining ideal of the topology.

The closure of any set
F\subset A
in the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology is equal to

\bigcap_{{}_{n\geq 0}}\left(F+\mathfrak{m}^{{}^{n}}\right)

(241)

In particular, the topology is said to be separable if and only if

\bigcap_{{}_{n\geq 0}}\mathfrak{m}^{{}^{n}}\ =\ 0.

(242)

The separable completion
\hat{R}
of the ring
R
in an
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology is isomorphic to the projective limit

\underset{\longleftarrow}{\lim}\left(R/\mathfrak{m}^{{}^{n}}\right).

(243)

The
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology of an
R
-module,
P
, is defined in a similar manner: its fundamental system of neighbourhoods of zero is given by the submodules
\mathfrak{m}^{{}^{n}}P
; in the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology,
P
becomes a topological
R
-module.

Let
R
be a commutative ring with identity with an
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology, and let
\hat{R}
be its completion; if
\mathfrak{m}
is an ideal of finite type, the topology in
\hat{R}
is
\hat{\mathfrak{m}}
-adic, and

\hat{\mathfrak{m}}^{{}^{n}}\ =\ \mathfrak{m}^{{}^{n}}\hat{R}.

(244)

If
\mathfrak{m}
is a maximal ideal, the
\hat{R}
is the local ring with maximal ideal
\hat{\mathfrak{m}}
.

A local ring topology is an adic topology defined by its maximal ideal (an
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology).

A fundamental tool in the study of adic topologies of rings is the following Lemma

Lemma
[Artin, Rees]

Let
R
be a commutative Noetherian ring, let
\mathbb{m}
be an ideal in
R
, let
E
be an
R
-module of finite type, and let
F
be a submodule of
E
. Then there is a
k
such that,
\forall n\geq 0
,

\mathfrak{m}^{{}^{n}}\left(\mathfrak{m}^{{}^{k}}E\cap F\right)\ =\ \mathfrak{m}^{{}^{k+n}}E\cap F

(245)

The topological interpretation of the ARtin-Rees Lemma shows that the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology of
F
is induced by the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology of
E
. It follows that:

1.

The completion
\hat{R}
of a ring
R
in the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology is a flat
R
-module.

2.

The completion
\hat{E}
of the
R
-module
E
of finite type is identical with

E\ \underset{R}{\otimes}\ \hat{R}.

(246)

3.

Krull’s Theorem holds.

Theorem
[Krull]

The
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology of a Noetherian
6
6

6

A ring,
R
, is said to be Noetherian if any ideal of
R
is finitely-generated.
ring is separable if and only if the set
1+\mathfrak{m}
contains no zero divisors. In particular, the topology is separable if
\mathfrak{m}
is contained in the (Jacobson) radical
7
7

7

The Jacobson radical
J(R)
of a ring
R
is the intersection of the maximal ideals of
R
. Since all maximal ideals are prime, the nilradical is contained in the Jacobson radical.
of the ring.

8.3.2
Matrix factorisation (MF)

Let
R
be a commutative regular
\mathbb{k}
-algebra. Take
{\cal W}\in R\backslash K
.A matrix factorisation of
{\cal W}
is defined by the pair
(P,dP)
, where

P\ =\ P^{{}^{0}}\ \oplus\ P^{{}^{1}}

(247)

is a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded, finitely-generated projective
R
-module, and
d_{{}_{P}}\ \in\ \text{End}^{{}^{odd}}(P)
is an
R
-linear morphism of odd degree with

d_{{}_{P}}^{{}^{2}}\ =\ {\cal W}\circ\ \text{id}_{{}_{P}}.

(248)

Then,
d_{{}_{P}}
consists of
\varphi\in\text{Hom}_{{}_{R}}(P^{{}^{1}},P^{{}^{0}})
and
\psi\in\text{Hom}_{{}_{R}}(P^{{}^{0}},P^{{}^{1}})
with

\varphi\ \circ\ \psi\ =\ {\cal W}\ \circ\text{id}_{{}_{P^{{}^{0}}}}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \psi\ \circ\ \varphi\ =\ {\cal W}\ \circ\ \text{id}_{{}_{P^{{}^{1}}}}

(249)

For MF
(P,d_{{}_{P}})
and
(P^{{}^{\prime}},d_{{}_{P^{{}^{\prime}}}})
, the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded module of
R
-linear morphisms from
P
to
P^{{}^{\prime}}
with a differential

d(f)\ =\ d_{{}_{P^{{}^{\prime}}}}\circ f-(-1)^{{}^{|f|}}f\circ d_{{}_{P}}

(250)

is denoted by MF(
P,P^{{}^{\prime}}
). Here
f
is a homogeneous
R
-linear morphisms and
|f|\ \in\ \mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
is the degree of
f
. This data defines a dg-category MF(
{\cal W}
), where composition of morphisms is naturally defined. Then,
[MF({\cal W})]
is a triangulated category.

Take

S\ \equiv\ R/{\cal W},

(251)

and let
D^{{}^{b}}(S)
be the derived category of complexes of
S
-modules with finitely-generated total cohomology.

A complex is
perfect
if it is quasi-isomorphic to a bounded complex of finitely-generated projective
S
-modules.

\underline{D}^{{}^{b}}(S)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ D^{{}^{b}}(S)/D^{{}^{b}}_{{}_{\text{Perf}}}(S)

(252)

defines a stabilised derived category of
S
, a.k.a. a stabilised derived category or a triangulated category of singularity, implying there is a triangulated functor

\text{cok}:\ [\text{MF}({\cal W})]\ \rightarrow\ \underline{D}^{{}^{b}}(S).

(253)

Let L be a finitely-generated
S
-module. Then, L is naturally considered as an object of
D^{{}^{b}}(S)
. A MF L
{}^{{}^{stab}}
is defined by

\text{cok}\left(\text{L}^{{}^{stab}}\right)\ =\ \text{L}

(254)

a.k.a. the stabilisation of L.

Let
f_{{}_{1}},...,f_{{}_{m}}
be a regular sequence in
R
and let
I\neq R
be the ideal generated by
f_{{}_{1}},...,f_{{}_{m}}
. Assume
I
contains
{\cal W}
. Take
w_{{}_{1}},...,w_{{}_{m}}\in R
with

{\cal W}\ =\ w_{{}_{1}}f_{{}_{1}}+w_{{}_{2}}f_{{}_{2}}+...+w_{{}_{m}}f_{{}_{m}}.

(255)

Let
V\simeq R^{{}^{m}}
be the free
R
-module of rank
m
with a basis
e_{{}_{1}},...,e_{{}_{m}}
and let
e_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{\text{V}}},...,e_{{}_{m}}^{{}^{\text{V}}}
be the dual basis. The contraction by
e_{{}_{i}}^{{}^{\text{V}}}
is denoted by
8
8

8

\bigwedge V
denotes the exterior algebra of a vector space
V
over a field,
\mathbb{k}
, defined as the quotient algebra of the tensor algebra,

\bigwedge V\ \overset{def.}{=}\ T(V)/I\ \ ,\ \ \ \ \text{where}\ \ \ \ \ \ T(V)\ =\ \bigoplus_{{}_{k=0}}^{{}^{\infty}}T^{{}^{k}}V.

(256)

c_{{}_{i}}\ \in\ \text{End}(\bigwedge V).

(257)

Defining
s_{{}_{o}},s_{{}_{1}}\ \in\ \text{End}(\bigwedge V)
as follows

s_{{}_{o}}\ =\ f_{{}_{1}}\iota_{{}_{1}}+f_{{}_{2}}\iota_{{}_{2}}+...+f_{{}_{m}}\iota_{{}_{m}}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ s_{{}_{1}}\ =\ w_{{}_{1}}e_{{}_{1}}\wedge+w_{{}_{2}}e_{{}_{2}}\wedge+...+w_{{}_{m}}e_{{}_{m}}\wedge,

(258)

where

\iota_{{}_{i}}\ \in\ \text{End}(\bigwedge V).

(259)

Then, the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
R
-module
\bigwedge V
equipped with odd degree morphism
s_{{}_{o}}+s_{{}_{1}}
is a MF of
{\cal W}
. The latter is a stabilisation of the
S
-module
R/I
.

Let Crit
({\cal W})
be the critical locus of
{\cal W}
, i.e. the scheme-theoretic zero-locus of d
{\cal W}
. Set

\text{Sing}({\cal W})\ =\ \text{Crit}({\cal W})\ \cap\ \text{Spec}(S)

(260)

which is the singular locus of Spec
(S)
. If Crit
({\cal W})
is zero-dimensional, then
{\cal W}
has isolated singularities. Let
\mathfrak{m}
be the maximal ideal of
R
, and let
\hat{R}_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
be the completion of the local ring
R_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
w.r.t. the
m
-adic topology. The element of
\hat{R}_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
corresponding to
{\cal W}
is denoted
\hat{w}_{{}_{m}}
. Then,
[\text{MF}(\hat{w}_{{}_{m}})]
is idempotent complete if
{\cal W}\in\mathfrak{m}
and
\hat{w}_{{}_{m}}
has isolated singularities. There is, therefore, a restriction functor from MF
({\cal W})
to MF
(\hat{w}_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}}})
.

By equivariance,

\text{cok}:\ [\text{MF}({\cal W})]\ \rightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(S),

(261)

and the restriction functor therefore leads to an equivalence between triangulated categories

\boxed{\ \ \ \overline{[\text{MF}({\cal W})]}\ \simeq\ \prod_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}\in\text{Sing}(S)}}[\text{MF}(\hat{w}_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}}})]\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bigg]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ }

(262)

if
S
features isolated singularities.

Define a dg-category of branes Br
({\cal W})
by

\text{Br}({\cal W})\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \prod_{{}_{c\in K}}\text{MF}({\cal W}-c).

(263)

Then, [Br
({\cal W})
] is a triangulated category. If
{\cal W}
has isolated singularities, then, the category of branes reads as follows

\overline{[\text{Br}({\cal W})]}\ \simeq\ \prod_{{}_{m\in\text{Crit}({\cal W})}}[\text{MF}(\hat{w}_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}}})].

(264)

Theorem

\left[\text{Equivariant HMS for $\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}$}\right]

There exists a fully-faithful
A_{{}_{infty}}
-functor

\phi:\ {\cal F}_{{}_{\cal L}}\ \rightarrow\ \text{Br}(F)_{{}_{\infty}}

(265)

for val
(\lambda)>0
and

\lambda\ \neq\ \pm 2\sqrt{-1}T^{{}^{1/2}}

(266)

by sending
\left(L_{{}_{u_{{}_{i}}}},\mathfrak{b}_{{}_{i}}\right)
, whose image split-generates
[\text{Br}(F)]
.

8.4
Categories of branes for Givental type potential functions

Given a commutative ring,
R
, and
f,g_{{}_{1}},...,g_{{}_{r}}\in R
,
\lambda_{{}_{1}},...,\lambda_{{}_{r}}\in\mathfrak{k}
, with
\mathfrak{k}
a regular algebra. Assume
g_{{}_{i}}
are invertible for every
i
. This data is usually encoded in the Givental potential

{\cal F}\ =\ f-\lambda_{{}_{1}}\log g_{1}-...-\lambda_{r}\log g_{{}_{r}}.

(267)

Although
{\cal F}
is not well-defined as a single-valued function, its differential can be defined as follows

d{\cal F}\ =\ df-\lambda_{{}_{1}}\frac{dg_{{}_{1}}}{g_{{}_{1}}}-...-\lambda_{{}_{r}}\frac{dg_{{}_{r}}}{g_{{}_{r}}},

(268)

with Crit
({\cal F})
defining the scheme-theoretic 0-locus of
d{\cal F}
. Assume that
{\cal F}
has isolated singularities, then

\text{dim}(\text{Crit}({\cal F}))\ =\ 0.

(269)

Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\text{Span}(R)
be a maximal ideal of
R
and
(\hat{R}_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}}},\hat{\mathfrak{m}})
be the completion w.r.t. the m-adic topology, with
\hat{\mathfrak{m}}
the maximal ideal. For
\mathfrak{h}\in R
, the corresponding element in
\hat{R}_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
is denoted by
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
.

For an invertible element
h\in\hat{R}_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}}}\backslash\hat{\mathfrak{m}}
, defining

\log\mathfrak{h}\ =\ \sum_{{}_{k=1}}^{{}^{\infty}}(-1)^{{}^{k-1}}\frac{1}{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{h}-\mathfrak{h}_{{}_{o}}}{\mathfrak{h}_{{}_{o}}}\right)^{{}^{k}}

(270)

where
\mathfrak{h}(0)\neq 0\in k
is the value of
\mathfrak{h}
at
\hat{\mathfrak{m}}
. Then,
\hat{\cal F}_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
at
{\mathfrak{m}}\in\text{Span}(R)
can be defined as

\hat{\cal F}_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \hat{f}_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}}}-\hat{f}_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}}}(0)-\lambda_{{}_{1}}\log\hat{g}_{{}_{1,m}}-...-\lambda_{{}_{r}}\hat{g}_{{}_{r,m}}\ \ \ \in\ \ \ \hat{\mathfrak{m}}.

(271)

Then, the dg-category of MF of Givental type potential
{\cal F}
is defined as

\text{Br}({\cal F})\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \prod_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}\in\text{Crit}({\cal F})}}\text{MF}(\hat{\cal F}_{{}_{\mathfrak{m}}}).

(272)

9
Kapustin-Orlov branes

9.1
Coisotropic branes

We start by introducing the notion of
coisotropic branes
, namely coisotropic objects in Fuk
(X,\omega)
,
4
,

Y\ \subset\ (X,\omega).

(273)

The simplest example is

\mathbb{C}^{{}^{n}}\ =\ \mathbb{R}^{{}^{n}}\oplus i\mathbb{R}^{{}^{n}}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \omega

(274)

with standard isotropic structure.

Take

\mathbb{R}^{{}^{n}}\oplus i\mathbb{R}^{{}^{k}}\ \ \ \subset\ \ \ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{n}}.

(275)

If
k=0
, it is half-dimensional, thereby implying it is actually Lagrangian. Hence, a co-isotropic one is such that it can take intermediate values in dimension, satisfying the following condition:

•

The restriction of
\omega
to
Y
has a constant rank.

•

The commutator of any two vector fields in
{\cal L}Y
also belongs to
{\cal L}Y
, with
{\cal L}Y
denoting the tangent bundle of the foliation.

From the data above, one can define the normal bundle of the foliation as the following quotient

{\cal F}\ =\ T_{{}_{Y}}/{\cal L}Y.

(276)

Interpreting
Y
as a first-class constraint surface in a phase space of a mechanical system, the meaning of the above foliation can be understood as follows: First-class constraints lead to gauge symmetries. A leaf in
Y
is precisely an orbit of a point under all gauge transformations.

Formally, the reduced phase space,
Y_{{}_{\text{red}}}
, describing gauge-invariant degrees of freedom, is the quotient of
Y
by the aforementioned gauge transformations. Equivalently,
Y_{{}_{\text{red}}}
is the space of leaves of the foliation
9
9

9

However, this space might not be a manifold, in particular, it might not even be a Hausdorff topological space. In general, it is unclear how to define the dynamics on
Y_{{}_{\text{red}}}
.
.

Additional structure:

\nabla
is a U(1)-connection on
Y
, whose curvature 2-form is

F\ =\ \frac{1}{2\pi i}\text{Curv}(\nabla),

(277)

such that

F^{{}^{b}}:\ T_{{}_{Y}}\ \longrightarrow\ T_{{}_{Y}}^{{}^{*}}

(278)

is required to vanish on the characteristic foliation, implying it is automatically integrable as a foliation. The characteristic foliation will be

\text{Char}\ =\ \mathbb{R}^{{}^{n+k}}\ \ \ \subset\ \ \ \mathbb{R}^{{}^{n}}\oplus i\mathbb{R}^{{}^{k}}.

(279)

On the complement part,

\omega^{{}^{-1}}F:\ T_{{}_{Y}}/\text{Char}\ \longrightarrow\ T_{{}_{Y}}/\text{Char}.

(280)

This is an almost complex structure, meaning

\left(\omega^{{}^{-1}}F\right)^{{}^{2}}\ =\ -1.

(281)

There are two conditions imposed on the 2-form. The underlying geometric structure is a coisotropic submanifold. On the character part (the Lagrangian part) it is a flat connection.

9.2
Fukaya category with a B-field

The effect of a
B
-field on
D^{{}^{b}}(X)
results in the fact that the sheaf
{\cal O}_{{}_{X}}
is replaced by a certain sheaf of noncommutative algebras. Importantly, in String Theory, the
B
-field makes the D-brane worldvolume noncommutative.

However, the effect of the
B
-field on the Fukaya category is quite different. For a 2D cymplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
, fix the almost complex structure
I
on
X
to be compatible with
\omega
, and thereby obtain a Hermitian metric on
X
. An object in the Fukaya category is a triple
(L,E,\nabla)
, where
L
is a Lagrangian submanifold,
E
is a trivial unitary vector bundle on
L
,
\nabla
is a flat connection on
E
.

Physically, this data is enough to specify the A-type boundary conditions for the classical
\sigma
-model, defining an A-type D-brane.

In this case, an object in the Fukaya category is defined as follows

\left(L,i,E,\nabla\right),

(282)

where
E
is a complex vector bundle on
L
,
\nabla
is a connection on
E
such that

F_{{}_{\nabla}}\ =\ 2\pi i\ d_{{}_{E}}B\bigg|_{{}_{L}}.

(283)

If
B=0
, then
F_{{}_{\nabla}}=0
. In the absence of the
B
-field, any oair
(L,i)
can be extended to an object of the Fukaya category. The situation is more complicated for
B\neq 0
. The original HMS correspondence required
E
to be a unitary connection. Nevertheless, this condition is too strong. Even in the case of the elliptic curve, one has to allow for non-unitary connections on the A-side if one wants to account for all bundles on the B-side.

Morphisms in the modified Fukaya category

Let

{\cal U}_{{}_{1}}\ =\ \left(L_{{}_{1}},i_{{}_{1}},E_{{}_{1}},\nabla_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ {\cal U}_{{}_{2}}\ =\ \left(L_{{}_{2}},i_{{}_{2}},E_{{}_{1}},\nabla_{{}_{1}}\right),

(284)

be two objects, such that
L_{{}_{1}},L_{{}_{2}}
intersect transversally. Morphisms from
{\cal U}_{{}_{1}}
to
{\cal U}_{{}_{2}}
in
Fuk(X)
form a complex of vector spaces defined by the rule

\text{Hom}\left({\cal U}_{{}_{1}},{\cal U}_{{}_{2}}\right)\ =\ \bigoplus_{{}_{x\in L_{{}_{1}}\cap L_{{}_{2}}}}\text{Hom}\left(E_{{}_{1}}|_{{}_{x}},E_{{}_{2}}|_{{}_{x}}\right).

(285)

(
285
) is graded in the following way: for each point
x\ \in\ L_{{}_{1}}\cap L_{{}_{2}}
, there are two points
i_{{}_{1}}(x)
and
i_{{}_{2}}(x)
on the universal cover of the Lagrangian Grassmannian of
T_{{}_{x}}X
. To these two points, we can associate an integer

\mu\left(i_{{}_{1}}(x),i_{{}_{2}}(x)\right),

(286)

constituting the Maslov index. By definition,
{Hom}^{{}^{i}}\left(E_{{}_{1}}|_{{}_{x}},E_{{}_{2}}|_{{}_{x}}\right)
has a
\mu\left(i_{{}_{1}}(x),i_{{}_{2}}(x)\right)
-grading.

The differential on (
285
) is defined by the rule

d(u)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{z\in L_{{}_{1}}\cap L_{{}_{2}}}}m_{{}_{1}}(u;z),

(287)

where

u\ \in\ \text{Hom}\left(E_{{}_{1}}|_{{}_{x}},E_{{}_{2}}|_{{}_{x}}\right),

(288)

and

m_{{}_{1}}(u;z)\ \in\ \text{Hom}\left(E_{{}_{1}}|_{{}_{z}},E_{{}_{2}}|_{{}_{z}}\right)

(289)

is given by

m_{{}_{1}}(u;z)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{\phi:D\rightarrow X}}\pm\exp\left(2\pi i\int_{{}_{D}}\phi^{{}^{*}}(-B+i\omega)\right)\ {\cal P}\ \exp\left(\oint_{{}_{\partial D}}\phi^{{}^{*}}\nabla\right),

(290)

where
\phi
is an (anti)-holomorphic map from the disk

D\ =\ \{|w|\leq 1,w\in\mathbb{C}\}

(291)

to
X
, such that

\phi(-1)\ =\ x\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \phi(1)\ =\ z

(292)

and

\phi([x,z])\ \subset\ L_{{}_{2}}\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \phi([z,x])\ \subset\ L_{{}_{1}}.

(293)

The path-ordered integral featuring in (
290
) is defined by the following rule

{\cal P}\ \exp\left(\oint_{{}_{\partial D}}\phi^{{}^{*}}\nabla\right)\ =\ {\cal P}\ \exp\left(\int_{{}_{x}}^{{}^{z}}\phi^{{}^{*}}\nabla_{{}_{2}}\right)\ u\ {\cal P}\ \exp\left(\int_{{}_{z}}^{{}^{x}}\phi^{{}^{*}}\nabla_{{}_{1}}\right).

(294)

10
Diffeomorphism classes of CY varieties

A longstanding problem in Geometry is the classification of geometric objects up to isomorphisms.

The minimal model (explain what this is).

In 1D, CY curves are genus-1 curves. In 2D, CY curves are K3-surfaces (not projective). In either case are they isomorphic. K3-surfaces are particularly important for Algebraic and Complex Geometry.

In higher D, the classification of CY manifolds is quite hard and many questions in the topological setting are still to be addressed. For example, the topology of CY manifolds of complex dimension greater or equal to 3 is not uniquely determined.

In this section, we will be focusing on simply-connected CY threefolds. Importantly, their diffeomorphism type is completely determined by:

•

The Hodge data.

•

The triple intersection in cohomology.

•

The 2
{}^{{}^{nd}}
Chern class.

We will be explaining each one of them in turn.

Example
The best example of a CY threefold is the smooth
quintic
hypersurface in projective space
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{4}}
, which is defined by a homogeneous polynomial of degree 5
10
10

10

\mathbb{P}^{{}^{4}}
is a particular example of a smooth toric Fano variety, playing a fundamental role in the construction of examples of CY manfolds. As we shall see, a toric Fano manifold ensures there always exists a codimension-1 submanifold that is CY.
.

10.1
Preliminary definitions

Homeomorphic:
An isomorphism in the category of topological spaces,
Top
, namely a homeomorphism

f:X\ \rightarrow\ Y

(295)

is a continuous map of topological spaces such that there is an inverse

f^{{}^{-1}}:\ Y\ \rightarrow\ X

(296)

that is also a continuous map of topological spaces.

Diffeomorphism:
Given two
k
-times differentiable manifolds (or smooth manifolds), a diffeomorphism

f:\ X\ \rightarrow\ Y

(297)

is a differentiable function such that there exists an inverse differentiable function
f^{{}^{-1}}
.

Relation to homeomorphisms: Every differomorphism is a homeomorphism, but the converse, in general, is not true.

Isomorphism:
Generalising the concept of bijection. It is an invertible morphism, hence a morphism with an inverse morphism.

Examples of isomorphisms:

1.

A bijection is an isomorphism in
Set
.

2.

A homeomorphism is an isomorphism in
Top
.

3.

A diffeomorphism is an isomorphism in
Diff
.

Divisor:
In Algebraic Geometry, a divisor in a given variety is a formal linear combination of sub-varieties of codimension-1

D\ =\ \sum_{{}_{i}}n_{{}_{i}}D_{{}_{i}}

(298)

on
X
induces a short exact sequence of abelian sheaves on
X
of the form

0\ \rightarrow\ {\cal O}_{{}_{X}}^{{}^{\times}}\ \rightarrow\ {\cal O}_{{}_{X}}\left(*Y\right)^{{}^{\times}}\ \overset{\nu_{{}_{Y}}}{\rightarrow}\ (\nu_{{}_{Y}})_{{}_{*}}\mathbb{Z}_{{}_{\tilde{Y}}}\ \rightarrow\ 0,

(299)

where
Y
is the
support
of
D
, namely

Y\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \bigcup_{{}_{i|n_{{}_{i}}\neq 0}}D_{{}_{i}}

(300)

and

\nu_{{}_{Y}}:\ \tilde{Y}\ \longrightarrow\ Y

(301)

is its
normalisation
in
Y
, corresponding to the map

\delta:\ H^{{}^{0}}\left(\tilde{Y},\mathbb{Z}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ H^{{}^{1}}\left(X,{\cal O}_{{}_{X}}^{{}^{\times}}\right).

(302)

Canonical Divisor:
The divisor corresponding to the canonical line bundle of an algebraic variety. Similarly, its
anticanonical divisor
is the additive inverse, corresponding to the anticanonical line bundle.

Canonical Line Bundle:
For
X
a complex manifold over the complex numbers, the K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler differential forms are holomorphic forms. For example, the canonical bundle for dim
{}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}(X)\ \equiv n
is
\Omega^{{}^{n,0}}
, a complex line bundle.

(Very) ample divisor:
An integral divisor
D
is very ample if
\phi:X\ \rightarrow\ \mathbb{P}^{{}^{n}}
defines an embedding of
X
.
D
is ample if
mD
, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, is very ample.

Toric varieties:
Irreducible varieties containing an algebraic torus as an open dense subset. An
n
-dimensional toroc variety is a veriety,
X
, containing an open dense subset isomorphic to

T\ =\ \text{Spec}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}\left[T_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{\pm 1}},...,T_{{}_{n}}^{{}^{\pm 1}}\right],

(303)

an affine variety equipped with an algebraic action

T\ \times\ X\ \rightarrow\ X

(304)

restricting to the multiplication action

T\ \times\ T\ \rightarrow\ T

(305)

implying a toric variety is essentially an equivariant enlargement of a torus.

10.2
CY

Let
X
be a complex manifold,
T_{{}_{X}}
its holomorphic tangent bundle.
X
is a CY of dimension
n
if it is a projective manifold with trivial canonical bundle and without holomorphic
p
-forms, i.e.

K_{{}_{X}}\ \equiv\ \Omega_{{}_{X}}^{{}^{p}}\ \simeq\ {\cal O}_{{}_{X}}\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ H^{{}^{0}}(X,\Omega_{{}_{X}}^{{}^{p}})\ =\ 0\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ p\ \in\ [0,n].

(306)

If dim
{}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}X=3\ \Rightarrow\ \Omega_{{}_{X}}^{{}^{3}}\ \simeq\ {\cal O}_{{}_{X}}
.
The
Hodge numbers
are defined as follows

h^{{}^{i,j}}(X)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \text{dim}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}H^{{}^{j}}\left(X,\Omega_{{}_{X}}^{{}^{i}}\right).

(307)

By renaming

h^{{}^{1,1}}(X)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ a\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ h^{{}^{1,2}}(X)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ b

(308)

from which to build a
Hodge diamond

\displaystyle 1

(309)

\displaystyle 0\ \ \ \ \ 0

\displaystyle 0\ \ \ \ \ a\ \ \ \ \ 0

\displaystyle 1\ \ \ \ \ b\ \ \ \ \ b\ \ \ \ \ 1

\displaystyle 0\ \ \ \ \ a\ \ \ \ \ 0

\displaystyle 0\ \ \ \ \ \ 0

\displaystyle 1

(
309
) shows that the topological type of CY manifold is not uniquely determined for 3 complex-dimensional manifolds. If
X_{{}_{1}},X_{{}_{2}}
are CY threefolds with different
a,b
, they are not homeomorphic. If they are the same, they are diffeomorphic.

10.3
Toric Fano Manifolds

A Fano manifold is a projective manifold,
F
, such that
-K_{{}_{X}}
is ample. If
F
is toric, then
-K_{{}_{X}}
is very ample. From Bertini’s Theorem, this implies that the generic section of
{\cal O}_{{}_{F}}(-K_{{}_{F}})
gives a smooth connected hypersurface
X\ \subseteq\ F
, such that
X\ \in\ |-K_{{}_{F}}|
, implying
X
is a smooth CY variety.

Importantly, this implies that, once we have a toric Fano manifold, then there always exists a submanifold of codimension-1 that is a CY manifold.
Hence, if
F
is 4 (complex)-dimensional, once can construct a 3D CY by means of the procedure outlined above.

Example

The projective space
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{4}}
is a smooth toric Fano manifold of dimension 4. The general quintic hypersurface is a smooth CY threefold. In such case,
a=1,b=101
.

10.4
Abstract and embedded deformations

The present subsection provides a brief overview of the deformations of a submanifold
X
in a manifold
F
. In particular, looking at the infinitesimal deformations of
X
as an abstract manifold ad in the embedded deformations
X
in
F
.

Abstract deformations

The functor of infinitesimal deformations of
X
as an abstract variety reads as follows

\text{Def}_{{}_{X}}:\ \text{Art}\ \rightarrow\ \text{Set},

(310)

where Def
{}_{{}_{X}}(A)
is the set of isomorphism classes of commutative diagrams

where
i,p_{{}_{A}}
denote a closed embedding and a flat morphism, respectively.

Embedded deformations

The counterpart of (
311
) for the case of embedded deformations of
X
in
F
is

H_{{}_{X}}^{{}^{F}}:\ \text{Art}\ \rightarrow\ \text{Set},

(311)

with
H_{{}_{X}}^{{}^{F}}(A)
now corresponding to the set of commutative diagrams

where
i,p_{{}_{A}}
have the same meaning as above.
In particular, there exists a well-defined forgetful morphism

\phi:\ H_{{}_{X}}^{{}^{F}}\ \rightarrow\ \text{Def}_{{}_{X}}

(312)

Example

Let
n\geq 4
, and
X
be the general anticanonical hypersurface in
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{n}}
.
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{n}}
is a smooth toric Fano variety and
X
a smooth CY manifold. For every CY manifold in a projective space,
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{n}}
, the embedded deformations of
X
in
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{n}}
are unobstructed.

All the infinitesimal deformations of the general anticanonical hypersurface
X
as an abstract variety are obtained as embedded deformations of
X
inside
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{n}}
.

A similar property holds for any smooth toric Fano variety, and not only for
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{n}}
.

Proposition

Let
F
be a smooth toric Fano variety with dim
F\ >\ 3
and denote by
X
a smooth connected hypersurface in
F
such that
X\ \in\ |-K_{{}_{F}}|
. Then, the forgetful morphism

\phi:\ H_{{}_{X}}^{{}^{F}}\ \rightarrow\ \text{Def}_{{}_{X}}

(313)

is smooth.

Lemma

Let
F
be a smooth toric Fano variety with dim
F\ >\ 3
. Then,

H^{{}^{2}}(F,T_{{}_{F}}\otimes{\cal O}_{{}_{F}}(K_{{}_{F}}))\ =\ 0.

(314)

10.5
Diffeomorphic 3D CY varieties

In 1966, C.T.C. Wall described the invariants that determine the diffeomorphism type of closed, smooth, simply-connected 6-manifolds with torsion-free cohomology.

Theorem [Wall]

Diffeomorphism classes of simply-connected, spin, oriented, closed 6-manifolds,
X
, with torsion-free cohomology correspond bijectively to isomorphism classes of systems of invariants consisting of

1.

Free abelian groups,
H^{{}^{2}}(X,\mathbb{Z}),H^{{}^{3}}(X,\mathbb{Z})
.

2.

A symmetric trilinear form

\mu:\ H^{{}^{2}}(X,\mathbb{Z})^{{}^{\otimes 3}}\ \rightarrow\ H^{{}^{6}}(X,\mathbb{Z})\ \simeq\ \mathbb{Z},

(315)

defined as follows

\mu(x,y,z)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ x\ \cup\ y\ \cup z,

(316)

with
x,y,z\ \in\ H^{{}^{2}}(X,\mathbb{Z})
.

3.

A linear map

p_{{}_{1}}:\ H^{{}^{2}}(X,\mathbb{Z})\ \rightarrow\ H^{{}^{6}}(X,\mathbb{Z})\ \simeq\ \mathbb{Z},

(317)

where

p_{{}_{1}}(x)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ p_{{}_{1}}(X)\ \cup\ x.

(318)

(
316
) and (
318
) are subject to the following constraints

\boxed{\ \ \ \ \mu(x,x,y)\ +\ \mu(x,y,y)\ =\ 0\ \ \ (\text{mod}\ 2)\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ 4\mu(x,x,x)\ -\ p_{{}_{1}}(X)\ =\ 0\ \ \ (\text{mod}\ 24).\ \color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bigg]\ \color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

(319)

Theorem

Let
(X,H)
be a very ample polarised CY threefold, i.e.
x=H
is a very ample divisor on
X
. Then, the following inequalities hold:

-36\mu(x,x,x)\ -\ 80\ \leq\ \frac{c_{{}_{3}}(X)}{2}\ =\ h^{{}^{1,1}}(X)-h^{{}^{1,2}}(X)\ \leq\ 6\mu(x,x,x)\ +\ 40.

(320)

If
X
is a CY threefold, then

p_{{}_{1}}(X)\ =\ -2c_{{}_{2}}(X)\ \in\ H^{{}^{4}}(X,\mathbb{Z})

(321)

and

\int_{{}_{X}}c_{{}_{3}}(X)\ =\ \chi(X)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{i=0}}^{{}^{6}}\ \text{dim}\ H^{{}^{i}}(X,\mathbb{R})\ =\ 2h^{{}^{1,1}}(X)-2h^{{}^{1,2}}.

(322)

Remark

By Wall’s Theorem, if
X
is simply-connected, spin, oriented, closed 6-manifold with torsion-free cohomology, then the diffeomorphism class is determined by the free abelian groups
H^{{}^{2}}(X,\mathbb{Z}),H^{{}^{3}}(X,\mathbb{Z})
and the form
\mu
and
p_{{}_{1}}
.

If
X
is CY,
\mu
and
p_{{}_{1}}
need to satisfy (
320
) outlined above. This is a necessary, but not sufficient condition for
X
to be CY.

In particular, let
X_{{}_{1}},X_{{}_{2}}
be simply-connected CY threefolds with torsion-free cohomology, and the same Hodge numbers

h^{{}^{1,1}}(X)\ =\ a\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ h^{{}^{1,2}}(X)\ =\ b.

(323)

To be diffeomorphic,
\mu,p_{{}_{1}}
should be the same, and satisfy (
320
).

Corollary

Let
X
be a CY threefold, with torsion-free cohomology, and

h^{{}^{1,1}}(X)\ =\ 1\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ h^{{}^{1,2}}(X)\ =\ h^{{}^{2,1}}(X)\ =\ b\ \ \ ,\ \ \ b\ \in\ \mathbb{N}.

(324)

Then,

H^{{}^{2}}(X,\mathbb{Z})\ \simeq\ \mathbb{Z}\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ H^{{}^{3}}(X,\mathbb{Z})\ \simeq\ \mathbb{Z}^{{}^{2+b}}.

(325)

Fix a generator,
H\ \in H^{{}^{2}}(X,\mathbb{Z})
, and
\mu(H,H,H)=m\ \in\ \mathbb{Z}
, then

m\ \geq\ \frac{b-81}{36}.

(326)

Example

If
m=5
, then
b\ \leq\ 261
. For
b=101
, we recover the generic quintic threefold in
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{4}}
.

10.6
Remarks on the moduli space of CY manifolds and mirror symmetry

Given
X
a CY, and
H
a primitive ample divisor, let
M_{{}_{m,c}}
denote the space of polarised varieties
11
11

11

Very little is known about the geometric structure of
M_{{}_{m,c}}
.

(X,H)
such that
H^{{}^{3}}=m
and
c_{{}_{2}}(H)H=c
for integers
m
and
c
.

Remark

Let
X
be a generic quintic in
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{4}}
. A hyperplane solution on
X
is a (very) ample divisor
H
such that
H^{{}^{3}}=5
.

10.6.1
The Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch Theorem

A deep result in Algebraic Geometry relating the Euler characteristic of vector bundles to characteristic classes. In turn, it is a generalisation to the relative setting of other theorems.

Theorem [Riemann-Roch]

Let
X
be a complete nonsingular curve of genus
g
. For any divisor
D
on
X
,

h^{{}^{0}}(D)\ -\ h^{{}^{0}}(K-D)\ =\ \text{deg}(D)+1-g

(327)

where
K
is a canonical divisor, and

h^{{}^{0}}(D)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \text{dim}_{{}_{\mathbb{K}}}H^{{}^{0}}(D)

(328)

over a base field
\mathbb{k}
.

Theorem [Riemann-Roch for Surfaces]

Let
X
be a complete nonsingular surface. For any divisor
D
on
X
,

\chi(D)\ =\ \chi({\cal O}_{{}_{X}})\ +\ \frac{1}{2}(D^{{}^{2}}-D-K)

(329)

Theorem [Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch]

Let
X
be a complete nonsingular variety. For any vector bundle
E
on
X
,

\chi(X,E)\ =\ \int_{{}_{X}}\text{ch}(E)\ \cdot\ \text{td}(T_{{}_{X}})

(330)

with ch
(E)
the Chern character, and td
T_{{}_{X}})
the Todd class.
This is a complex analysis result translated into Algebraic Geometry.

Theorem [Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch]

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a proper morphism of nonsingular varieties with
X
quasi-projective. Then, for any
\alpha\in K(X)
,

\text{ch}(f_{{}_{*}}\alpha)\cdot\text{td}(T_{{}_{Y}})\ =\ f_{{}_{*}}\left(\text{ch}(\alpha)\cdot\text{td}(T_{{}_{X}})\right).

(331)

For
Y=\text{Spec}(\mathbb{k})
, it reduces to the HRR Theorem.

The Chern and Todd class are defined as follows.

Chern classes
For a given scheme,
X
, Chern classes associate to each vector bundle
E
on
X
a collection of classes
c_{{}_{k}}(E)\ \in\ A^{{}^{k}}(X)
reflecting the geometry of
E
. This is succinctly denoted as follows:

c(E)\ =\ 1+c_{{}_{1}}(E)+...

(332)

Todd class

Suppose
E
admits a complete filtration

E\ =\ E_{{}_{r}}\ \supset\ E_{{}_{r-1}}\ \supset\ ...\ \supset E_{{}_{1}}\ \supset\ E_{{}_{0}}\ =\ 0

(333)

of subbundles, such that rank
E_{{}_{K}}=k
.
Let

L_{{}_{k}}\ =\ E_{{}_{k}}/E_{{}_{k-1}}\ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \text{for}\ \ \ 1\leq k\leq r.

(334)

Then, by the Whitney sum formula,

c(E)\ =\ \prod_{{}_{k=1}}^{{}^{r}}c(L_{{}_{k}})\ =\ \prod_{{}_{k=1}}^{{}^{r}}(1+\alpha_{{}_{k}})

(335)

where
\alpha_{{}_{k}}=c_{{}_{1}}(L_{{}_{k}})
.
\alpha_{{}_{k}}
are referred to as the Chern roots of
E
. The Chern character then reads

\displaystyle\text{ch}(E)

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{{}_{k=1}}^{{}^{r}}\text{exp}(\alpha_{{}_{k}})

(336)

\displaystyle=\ r+c_{{}_{1}}+\frac{1}{2}(c_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{2}}-2c_{{}_{2}})+\frac{1}{6}(c_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{3}}-3c_{{}_{1}}c_{{}_{2}}+c_{{}_{3}})+...

where
r
denotes the rank of
K
.
The Todd class is then defined as

\text{td}(E)\ =\ \prod_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{r}}Q(\alpha_{{}_{i}}),

(337)

where

Q(\alpha)\ =\ \frac{\alpha}{1-e^{{}^{-\alpha}}}\ =\ 1+\frac{1}{2}\alpha+\sum_{{}_{k=1}}^{{}^{\infty}}(-1)^{{}^{k-1}}\frac{B_{{}_{k}}}{(2k)!}\alpha^{{}^{2k}}

(338)

with
B_{{}_{k}}
denoting the Bernoulli numbers.

The Todd class therefore reads

\text{td}(E)\ =\ 1+\frac{1}{2}c_{{}_{1}}+\frac{1}{12}(c_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{2}}+c_{{}_{2}})+\frac{1}{24}c_{{}_{1}}c_{{}_{2}}+...

(339)

10.6.2
Mirror symmetry considerations

On a CY manifold,
X
, The GRR Theorem reads as follows

\chi(H)\ =\ \frac{H^{{}^{3}}}{6}+\frac{1}{12}c_{{}_{2}}(X)H

(340)

For
H
a divisor on
X
,

\chi(H)\ =\ \chi({\cal O}_{{}_{H}}(H)).

(341)

Example

A linear section of a quintic is a surface in 3D projective space. The 2
nd
Betti number is 53, hence, the Euler characteristic is 55. Therefore, we get

\chi({\cal O}_{{}_{H}}(H))\ =\ 5\ \ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \ 5\ =\ \frac{H^{{}^{3}}}{6}+\frac{1}{12}c_{{}_{2}}(X)H\ \ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \ c_{{}_{2}}(X)H=50

(342)

Therefore, the pair
(X,H)
belongs to the space
M_{{}_{5,50}}
where
X
is a quintic in
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{4}}
and
H
is a hyperplane section. The Hodge numbers of
X
are given by
(a,b)=(1,101)
. For its mirror,
\tilde{X}
,
(a,b)=(101,1)
.

Proposition

Given a pair
(X,H)
as previously defined, there is a primitive ample divisor
D
on
X^{{}^{\prime}}
such that
(\tilde{X},D)
belongs to
M_{{}_{5,50}}
.

Example

For the generic quintic threefold,
X
, in
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{4}}
,
a=1,b=101,m=5,c=50
, satisfying the previous conditions, (
319
). Therefore,
X
lies in the space

M_{{}_{5,50}}\ =\ \{(X,H)|H^{{}^{3}}=5,c_{{}_{2}}(X)\cdot H=50\}

(343)

The mirror
\tilde{X}
of
X
is a smooth CY threefold with
a=101,b=1,m=5,c=50
. So, it lies in the same space
M_{{}_{5,50}}
, but the Hodge numbers are exchanged, implying
X,\tilde{X}
are, neither, diffeomorphic nor deformation equivalent.

11
Kapustin-Orlov branes: further details

To any complex torus equipped with a flat K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler metric and a closed 2-form, they associate an
{\cal N}=2
superconfromal vertex algebra (SCVA)
12
12

12

See
VOA
.
.

11.1
Bockstein homomorphism

A Bockstein homomorphism is a connecting homomorphism associated with a short exact sequence of abelian groups

0\ \longrightarrow\ P\ \longrightarrow\ R\ \longrightarrow\ 0,

(344)

introduced as coefficients into a chain complex,
C
,

\beta:\ H_{{}_{i}}(C,R)\ \longrightarrow\ H_{{}_{i-1}}(C,P),

(345)

such that

\beta\beta\ =\ 0\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \beta(a\cup b)\ =\beta(a)\ \cup\ b+(-1)^{{}^{\text{dim}a}}a\cup\beta(b).

(346)

11.2
Grothendieck group

The Grothendieck group of an additive category is an Abelian group that is assigned to it by a universal additive mapping property.

Let
C
be a small additive category with set objects, Ob
(C)
, and let
G
be an Abelian group. A mapping

\phi:\ \text{Ob}(C)\ \longrightarrow\ G

(347)

is said to be additive if for any exact sequence

0\ \longrightarrow\ L\ \longrightarrow\ M\ \longrightarrow\ N\ \longrightarrow\ 0

(348)

in
C
, the relation

\phi(M)\ =\ \phi(L)+\phi(N)

(349)

is valid.

There exists a group
K_{{}_{o}}(C)
, called the Grothendieck group of
C
, and an additive mapping

K_{{}_{o}}:\ \text{Ob}(C)\ \longrightarrow\ G

(350)

a.k.a. the universal mapping, such that, for any additive mapping

\text{Ob}(C)\ \longrightarrow\ G

(351)

there exists a unique homomorphism

\xi:\ K_{{}_{o}}(C)\ \longrightarrow\ G

(352)

such that

\phi\ =\ \xi\circ K_{{}_{o}}

(353)

This construction was first studied by Grothendieck for categories of coherent and locally free sheaves on schemes in proving the Riemann-Roch Theorem.

The group
K_{{}_{o}}(C)
is uniquely defined (up to isomorphism) and can be given by generators,
[L]
, one for each object
L\subset C
, and by the following relations

[L]\ -\ [N]\ -\ [M]\ =\ 0

(354)

for each exact sequence

0\ \longrightarrow\ L\ \longrightarrow\ M\ \longrightarrow\ N\ \longrightarrow\ 0.

(355)

11.3
Picard group

A Picard group is an Abelian group defined for a symmetric monoidal category as the group of its isomorphism classes of objects which are invertible w.r.t. the tensor product.

Most often, Pic
(X)
is a space of invertible objects in a monoidal category of vector bundles over
X
. For the case in which
X
is a complex manifold, these objects are holomorphic vector bundles, thereby implying Pic
(X)
is an isomorphism class of holomorphic line bundles.

11.4
Physicist’s Mirror Symmetry

A CY triple is defined by the following

(X,G,{\cal B}),

(356)

where
X
is a compact complex manifold with a trivial canonical bundle,
G
is a Ricci-flat K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler metric on
X
,
{\cal B}
is a class in
H^{{}^{2}}(X,\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
is the kernel of the Bockstein homomorphism

H^{{}^{2}}(X,\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\ \longrightarrow\ H^{{}^{3}}(X,\mathbb{Z}).

(357)

The class
{\cal B}
can be lifted to a class
b\in H^{{}^{2}}(X,\mathbb{R})
, and a closed 2-form
B
representing it, conventionally referred to as the B-field.

Kapustin and Orlov understood that to each such triple, (
356
), one can associate an
{\cal N}=2
superconformal vertex algebra (SCVA), which we now turn to describing.

11.4.1
SCVAs

Vertex algebras play a crucial role in a Physicist’s formulation of HMS [Kapustin, Orlov]. A vertex algebra is an algebraic counterpart of a 2D CFT.

A chiral algebra is a vector superspace,
V
, together with a map

Y:\ V\ \longrightarrow\ \text{End}\ V\left[\left[z,z^{{}^{-1}}\right]\right]

(358)

satisfying certain properties. Specifically,
Y
maps states to quantum fields. Chiral algebras are CFTs such that all fields are meromorphic (i.e. independent w.r.t.
\overline{z}
). Only a very special class of CFTs have this property.

2 different quadruples
(\Gamma,I,G,B)
, and
(\Gamma^{{}^{\prime}},I^{{}^{\prime}},G^{{}^{\prime}},B^{{}^{\prime}})
yield isomorphic
{\cal N}=2
superconformal vertex algebras (SCVAs).

Denoting by

\Gamma^{{}^{*}}\ =\ \text{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathbb{Z})

(359)

the dual lattice in
U^{{}^{*}}
, and with
T^{{}^{*}}
the dual torus
U^{{}^{*}}/\Gamma^{{}^{*}}
, there is a natural pairing

l:\ \Gamma\ \oplus\ \Gamma^{{}^{*}}\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{Z}.

(360)

There is also a natural
\mathbb{Z}
-valued symmetric bilinear form
q
on
\Gamma\ \oplus\ \Gamma^{{}^{*}}
defined as follows

q((w_{{}_{1}},m_{{}_{1}}),(w_{{}_{2}},m_{{}_{2}}))\ =\ l(w_{{}_{1}},m_{{}_{2}})+l(w_{{}_{2}},m_{1}),

(361)

with
w_{{}_{1,2}}\in\Gamma
and
m_{{}_{1,2}}\in\Gamma^{{}^{*}}
. Given
G,I<B
, we can define two complex structures on
T\times T^{{}^{*}}

{\cal I}(I,B)\ =\ \left(\begin{matrix}&I\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\\
&BI+I^{{}^{t}}B\ \ \ \ \ -I^{{}^{t}}\\
\end{matrix}\right)

(362)

{\cal J}(G,I,B)\ =\ \left(\begin{matrix}&-IG^{{}^{-1}}B\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ IG^{{}^{-1}}\\
&GI-BIG^{{}^{-1}}B\ \ \ \ \ \ BIG^{{}^{-1}}\\
\end{matrix}\right),

(363)

where

{\cal I\ ,\ J}\ \ \ \in\ \ \ \text{End}\left(U\oplus U^{{}^{*}}\right),

(364)

G\ ,\ B\ \ \ \in\ \ \ \text{Hom}\left(U,U^{{}^{*}}\right)\ \ \ ,\ \ \ I^{{}^{t}}\ =\ \text{End}\left(U^{{}^{*}}\text{conj. to}I\right).

(365)

{\cal J}
depends on
G,I
only by means of the combination
\omega=GI
, and therefore only depends on the symplectic structure on
T
and the
B
-field.

Two main theorems stated by Kapustin and Orlov are presented for completeness.

Theorem 2.1
[Kapustin, Orlov]

Vert
(\Gamma,I,G,B)
is isomorphic to Vert
(\Gamma^{{}^{\prime}},I^{{}^{\prime}},G^{{}^{\prime}},B^{{}^{\prime}})
if and only if there exists an isomorphism of lattices
\Gamma\oplus\Gamma^{{}^{*}}
, and
\Gamma^{{}^{\prime}}\oplus\Gamma^{{}^{*\prime}}
, which takes
q
to
q^{{}^{\prime}}
,
{\cal I}
to
{\cal I}^{{}^{\prime}}
and
{\cal J}
to
{\cal J}^{{}^{\prime}}
.

Theorem 2.2
[Kapustin, Orlov]

Vert
(\Gamma,I,G,B)
is mirror to Vert
(\Gamma^{{}^{\prime}},I^{{}^{\prime}},G^{{}^{\prime}},B^{{}^{\prime}})
if and only if there is an isomorphism of lattices
\Gamma\oplus\Gamma^{{}^{*}}
and
\Gamma^{{}^{\prime}}\oplus\Gamma^{{}^{\prime*}}
, which takes
q
to
q^{{}^{\prime}}
,
{\cal I}
to
{\cal J}^{{}^{\prime}}
and
{\cal J}
to
{\cal I}^{{}^{\prime}}
.

11.4.2
Kapustin-Orlov branes: non-Lagrangians

Given the Grothendieck group of
D^{{}^{b}}(X)
, the Chern character reads

\text{ch}:\ K_{{}_{o}}\left(D^{{}^{b}}(X)\right)\otimes\mathbb{Q}\ \rightarrow\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(X,\mathbb{Q}).

(366)

Its image is contained in

H^{{}^{\bullet}}(X,\mathbb{Q})\cap\left(\bigoplus_{{}_{p}}H^{{}^{p,p}}(X)\right)\ \ \ \subset\ \ \ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(X,\mathbb{C}).

(367)

In particular, dim Im(ch) can jump. For the case of abelian varieties, this is particularly manifest.

\text{Im(ch)}\otimes\mathbb{C}\ =\ \bigoplus_{{}_{p}}H^{{}^{p,p}}\ \ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \ \text{dim}_{{}_{\mathbb{Q}}}\text{Im(ch)}\ =\ \left(\begin{matrix}&2n\\
&n\end{matrix}\right).

(368)

In particular, there exists a functor

\Phi:\ K_{{}_{o}}\left(D{\cal F}\left(A,\omega_{{}_{A}}\right)\right)\otimes\mathbb{Q}\ \rightarrow\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(A,\mathbb{Q})

(369)

closing the following commutative diagram

Under
\Phi
, a flat vector bundle on a Lagrangian submanifold goes to the corresponding cycle in the middle cohomology
H^{{}^{n}}(A,\mathbb{Q})
. Such Lagrangians belong to the kernel of a surjective map

H^{{}^{n}}(A,\mathbb{C})\ \xrightarrow{\cdot\left[\omega_{{}_{A}}\right]}\ H^{{}^{n+2}}(A,\mathbb{C})

(370)

whose dimension is

\left(\begin{matrix}&2n\\
&n\end{matrix}\right)-\left(\begin{matrix}&2n\\
&n+2\end{matrix}\right)\ <\ \text{dim Im(ch)}.

(371)

Denoting by
B=E_{{}_{\tau}}^{{}^{n}}
the elliptic curve with complex multiplication, Lagrangian submanifolds with flat vector bundle do not generate a mirror of
D^{{}^{b}}(B)
.

Theorem

There is a cohomologically fully-faithful
A_{{}_{\infty}}
functor for val
(\lambda)>0
and
\lambda\neq\pm\sqrt{-1}T^{{}^{1/2}}
,

\boxed{\ \ \ \phi:\ {\cal F}_{{}_{\cal L}}\ \rightarrow\ \text{Br}({\cal W})_{{}_{\infty}}\ \color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bigg]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

(372)

The mirror correspondence induces an isomorphism of the cohomology vector spaces

\beta:\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(B,\mathbb{Q})\ \xrightarrow{\sim}\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(A,\mathbb{Q}).

(373)

Lagrangian branes in
A
with flat vector bundles cannot generate the mirror of
D^{{}^{b}}(B)
.

To obtain the mirror of
D^{{}^{b}}(B)
, look at the mirror symmetry correspondence for
B=E_{{}_{\tau}}^{{}^{n}}
more explicitly. For simplicity,
\tau=i
, such that
E_{{}_{\tau}}
is a square torus. Consider a decomposition of the lattice

H^{{}^{1}}(B,\mathbb{Z})\ =\ \Gamma\ \oplus\ \Sigma

(374)

with bases

\Gamma\ =\ <x_{{}_{1}},...,x_{{}_{n}}>\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \Sigma\ =\ <y_{{}_{1}},...,y_{{}_{n}}>

(375)

such that

I_{{}_{B}}:\ x_{{}_{i}}\ \longrightarrow\ y_{{}_{i}}

(376)

I_{{}_{B}}:\ -y_{{}_{i}}\ \longrightarrow\ x_{{}_{i}}.

(377)

The mirror manifold,
A
, is a torus

\left(\Gamma^{{}^{*}}\oplus\Sigma\right)\ \otimes\ \mathbb{R}\ /\ \left(\Gamma^{{}^{*}}\oplus\ \Sigma\right)

(378)

equipped with a constant symplectic form

\omega_{{}_{A}}\ =\ \sum_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{n}}l_{{}_{i}}\wedge y_{{}_{i}}.

(379)

In this case, the map
\beta
is defined as follows: let
T
be a real
3n
-dimensional torus

\mathbb{T}\otimes\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{T},

(380)

where

\mathbb{T}\ =\ \Gamma\ \oplus\ \Sigma\ \oplus\ \Gamma^{{}^{*}}.

(381)

The torus
T
has natural projection
p
and
q
to the tori
A
and
B
,

\displaystyle T\ \xrightarrow{\ \ \ q\ \ \ }\ B

(382)

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\downarrow

\displaystyle A

Let
P
be a complex line bundle on
T
defined by its first Chern class

c_{{}_{1}}(P)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{n}}x_{{}_{i}}\cdot l_{{}_{i}}

(383)

\text{ch}(P)\ =\ \exp\left(c_{{}_{1}}(P)\right)\ \in\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(T,\mathbb{Q})

(384)

H^{{}^{\bullet}}(B,\mathbb{Q})\ \xrightarrow{\beta}\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(A,\mathbb{Q})

(385)

is given by

\beta(a)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ p_{{}_{*}}\left(\text{ch}(P)\cdot q^{{}^{*}}(a)\right)

(386)

using this for calculating the subspace
\beta\left(\text{Im}(\text{ch})\right)
. To prove the existence of objects in the mirror of
D^{{}^{b}}(B)
that are not Lagrangian submanifolds, we let
n=2
for simplicity and consider a holomorphic line bundle
L
on
A
whose first Chern class is equal to

c_{{}_{1}}\ =\ x_{{}_{1}}\cdot x_{{}_{2}}+y_{{}_{1}}\cdot y_{{}_{2}}.

(387)

Such a holomorphic line bundle exists because
c_{{}_{1}}\in H^{{}^{1,1}}(B)
. The moduli space of such holomorphic line bundles is a homogeneous space over
\text{Pic}^{{}^{0}}(A)
. More explicitly,
L
is of the form

L\ =\ {\cal O}(-D)\ \otimes\ N

(388)

where

N\ \in\ \text{Pic}^{{}^{0}}(A)\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ D\ =\ \Gamma-\{\text{pt}\}\times E-E\times\{\text{pt}\}

(389)

and
\Gamma
is the graph of automorphism of
E
given by multiplication by
i
. A direct calculation shows that

\beta(\text{ch}(L))\ =\ \left(1;y_{{}_{1}}\cdot y_{{}_{2}}-l_{{}_{1}}\cdot l_{{}_{2}};-y_{{}_{1}}\cdot y_{{}_{2}}\cdot l_{{}_{1}}\cdot l_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \in\ H^{{}^{2k}}(A,\mathbb{Q})\ \ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ k\in\mathbb{N}

(390)

is equal to the Chern character of a complex line bundle,
L
, on
B
with

c_{{}_{1}}(M)\ =\ y_{{}_{1}}\cdot y_{{}_{2}}\ -\ l_{{}_{1}}\cdot l_{{}_{2}}.

(391)

The complex line bundle is therefore expected to be an object in the mirror of
D^{{}^{b}}(B)
and the invertible coherent sheaf
L
goes to an object in
D^{{}^{b}}(B)
under Mirror Symmetry.

The subspace
\beta\left(\text{Im}(\text{ch})\right)
consists of elements

(r,c,s)\ \in\ H^{{}^{2k}}(A,\mathbb{Q})\ \ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ k\in\mathbb{N}

(392)

such that

c\cdot\omega_{{}_{A}}\ =\ 0\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ s\ =\ \frac{1}{2}r\omega_{{}_{A}}^{{}^{2}}.

(393)

Likewise, for all
n>2
, we can find elements of
\beta\left(\text{Im}(\text{ch})\right)
which do not belong to the middle cohomology group of
A
, and therefore corresponds to non-Lagrangian objects of the mirror of
D^{{}^{b}}(B)
.

Such phenomenon, in general, does not occur for odd-dimensional Calabi-Yau manifolds that are complete intersections in projective spaces. However, for even-dimensional CYs (e.g. K3-surfaces) or more general odd-dimensional CYs, the situation is similar to that of abelian varieties, namely non-Lagrangian A-branes feature at special points in the moduli space of symplectic structures.

12
HMS for elliptic curves

An elliptic curve is the Riemann surface given by the quotient
\mathbb{C}/\Lambda
and its inherited complex structure, for
\Gamma
a
\mathbb{Z}^{{}^{2}}
-lattice,
5
;
6
.

Choosing

\Lambda\ =\ <1,\tau>,

(394)

we get a bijective relationship between elliptic curves and choices of
\tau
with Image
(\tau)>0
up to modular transformations.

Conventional notation:

E_{{}_{\tau}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \mathbb{C}/<1,\tau>

(395)

12.1
Additive and pre-additive categories

A category is said to be additive if:

1.

Ab-enriched.

2.

Admits finite coproducts.

An Ab-enriched category,
{\cal C}
, is one such that for any pair of objects,
a,b
, the Hom-set, Hom
{}_{{}_{\cal C}}
(a,b), is equipped with the structure of an abelian group, implying for any triple of objects

a,b,c,\ \in\ \text{Obj}({\cal C})

(396)

\circ_{{}_{a,b,c,}}:\ \text{Hom}_{{}_{\cal C}}(a,b)\ \times\ \text{Hom}_{{}_{\cal C}}(b,c)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Hom}_{{}_{\cal C}}(a,c)

(397)

is bilinear.

A pre-additive category is one such that each morphism set
\text{Hom}_{{}_{\cal A}}(x,y)
is endowed with the structure of an abelian group such that any composition (
397
) is bilinear. A functor of pre-additive categories

{\cal F}:\ {\cal A}\ \longrightarrow\ {\cal B}

(398)

is additive if and only if

{\cal F}:\ \text{Hom}(x,y)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Hom}({\cal F}(x),{\cal F}(y))

(399)

is a Hom of abelian groups for any pair
x,y\in
Ob
({\cal A})
.

12.2
Serre duality

In complex analytic geometry, Serre duality is the duality induced by the Hodge star operator on the Dolbeault complex.

This generalises to suitable non-singular projective algebraic varieties over the base rings.

Let
X
be a Hamiltonian manifold of complex dimension

\text{dim}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}(\Sigma)\ =\ n.

(400)

Its Riemann metric induces a Hodge star operator on the Dolbeault complex

\star:\ \Omega^{{}^{p,q}}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ \Omega^{{}^{n-q,n-p}}(X).

(401)

For
X
a compact Hermitian manifold, a bilinear form is defined as follows

\int_{{}_{X}}(-)\wedge\star(-):\ \Omega^{{}^{p,q}}(X)\ \otimes\ \Omega^{{}^{n-p,n-q}}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{C}

(402)

as the integral of differential forms

(\alpha,\beta)\ \mapsto\ \int_{{}_{X}}\alpha\wedge\star\ \beta.

(403)

Proposition
(Serre duality)

The pairing (
402
) induces a non-degenerate sesquilinear form on Dolbeault cohomology

\int_{{}_{X}}(-)\wedge\star(-):\ H^{{}^{p,q}}(X)\ \otimes\ H^{{}^{n-p,n-q}}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{C}.

(404)

Relation to Poincare’ duality follows from the Hodge Decomposition Theorem

H^{{}^{k}}(X,\mathbb{C})\ \simeq\ \bigoplus_{{}_{p+q=k}}H^{{}^{p,q}}(X).

(405)

In practice, if

\text{dim}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}(\Sigma)\ =\ n.

(406)

Serre duality induces an isomorphism in ordinary cohomology of the form

H^{{}^{k}}(X,\mathbb{C})\ \xrightarrow{\simeq}\ H^{{}^{2n-k}}(X,\mathbb{C}).

(407)

12.3
CY structure

Let
\tilde{M}
be a CY manifold with its unique Ricci-flat K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler metric and K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler form.

When
\tilde{M}
is a torus with flat metric

ds^{{}^{2}}\ =\ A\left(\text{d}x^{{}^{2}}+\text{d}y^{{}^{2}}\right)

(408)

such that

\text{Vol}\left(\tilde{M}\right)\ =\ A.

(409)

Specifying an element

b\ \in\ H^{{}^{2}}\left(\tilde{E};\mathbb{R}\right)/H^{{}^{2}}\left(\tilde{E};\mathbb{Z}\right)

(410)

and a complexified K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler form

\omega\ \overset{def.}{=}\ b+iK.

(411)

12.4
Generalised Theta functions

\theta
-functions are particularly useful as a bases for the B-side homsets. They descend to holomorphic sections of line bundles on
E_{{}_{\tau}}
, and provide a bases for our B-side homsets.

The generalised
\theta
-function reads as follows

\Theta[a,z_{{}_{o}}](\tau,z)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}}e^{{}^{\pi i[(m+a)^{{}^{2}}\tau+2(m+a)(z+z_{{}_{o}})]}}.

(412)

Thinking of
\theta
functions as as line bundle homomorphisms, we can define compositions on the B-side by making use of the following addition formula:

\displaystyle\Theta\left[\frac{a}{n_{{}_{1}}},0\right]\left(n_{{}_{1}}\tau,z_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \Theta\left[\frac{b}{n_{{}_{2}}},0\right]\left(n_{{}_{2}}\tau,z_{{}_{2}}\right)\ =

(413)

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{{}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}/k\mathbb{Z}}}\Theta\left[\frac{c_{{}_{j}}}{k},0\right]\left(k\tau,z_{{}_{1}}+z_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \Theta\left[\frac{n_{{}_{2}}c_{{}_{j}}-kb}{n_{{}_{1}}n_{{}_{2}}k},0\right]\left(n_{{}_{1}}n_{{}_{2}}k\tau,n_{{}_{2}}z_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{1}}z_{{}_{2}}\right),

for
a,b\in\mathbb{Q},n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{{}_{\geq 1}},k=n_{{}_{1}}+n_{{}_{2}},c_{{}_{j}}=jn_{{}_{1}}+a+b
.

12.5
B-fields

12.6
The Fukaya category

Take
\tau=iA\ \in\ i\mathbb{R}
; consider three Lagrangian submanifolds,
L_{{}_{0}},L_{{}_{1}},,L_{{}_{2}}
, whose lifts to
\mathbb{R}^{{}^{2}}
can be chosen such that they all crss the origin, with 0,1,2,-slope, respectively.

Assuming trivial connection, we can suppress the choice of grading altogether.

Requiring

\mathbb{C}^{{}^{\left|{\mathfrak{L}}_{{}_{i}}\cap{\mathfrak{L}}_{{}_{j}}\right|}}\ =\ \text{Hom}\left({\mathfrak{L}}_{{}_{i}},{\mathfrak{L}}_{{}_{j}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Hom}\left({\cal L}_{{}_{i}},{\cal L}_{{}_{j}}\right)\ =\ H^{{}^{0}}\left({\cal L}_{{}_{j}}\otimes{\cal L}_{{}_{i}}^{{}^{*}}\right),

(414)

the following relation follow

\mu_{{}_{2}}\left(e_{{}_{1}},e_{{}_{1}}\right)\ =\ C\left(e_{{}_{1}},e_{{}_{1}},e_{{}_{1}}\right)\cdot e_{{}_{1}}\ +\ C\left(e_{{}_{1}},e_{{}_{1}},e_{{}_{2}}\right)\cdot e_{{}_{2}},

(415)

where

C\left(e_{{}_{1}},e_{{}_{1}},e_{{}_{1}}\right)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\ e^{{}^{-2\pi An^{{}^{2}}}}\ \equiv\ \Theta[0,0](2iA,0)\ =\ \Theta_{{}_{2\tau}}(0)

(416)

C\left(e_{{}_{1}},e_{{}_{1}},e_{{}_{2}}\right)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\ e^{{}^{-2\pi A\left(n+1/2\right)^{{}^{2}}}}\ \equiv\ \Theta\left[\frac{1}{2},0\right](\tau,0).

(417)

Figure 10:

We can therefore use the generalised theta-function to express morphisms on the A-side, such as

\mu_{{}_{2}}\left(e_{{}_{1}}\otimes e_{{}_{2}}\right)\ =\ \Theta_{{}_{2\tau}}(0)\cdot\Theta_{{}_{2\tau}}(2z)+\Theta\left[\frac{1}{2},0\right](2\tau,0)\cdot\Theta\left[\frac{1}{2},0\right](2\tau,2z).

(418)

In essence, given a triple of Lagrangians, we can succinclty relate the morphisms between them in terms of generalised theta-functions as follows

\displaystyle\text{Hom}\left(\mathfrak{L}_{{}_{0}},\mathfrak{L}_{{}_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\ \text{Hom}\left({\cal L}_{{}_{0}},{\cal L}_{{}_{1}}\right)

(419)

\displaystyle e_{{}_{1}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\ \ \ \ \ \Theta_{{}_{\tau}}(z)

\displaystyle\text{Hom}\left(\mathfrak{L}_{{}_{1}},\mathfrak{L}_{{}_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\ \text{Hom}\left({\cal L}_{{}_{1}},{\cal L}_{{}_{2}}\right)

(420)

\displaystyle e_{{}_{1}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\ \ \ \ \ \Theta_{{}_{\tau}}(z)

\displaystyle\text{Hom}\left(\mathfrak{L}_{{}_{0}},\mathfrak{L}_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Hom}\left({\cal L}_{{}_{0}},{\cal L}_{{}_{2}}\right)

(421)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&e_{{}_{1}}\ \ \ \mapsto\ \ \ \ \ \Theta[0,0](2\tau,2z)\\
&\\
&e_{{}_{2}}\ \ \ \mapsto\ \ \ \ \ \Theta\left[\frac{1}{2},0\right](2\tau,2z)\\
\end{cases}

12.6.1
The shifted case

Shifting
\mathfrak{L}_{{}_{2}}
, the relations derived in the previous subsection become

\mu_{{}_{2}}\left(e_{{}_{1}}\otimes e_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{\prime}}\right)\ =\ C\left(e_{{}_{1}},e_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{\prime}},p_{{}_{1}}\right)\cdot p_{{}_{1}}\ +\ C\left(e_{{}_{1}},e_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{\prime}},p_{{}_{2}}\right)\cdot p_{{}_{2}},

(422)

where

C\left(e_{{}_{1}},e_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{\prime}},p_{{}_{1}}\right)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\ e^{{}^{-2\pi A\left(n+x_{{}_{o}}\right)^{{}^{2}}}}\ \equiv\ \Theta[x_{{}_{o}},0](2\tau,0)

(423)

C\left(e_{{}_{1}},e_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{\prime}},p_{{}_{2}}\right)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\ e^{{}^{-2\pi A\left(n+1/2-x_{{}_{o}}\right)^{{}^{2}}}}\ =\ \sum_{{}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\ e^{{}^{-2\pi A\left(n+x_{{}_{o}}+1/2\right)^{{}^{2}}}}\ \equiv\ \Theta\left[\frac{1}{2}+x_{{}_{o}},0\right](2\tau,0).

(424)

\displaystyle\text{Hom}\left(\mathfrak{L}_{{}_{0}},\mathfrak{L}_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Hom}\left({\cal L}_{{}_{0}},{\cal L}_{{}_{2}}\right)

(425)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&p_{{}_{1}}\ \ \ \mapsto\ \ \ \ \ \Theta[0,0](2\tau,2z)\\
&\\
&p_{{}_{2}}\ \ \ \mapsto\ \ \ \ \ \Theta\left[\frac{1}{2},0\right](2\tau,2z)\\
\end{cases}

Figure 11:

12.6.2
Some explicit steps

Objects are graded, oriented, closed geodesics of
E^{{}^{\rho}}
equipped with a flat complex vector bundle of monodromy with unit eigenvalues.

\text{Hom}\left({\cal L}_{{}_{0}},{\cal L}_{{}_{1}}\right)\ =\ \text{span}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}\left\{\Theta_{{}_{\tau}}(z)\right\}

(426)

\text{Hom}\left({\cal L}_{{}_{1}},{\cal L}_{{}_{2}}\right)\ =\ H^{0}\left(t^{{}^{*}}_{{}_{2x_{{}_{o}}\tau}}{\cal L}_{{}_{\varphi_{{}_{o}}}}\right)\ =\ \text{span}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}\left\{t^{{}^{*}}_{{}_{2x_{{}_{o}}\tau}}\Theta_{{}_{\tau}}(z)\right\}.

(427)

The Lagrangian composition

{\cal L}_{{}_{o}}\ \longrightarrow\ {\cal L}_{{}_{1}}\ \longrightarrow\ {\cal L}_{{}_{2}}

(428)

is therefore determined by the product

\displaystyle\Theta_{{}_{\tau}}(z)\ \cdot\ \Theta_{{}_{\tau}}(z+2x_{{}_{o}}\tau)\ =

(429)

\displaystyle=\ \Theta_{{}_{2\tau}}(2x_{{}_{o}}\tau)\ \Theta_{{}_{2\tau}}(2z+2x_{{}_{o}}\tau)\ +\ \Theta\left[\frac{1}{2},0\right](2\tau,2x_{{}_{o}}\tau)\ \Theta\left[\frac{1}{2},0\right](2\tau,2z+2x_{{}_{o}}\tau)\

\displaystyle=\ e^{{}^{-2\pi ix_{{}_{o}}^{{}^{2}}\tau}}\left[\Theta\left[x_{{}_{o}},0\right](2\tau,2z+2x_{{}_{o}}\tau)\ \Theta\left[0,2x_{{}_{o}}\tau\right](2\tau,2z)\ +\right.

\displaystyle+\ \left.\Theta\left[x_{{}_{o}}+\frac{1}{2},0\right](2\tau,0)\ \Theta\left[\frac{1}{2},2x_{{}_{o}}\tau\right](2\tau,2z)\right].

12.6.3
The Fukaya-Kontsevich category

Given two Lagrangians and a morphism in between them,

(L,\alpha,M)\ \longrightarrow\ \left(L^{{}^{\prime}},\alpha^{{}^{\prime}},M^{{}^{\prime}}\right)

(430)

the morphisms in the graded Fukaya category read as follows

\text{Hom}_{{}_{{\text{Fuk}}^{{}^{0}}\left(E^{{}^{\tau}}\right)}}\left({\cal L},{\cal L}^{{}^{\prime}}\right)\ =\ \begin{cases}&\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{if}\ \ \alpha^{{}^{\prime}}-\alpha\ \notin\ [0,1)\\
&\\
&\underset{p\in L\cap L^{{}^{\prime}}}{\bigoplus}\text{Hom}\left(M_{{}_{p}},M_{{}_{p}}^{{}^{\prime}}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{if}\ \ \alpha^{{}^{\prime}}-\alpha\ \in\ [0,1).\\
\end{cases}

(431)

Lemma

For
{\cal L}_{{}_{1}},{\cal L}_{{}_{2}}
objects in
\text{Fuk}^{{}^{0}}\left(E^{{}^{\tau}}\right)
, there exists a canonical isomorphism

\text{Hom}\left({\cal L}_{{}_{1}},{\cal L}_{{}_{2}}[1]\right)\ \simeq\ \text{Hom}\left({\cal L}_{{}_{2}},{\cal L}_{{}_{1}}\right)^{{}^{*}}.

(432)

\text{Fuk}^{{}^{0}}\left(E^{{}^{\tau}}\right)
is a pre-additive category, however,
D^{{}^{b}}\left(E_{{}_{\tau}}\right)
is additive.

To recover a categorical equivalence with the B-side, we need to introduce the Fukaya-Kontsevich category,
{\cal FK}^{{}^{0}}\left(E^{{}^{\tau}}\right)
, defined as the 0
{}^{{}^{th}}
-cohomology of the Fukaya category on the elliptic curve

{\cal FK}^{{}^{0}}\left(E^{{}^{\tau}}\right)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ H^{{}^{0}}\left(\text{Fuk}\left(E^{{}^{\tau}}\right)\right).

(433)

Unlike previous examples we have discussed so far, the elliptic curve provides a first example where the
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-structure can be relaxed for the correspondence to hold.
Notice that the HMS correspondence might have been recovered in an alternative way, namely by equipping
D^{{}^{b}}\left(E_{{}_{\tau}}\right)
with an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-structure. Albeit such direction has been explored by other authors in the literature, it is still far from complete due to their analysis being restricted to the transversally-intersecting cases alone.

For the case of the torus,
M=E_{{}_{\tau}}
, is an elliptic curve with modular parameter
\tau
, and
\tilde{M}=\tilde{E}^{{}^{\rho}}
a torus with complexified K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler parameter

\rho\ =\ iA+b,

(434)

where
A
denotes the area, and
b
defines a class in

H^{{}^{2}}\left(\tilde{M};\mathbb{R}\right)\ /\ H^{{}^{2}}\left(\tilde{M};\mathbb{Z}\right).

(435)

The mirror map therefore reads

\rho\ \longleftrightarrow\ \tau.

(436)

Generalising this result to higher-dimensional CY manifolds is still an open question, the reason being that the Fukaya category composition depends on the K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler form, i.e. on the unique CY metric.

The exact form on this metric is yet unkown. Abelian varieties are simplest extension of the case of the elliptic curve.

12.7
The mirror dual

Local sections of a vector bundle are an example of a coherent sheaf.

Lemma

Let
{\cal F},{\cal G}
be coherent sheaves on
E_{{}_{\tau}}
; then there exists an isomorphism

\text{Hom}({\cal F},{\cal G})\ \simeq\ \text{Ext}^{{}^{1}}({\cal G},{\cal F})^{{}^{*}}.

(437)

For any pair objects of an abelian category,
A,B
,

\text{Hom}_{{}_{\cal DA}}(A[m],B[n])\ \simeq\ \text{Hom}_{{}_{\cal DA}}(A,B[m-n])\ \simeq\ \text{Ext}^{{}^{m-n}}(A,B)

(438)

\text{Hom}_{{}_{D^{{}^{b}}(E_{{}_{\tau}})}}(A,B[1])\ =\ \text{Ext}^{{}^{1}}(A,B)\ =\ \text{Hom}(B,A)^{{}^{*}}

(439)

\text{Hom}_{{}_{D^{{}^{b}}(E_{{}_{\tau}})}}(A,B)\ =\ \text{Ext}^{{}^{0}}(A,B)\ =\ \text{Hom}(A,B).

(440)

The last equality in (
439
) and (
440
) follows from the following

Lemma

Let
A_{{}_{1}},A_{{}_{2}}
be two coherent sheaves on the elliptic curve,
E_{{}_{\tau}}
, then

\text{Ext}^{{}^{1}}\left(A_{{}_{2}},A_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \simeq\ \text{Hom}\left(A_{{}_{1}},A_{{}_{2}}\right)^{{}^{*}}.

(441)

Proof

Let
A_{{}_{2}}
be an arbitrary coherent sheaf.
E_{{}_{\tau}}
being smooth projective implies there exists a short-exact sequence

0\ \longrightarrow\ F^{{}^{\prime}}\ \longrightarrow\ A_{{}_{2}}\ \longrightarrow\ 0

(442)

with locally-free sheaves
F
and
F^{{}^{\prime}}
. By applying the covariant functors Ext
{}^{{}^{1}}(A_{{}_{1}},.)
and Hom
(.,A_{{}_{1}})^{{}^{*}}
we obtain the following diagram

\displaystyle\text{Ext}^{{}^{1}}\left(A_{{}_{1}},F^{{}^{\prime}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Ext}^{{}^{1}}\left(A_{{}_{1}},F\right)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Ext}^{{}^{1}}\left(A_{{}_{1}},A_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ 0

(443)

\displaystyle\simeq\ \ \downarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \simeq\ \ \downarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \downarrow

\displaystyle\text{Hom}\left(F^{{}^{\prime}},A_{{}_{1}}\right)^{{}^{*}}\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Hom}\left(F,A_{{}_{1}}\right)^{{}^{*}}\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Hom}\left(A_{{}_{2}},A_{{}_{1}}\right)^{{}^{*}}\ \longrightarrow\ 0

The Lemma follows from commutativity of the diagram (
443
), in turn, due to Serre duality.

Lemma

\text{Ext}^{{}^{k}}(A,B)\ =\ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{if}\ \ k\neq 0,1

(444)

for
A,B
coherent sheaves on a Riemann surface.

In general, for a complex
n
-dimensional manifold, the Ext
{}^{{}^{k}}
-functor is zero for
k>n
.

12.8
The equivalence

We now turn to outlining the proof of the main theorem in [Polishchuk, Zaslow] and [Kreussler], namely that

\boxed{\ \ \ \Phi_{{}_{\tau}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}\left(E_{{}_{\tau}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ {\cal FK}^{{}^{0}}\left(E^{{}^{\tau}}\right)\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bigg]\ \ }

(445)

is an equivalence of additive categories compatible with the shift functor.

Proof

Replacing
D^{{}^{b}}\left(E_{{}_{\tau}}\right)
by the equivalent subcategory whose objects are finite direct sums of shifted indecomposable coherent sheaves on
E_{{}_{\tau}}
.

\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}
can be defined on objects by knowing how it acts on
A=A[0]
(an indecomposable coherent sheaf on
E_{{}_{\tau}}
). Defining
A
as a tensor product of line bundles with unipotent bundle on
E_{{}_{\tau}}
. By means of Theorem 2.6, they prove that
\Phi_{{}_{r\tau}}(A)
can be, in turn, extended to all indecomposable vector bundles. The definition of
\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}(A)
thereby involves elements common to the SYZ description, with

A\ =\ {\cal L}(\varphi)\ \otimes\ F\left(V,\exp(N)\right)

(446)

with

\varphi\ =\ t^{{}^{*}}_{{}_{a\tau+b}}\varphi_{{}_{o}}\cdot\varphi_{{}_{o}}^{{}^{n-1}}

(447)

and
V
a finite-dimensional complex vector space, and
N\in\text{End}(V)
a cyclic nilpotent endomorphism,

\Phi(A)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left(L,\alpha,M\right)

(448)

with

L\ \equiv\ \left(a+t,(n-1)a+nt\right).

(449)

\alpha
is the unique real number such that

-\frac{1}{2}\ \leq\ \alpha\ \leq\ \frac{1}{2}\ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \exp(i\pi\alpha)\ =\ \frac{1+in}{\sqrt{1+n^{{}^{2}}\ }},

(450)

and

M\ =\ \exp\left(-2\pi\ i\ b\mathbf{1}_{{}_{V}}+N\right).

(451)

Note that

t^{{}^{\prime*}}\Phi(A)\ =\ \Phi\left(t^{{}^{*}}A\right)

(452)

if
A
is a vector bundle on
E_{{}_{\tau}}
,

t:\ E_{{}_{\tau}}\ \longrightarrow\ E_{{}_{\tau}}

(453)

denotes the translation by
\frac{m}{n}\tau
,

t(z)\ =\ z+\frac{m}{n}\tau,

(454)

with

t^{{}^{\prime}}:\ E^{{}^{\tau}}\ \longrightarrow\ E^{{}^{\tau}}

(455)

the corresponding translation on the symplectic side,

t^{{}^{\prime}}(x,y)\ =\ \left(x-\frac{m}{n},y\right).

(456)

If

\pi_{{}_{r}}:\ E_{{}_{r\tau}}\ \longrightarrow\ E_{{}_{\tau}}

(457)

is an isogeny, and
A
is a vector bundle on
E_{{}_{r\tau}}
of the form considered above, and

p_{{}_{r}}:\ E^{{}^{r\tau}}\ \longrightarrow\ E^{{}^{\tau}}

(458)

is the corresponding morphism on the symplectic side, one can define

\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\left(\pi_{{}_{r^{{}^{*}}}}A\right)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ p_{{}_{r^{{}^{*}}}}\Phi_{{}_{r\tau}}(A).

(459)

More explicitly, this means that, for

A\ =\ {\cal L}(\varphi)\ \otimes\ F(V,\exp(N)),

(460)

\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\left(\pi_{{}_{r^{{}^{*}}}}A\right)\ =\ (L,\alpha,M),

(461)

where
L
isgiven by the line with
x
-incerpt
\frac{ra}{n}
and slope
\frac{n}{r}
,
-\frac{1}{2}<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2}
is its corresponding log, and

M\ =\ \exp\left(-2\pi\ i\ b\mathbf{1}_{{}_{V}}+N\right)

(462)

since
p_{{}_{r}}
defines an isomorphism between the underlying Lagrangian submanifolds.

Let

A\ =\ S(a\tau+b,V,N)

(463)

be a torsion sheaf on
E_{{}_{\tau}}
supported on
x=a\tau+b
. Then,

\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}(A)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left(L,\frac{1}{2},\exp\left(2\pi\ ib\mathbf{1}_{{}_{V}}+N\right)\right),

(464)

where
L
is defined by
(-a,t)
.

To get a functor,
\Phi
, we need to define its effect on morphisms. Implementing compatibility with finite direct sums and shifts,

\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}:\ \text{Hom}_{{}_{D^{{}^{b}}(E_{{}_{\tau}})}}\left(A_{{}_{1}},A_{{}_{2}}[n]\right)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Hom}_{{}_{{\cal FK}^{{}^{0}}(E^{{}^{\tau}})}}\left(\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\left(A_{{}_{1}}\right),\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\left(A_{{}_{2}}\right)[n]\right).

(465)

Both sides vanish if
n\notin\{0,1\}
, implying we only need to consider the
n=0,1
case. From Serre duality, and the fact that

\text{Hom}_{{}_{D^{{}^{b}}(E_{{}_{\tau}})}}\left(A_{{}_{1}},A_{{}_{2}}[1]\right)\ =\ \text{Ext}^{{}^{1}}\left(A_{{}_{1}},A_{{}_{2}}\right),

(466)

\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\forall n
follows from its definition for
n=0
. This can be treated for, both the case in which the Lagrangian submanifolds are equivalent of different.

If
L_{{}_{1}}\neq L_{{}_{2}}
, for [PZ]

A_{{}_{i}}\ =\ {\cal L}\left(\varphi_{{}_{i}})\otimes F(V_{{}_{i}},N_{{}_{i}}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \Phi_{{}_{\tau}}(A_{{}_{i}})\ =\ \left(L_{{}_{i}},\alpha_{{}_{i}},M_{{}_{i}}\right)

(467)

\varphi_{{}_{i}}\ =\ t_{{}_{x_{{}_{i}}}}^{{}^{*}}\ \varphi_{{}_{o}}\cdot\varphi_{{}_{o}}^{{}^{n_{{}_{i}}-1}}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ x_{{}_{i}}\ =\ a_{{}_{i}}\tau+b_{{}_{i}}

(468)

H^{{}^{0}}({\cal L}(\varphi)))\otimes V\ \longrightarrow\ H^{{}^{0}}({\cal L}(\varphi)\otimes F(V,\exp(N))).

(469)

Assuming
n_{{}_{1}}<n_{{}_{2}}
,

\text{Hom}\left(A_{{}_{1}},A_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \simeq\ H^{{}^{0}}\left({\cal L}\left(\varphi_{{}_{2}}\varphi_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{-1}}\right)\right)\ \otimes\ \text{Hom}\left(V_{{}_{1}},V_{{}_{2}}\right)

(470)

The basis for this vector space is formed by the theta functions

t^{{}^{*}}_{{}_{x_{{}_{12}}}}\Theta\left[\frac{k}{n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}}},0\right]\left((n_{{}_{2}}-n_{{}_{1}})\tau,(n_{{}_{2}}-n_{{}_{1}})z\right).

(471)

The isomorphism

\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}:\ \text{Hom}_{{}_{D^{{}^{b}}\left(E_{{}_{\tau}}\right)}}\left(A_{{}_{1}},A_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Hom}_{{}_{{\cal FK}^{{}^{0}}\left(E^{{}^{\tau}}\right)}}\left(\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\left(A_{{}_{1}}\right),\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\left(A_{{}_{2}}\right)\right)

(472)

is obtained by specifying a bijection between these theta functions and the intersection points.

If
L_{{}_{1}}=L_{{}_{2}}
, instead,

\text{Hom}_{{}_{Fuk^{{}^{o}}}}\left(\left(L_{{}_{1}},\alpha_{{}_{1}},M_{{}_{1}}\right),\left(L_{{}_{2}},\alpha_{{}_{2}},M_{{}_{2}}\right)\right)\ \neq\ 0,

(473)

the spaces of morphisms are trivial iff
\varphi_{{}_{1}}\neq\varphi_{{}_{2}}
. If
\varphi_{{}_{1}}\equiv\varphi_{{}_{2}}
, the operators in
M_{{}_{i}}
have the same eigenvalue,
\exp(-2\pi ib_{{}_{1}})
, implying

H^{{}^{0}}\left(L_{{}_{1}},\text{Hom}(M_{{}_{1}},M_{{}_{2}})\right)\ =\ \left\{f:V_{{}_{1}}\longrightarrow V_{{}_{2}}|\exp(N_{{}_{1}})\ \circ\ f\ =\ f\ \circ\ \exp(N_{{}_{2}})\right\}.

(474)

The next case to be addressed is that in which
\Phi
is a homomorphism between arbitrary vector bundles on
E_{{}_{\tau}}
. Given two positive integers,
r_{{}_{1}},r_{{}_{2}}
, and

{\cal E}_{{}_{i}}\ =\ {\cal L}\left(\varphi_{{}_{i}}\right)\ \otimes\ F\left(V_{{}_{i}},\exp\left(N_{{}_{i}}\right)\right)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ E_{{}_{r_{{}_{i}}\tau}}

(475)

E\ \overset{def.}{=}\ E_{{}_{r_{{}_{1}}\tau}}\ \underset{E_{{}_{\tau}}}{\times}\ E_{{}_{r_{{}_{2}}\tau}}

(476)

and denote the projections by

\tilde{\pi}_{{}_{r_{{}_{i}}}}:\ E\ \longrightarrow\ E_{{}_{r_{{}_{i}}\tau}}.

(477)

The curve
E
is a disjoint union of

d\ =\ \text{gcd}\left(r_{{}_{1}},r_{{}_{2}}\right)

(478)

elliptic curves and the restrictions
\tilde{\pi}_{{}_{r_{{}_{i}}v}}
of the projections
\tilde{\pi}_{{}_{r_{{}_{i}}}}
onto the components that are isogenies composed with translations.

Specifically,

E\ =\ E_{{}_{\tau}}\ \times\ \mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z},

(479)

with

r\ \overset{def}{=}\ \frac{r_{{}_{1}}r_{{}_{2}}}{d}

(480)

and

\pi_{{}_{r_{{}_{i}},v}}:\ E_{{}_{r\tau}}\ \times\{\nu\}\ \longrightarrow\ E_{{}_{r_{{}_{i}}\nu}}

(481)

is the composition of isogenies

E_{{}_{r\tau}}\ \longrightarrow\ E_{{}_{r_{{}_{i}}\tau}}

(482)

defined by

\Gamma_{{}_{r_{{}_{i}}\tau}}\ \subset\ \Gamma_{{}_{r\tau}}

(483)

translating by
\nu\tau
on
E_{{}_{r_{{}_{1}}\tau}}
and the identity on
E_{{}_{r_{{}_{2}}\tau}}
.

Using the translation by
s_{{}_{i}}\tau
on
E_{{}_{r_{{}_{i}}\tau}}
for any pair
\left(s_{{}_{1}},s_{{}_{2}}\right)
with

s_{{}_{1}}-s_{{}_{2}}\ \equiv\ \nu\ \text{mod}\ d.

(484)

Two such choices differ by a translation on
E_{{}_{r\tau}}
by an integer multiple of
\tau
. Making use of the flat base change theorem, and the adjointness properties of
\pi_{{}_{*}}
and
\pi^{{}^{*}}
, obtain a canonical isomorphism

\text{Hom}\left(\pi_{{}_{r_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{*}}}}{\cal E}_{{}_{1}},\pi_{{}_{r_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{*}}}}{\cal E}_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \simeq\ \text{Hom}\left(\tilde{\pi}_{{}_{r_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{*}}}}{\cal E}_{{}_{1}},\tilde{\pi}_{{}_{r_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{*}}}}{\cal E}_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \simeq\ \bigoplus_{{}_{\nu=1}}\text{Hom}\left(\pi_{{}_{r_{{}_{1}}\nu}}^{{}^{*}}{\cal E}_{{}_{1}},\pi_{{}_{r_{{}_{2}}\nu}}^{{}^{*}}{\cal E}_{{}_{2}}\right).

(485)

On the symplectic side, we can define
\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}
by the following commutative diagram

\displaystyle\text{Hom}\left(\pi_{{}_{r_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{*}}}}{\cal E}_{{}_{1}},\pi_{{}_{r_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{*}}}}{\cal E}_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \xrightarrow{\sim}\ \ \ \ \ \ \bigoplus\text{Hom}\left(\pi_{{}_{r_{{}_{1}}\nu}}^{{}^{*}}{\cal E}_{{}_{1}},\pi_{{}_{r_{{}_{2}}\nu}}^{{}^{*}}{\cal E}_{{}_{2}}\right)

(486)

\displaystyle\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\ \downarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \downarrow\oplus\ \Phi_{{}_{\tau}}

\displaystyle\text{Hom}\left(\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\left(\pi_{{}_{r_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{*}}}}{\cal E}_{{}_{1}}\right),\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\left(\pi_{{}_{r_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{*}}}}{\cal E}_{{}_{2}}\right)\right)\ \xrightarrow{\sim}\ \bigoplus\text{Hom}\left(\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\left(\pi_{{}_{r_{{}_{1}}\nu}}^{{}^{*}}{\cal E}_{{}_{1}}\right),\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\left(\pi_{{}_{r_{{}_{2}}\nu}}^{{}^{*}}{\cal E}_{{}_{2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\ \ \ \downarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \downarrow\ \ \ \ \simeq

\displaystyle\text{Hom}\left(p_{{}_{r_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{*}}}}\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\left({\cal E}_{{}_{1}}\right),p_{{}_{r_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{*}}}}\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\left({\cal E}_{{}_{2}}\right)\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \xrightarrow{\sim}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \bigoplus\text{Hom}\left(\tilde{p}_{{}_{r_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{*}}}}\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\left({\cal E}_{{}_{1}}\right),\tilde{p}_{{}_{r_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{*}}}}\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\left({\cal E}_{{}_{2}}\right)\right)

where

\phi_{{}_{r\tau}}\left(\pi^{{}^{*}}({\cal E})\right)\ \simeq\ p^{{}^{*}}\left(\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}({\cal E})\right)

(487)

If

{\cal E}\ \simeq\ {\cal L}(\varphi)\ \otimes\ F\left(V,\exp(N)\right),

(488)

\text{Hom}\left(A_{{}_{1}},A_{{}_{2}}\right)\ =\ \text{Ext}^{{}^{1}}\left(A_{{}_{1}},A_{{}_{2}}\right)\ =\ 0

(489)

if
A_{{}_{1}}
is a torsion sheaf and
A_{{}_{2}}
is locally free.

The most important part of the proof is showing commutativity of the following diagram

\displaystyle\text{Hom}\left(A_{{}_{1}},A_{{}_{2}}[k]\right)\ \otimes\ \text{Hom}\left(A_{{}_{2}}[k],A_{{}_{3}}[l]\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \longrightarrow\ \ \ \ \text{Hom}\left(A_{{}_{1}},A_{{}_{3}}[l]\right)

(490)

\displaystyle\downarrow\ \ \ \Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\otimes\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \downarrow\ \ \ \Phi_{{}_{\tau}}\ \ \ \ \ \

\displaystyle\text{Hom}\left(\Phi\left(A_{{}_{1}}\right),\Phi\left(A_{{}_{2}}\right)[k]\right)\ \otimes\ \text{Hom}\left(\Phi\left(A_{{}_{2}}\right)[k],\Phi\left(A_{{}_{3}}\right)[l]\right)\ \ \ \ \longrightarrow\ \ \ \ \text{Hom}\left(\Phi\left(A_{{}_{1}}\right),\left(A_{{}_{3}}\right)[l]\right)

Assuming
k=l=0
,

A_{{}_{\nu}}\ =\ {\cal L}\left(\varphi_{{}_{\nu}}\right)\ \oplus\ F\left(V_{{}_{\nu}},\exp\left(N_{{}_{\nu}}\right)\right),\ \ \ \ \ \forall\ \nu.

(491)

1.

If
L_{{}_{1}}\ \neq\ L_{{}_{2}}\ \neq\ L_{{}_{3}}\ \neq\ L_{{}_{1}}
, commutativity of (
490
) follows from an addition formula for theta functions.

2.

If
L_{{}_{1}}\ =\ L_{{}_{2}}\ \neq\ L_{{}_{3}}
, assuming
\varphi_{{}_{1}}=\varphi=2
(otherwise Hom
\left(A_{{}_{1}},A_{{}_{2}}\right)=0
)

\text{Hom}\left(A_{{}_{1}},A_{{}_{2}}\right)\ =\ \text{Hom}\left(F\left(V_{{}_{1}},\exp\left(N_{{}_{1}}\right)\right),F\left(V_{{}_{2}},\exp\left(N_{{}_{2}}\right)\right)\right)\ =\ \left\{f\in\text{Hom}\left(V_{{}_{1}},V_{{}_{2}}\right)|f\circ N_{{}_{1}}\ =\ N_{{}_{2}}\circ f\right\}.

(492)

Under
\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}
, it is identified with

\text{Hom}\left(\Phi\left(A_{{}_{1}}\right),\Phi\left(A_{{}_{2}}\right)\right)\ =\ H^{{}^{0}}\left(L_{{}_{1}},\text{Hom}\left(M_{{}_{1}},M_{{}_{2}}\right)\right)\ =\ \left\{f\in\text{Hom}\left(V_{{}_{1}},V_{{}_{2}}\right)|f\circ N_{{}_{1}}\ =\ N_{{}_{2}}\circ f\right\}.

(493)

Likewise if
L_{{}_{1}}\ \neq\ L_{{}_{2}}\ =\ L_{{}_{3}}
.

3.

If
L_{{}_{1}}\ =\ L_{{}_{2}}\ =\ L_{{}_{3}}
, commutativity of
\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}
follows from Lemma 2.5.

Altogether, this completes the proof that
\Phi
is effectively a functor. To complete the proof of the Theorem, it is enough to show that any indecomposable object in
{\cal FK}^{{}^{0}}\left(E^{{}^{\tau}}\right)
is isomorphic to an object of the form
\Phi_{{}_{\tau}}(A)
.

Indecomposability of an object of the kind

(L,\alpha,M)

(494)

means the local system
M
is indecomposable. Assuming the eigenvalues of the monodromy are of modulus 1, we can describe

M\ =\ \exp\left(-2\pi\ ib+N\right)\ \in\ \text{GL}(V)

(495)

with
N
a cyclic nilpotent endomorphism,
b\in\mathbb{R}
.

13
SYZ

13.1
SYZ MS for Toric Varieties

Conjecture
[SYZ]

Given two CY mirrors,
X,\hat{X}
,
7
;
8
:

1.

Both,
X,\hat{X}
, admit special Lagrangian torus fibrations with sections

\rho:\ X\ \longrightarrow\ B\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \hat{\rho}:\ \hat{X}\ \longrightarrow\ B

(496)

over the same base,
B
, which are fiberwise dual to each other, namely

\rho^{{}^{-1}}(b)\ \subset\ X\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \hat{\rho}^{{}^{-1}}(b)\ \subset\ \hat{X}

(497)

over the same point
b\in B
are dual tori.

2.

There is a Fourier-Mukai transform exchanging symplecto-geometric data on
X
with complex geometric data on
\hat{X}
, and viceversa.

Together with Kontsevich’s HMS, it provides the other major mathematical approach for studying Mirror Symmetry. SYZ is a geometric construction implementing Mirror Symmetry. Importantly, quantum corrections need to be added.

13.1.1
Semi-flat SYZ

This constitutes an example in which special Lagrangian fibrations are non-singular.

SYZ explains MS in an exhaustive manner. However, this description only remains valid in the large complex structure/large volume limit, in which case quantum corrections are suppressed. Away from such limits, singular Lagrangian torus fibrations inevitably emerge.

Take

\rho:\ X\ \longrightarrow\ B

(498)

without singular fibers. This naturally defines two
tropical affine structures
on the base
B
, namely the symplectic and complex affine structures.

Let

L_{{}_{b}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \rho^{{}^{-1}}(b)

(499)

be a fiber of
\rho
. Then,

v\ \in\ T_{{}_{b}}B

(500)

can be lifted to a vector field normal to the fiber
L_{{}_{b}}
, and this determines teh following differential forms

\alpha\ \equiv\ -\iota_{{}_{V}}\omega\ \in\ \Omega^{{}^{1}}(L_{{}_{b}};\mathbb{R})\ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \beta\ \equiv\ \iota_{{}_{V}}\text{Im}\Omega\ \in\ \Omega^{{}^{n-1}}(L_{{}_{b}};\mathbb{R}),

(501)

where
\omega
defines the K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler form, whereas
\Omega
denotes the holomorphic volume form on the CY,
X
.

The SYZ conjecture explains MS beautifully in the semi-flat case. However, this description only remains valid in th elarge complex structure/large volume limit, whereas all quantum corrections are being suppressed. Away from these limits, special Lagrangian torus fibrations will have singular fibers, and the SYZ mirror construction must be modified by instanton corrections, which, as suggested again by SYZ should come from holomorphic disks bounded by the Lagrangian torus fibers.

13.1.2
LG models as mirrors

Prediction:
genus-0 closed GW invariants of
X
are encoded in the deformation or unfolding of
{\cal W}

{\cal Q}H^{{}^{\bullet}}(X)\ \simeq\ \text{Jac}({\cal W}),

(502)

where the LHS of (
502
) denotes the small quantum cohomology ring of
X
, and the RHS denotes the Jacobian ring.

Let
X=X_{{}_{\Sigma}}
ne an
n
-dimensional compact toric K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler manifold defined by a fan
\Sigma
in
N_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{{}^{n}}
. The mirror of
X
is conjecturally given by a LG-model
(\hat{X},{\cal W})
, where
\hat{X}
is the algebraic torus

T_{{}_{M}}^{{}^{\mathbb{C}}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ M\underset{\mathbb{Z}}{\otimes}\ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{\times}}\ \simeq\ \left(\mathbb{C}\right)^{{}^{n}}

(503)

such that

\text{Jac}({\cal W})\ =\ \frac{\mathbb{C}\left[z_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{\pm 1}},...,z_{{}_{n}}^{{}^{\pm 1}}\right]}{\left<z_{{}_{1}}\frac{\partial{\cal W}}{\partial z_{{}_{1}}},...,z_{{}_{n}}\frac{\partial{\cal W}}{\partial z_{{}_{n}}}\right>}.

(504)

Tensoring the divisor sequence

0\ \longrightarrow\ M\ \longrightarrow\ \bigoplus_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{m}}\mathbb{Z}D_{{}_{i}}\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{K}^{{}^{V}}\ \longrightarrow\ 0

(505)

by
\mathbb{C}^{{}^{\times}}
, we get the exact sequence

0\ \longrightarrow\ T_{{}_{M}}^{{}^{\mathbb{C}}}\ \longrightarrow\ \left(\mathbb{C}\right)^{{}^{m}}\ \longrightarrow\ \hat{\cal M}_{{}_{B}}\ \longrightarrow\ 0,

(506)

and the mirror is given by the family of algebraic tori

\left(\mathbb{C}\right)^{{}^{m}}\ \longrightarrow\ \hat{\cal M}_{{}_{B}}.

(507)

The mirror of
X
is the pair
\left(\hat{X},{\cal W}_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\text{HV}}}\right)
,
13
, where

{\cal W}_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\text{HV}}}\ \in\ \mathbb{C}\left[z_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{\pm 1}},...,z_{{}_{n}}^{{}^{\pm 1}}\right]

(508)

over
\hat{\cal M}_{{}_{B}}
. Explicitly, (
508
) reads

{\cal W}_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\text{HV}}}\ =\ \sum_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{m}}\hat{C}_{{}_{i}}z^{{}^{v_{{}_{i}}}},

(509)

where

z^{{}^{V}}\ \equiv\ z_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{v^{{}^{1}}}}...z_{{}_{n}}^{{}^{v^{{}^{n}}}}\ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ v=\left(v^{{}^{1}},...,v^{{}^{n}}\right)\ \in\ N\ \simeq\ \mathbb{Z}^{{}^{n}},

(510)

and the coefficients satisfy the following constraint

\hat{C}_{{}_{i}}\ \in\ \mathbb{C}\ \ \ \ \text{s.t.}\ \ \ \ t_{{}_{a}}\ =\ \prod_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{m}}\ \hat{C}_{{}_{i}}^{{}^{D_{{}_{a}}\cdot\gamma_{{}_{a}}}}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ a=1,...,r

(511)

with

t\ =\ \left(t_{{}_{1}},...,t_{{}_{r}}\right)

(512)

being complex coordinates on

\hat{\cal M}_{{}_{B}}\ \simeq\ \left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{\times}}\right)^{{}^{r}}.

(513)

13.1.3
SYZ beyond CYs
