[Auroux] consider pairs
(X,D)
,
X
a compact K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler manifold,
D
a simple normal-crossing anti-canonical divisor,
D\subset X
. It makes sense to consider Lagrangian submanifolds in
X\backslash D
.

In presence if a special Lagrangian torus fibration

\rho:\ X\backslash D\ \longrightarrow\ B

(514)

we can apply the SYZ program to determine
\hat{X}
.

Take
X=X_{{}_{\Sigma}}
to be a compact toric K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler manifold; a canonical choice of
D
is the union of all toric prime divisors
D_{{}_{1}},...,D_{{}_{m}}
. Equipping
X
with a toric K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler structure
\omega
, there is an action on
(X,\omega)
by the torus

T_{{}_{N}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ N\otimes_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}}}U(1)\ \simeq\ T^{{}^{n}}

(515)

which is Hamiltonian. The associated moment map

\rho:\ X\ \longrightarrow\ \Delta

(516)

with
\Delta\subset M_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}
a Lagrangian torus fibration.
\Delta
is viewed as a tropical affine manifold with boundary
\partial\Delta
. Note that the restriction of
\rho
to the complement

U_{{}_{o}}\ =\ X\backslash D

(517)

is a Lagrangian
T^{{}^{n}}
-fibration without singular fibers. Indeed,

\rho|_{{}_{U_{{}_{o}}}}:\ U_{{}_{o}}\ \longrightarrow\ \Delta^{{}^{o}},

(518)

where
\Delta^{{}^{o}}
denotes the interior of
\Delta
.

In complete analogy with the semi-flat case, we therefore have that

U_{{}_{o}}\ =\ X\ \backslash\ D\ \simeq\ T^{{}^{*}}\Delta^{{}^{o}}/\Lambda^{{}^{\text{V}}},

(519)

where
\Lambda^{{}^{\text{V}}}
is the trivial lattice bundle
\Delta^{{}^{o}}\times N
. The SYZ mirror
\hat{X}
is therefore simply given by taking

\hat{X}\ =\ T\Delta^{{}^{o}}/\Lambda,

(520)

where
\Lambda
is the dual lattice bundle

\Lambda\ =\ \Delta^{{}^{o}}\ \times\ N.

(521)

Notice that the SYZ mirror
\hat{X}
is a bounded domain inside the algebraic torus

TM_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}/\Lambda\ =\ T_{{}_{M}}^{{}^{\mathbb{C}}}\ \simeq\ \left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{\times}}\right)^{{}^{n}}

(522)

instead of the whole space.

Let
L\subset X
be a Lagrangian torus fiber of the moment map
\rho
. Fixing a relative homotopy class

\beta\ \in\ \pi_{{}_{2}}(X,L)

(523)

and considering the moduli space
\overline{\cal M}_{{}_{k}}(L;\beta)
of stable maps from genus-0 bordered Riemann surfaces with connected boundary and
k
-boundary marked points respecting the cyclic order represented by the class
\beta
. This moduli space is a stable map compactification of the space of holomorphic disks,namely of the holomorphic embeddings

\left(D^{{}^{2}},\partial D^{{}^{2}}\right)\ \hookrightarrow\ (X,L).

(524)

\overline{\cal M}_{{}_{k}}(L;\beta)
admits a Kunaishi structure with virtual dimension

\text{vir\ dim}_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}\ \overline{\cal M}_{{}_{k}}(L;\beta)\ =\ n+\ \mu(\beta)\ +\ k\ -\ 3,

(525)

with
\mu(\beta)
denoting the Maslov index.

The genus-0 Gromov-Witten invariant is defined as follows

n_{{}_{\beta}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \text{ev}_{{}_{*}}\left(\left[\overline{\cal M}_{{}_{k}}(L;\beta)\right]^{{}^{\text{vir}}}\right)\ \in\ H_{{}_{n}}(L;\mathbb{Q})\ \simeq\ \mathbb{Q},

(526)

where

\text{ev}:\ \overline{\cal M}_{{}_{k}}(L;\beta)\ \longrightarrow\ L

(527)

is the evaluation at the unique boundary marked point. The numbers
n_{{}_{\beta}}
are independent w.r.t. the choice of perturbations, and are therefore invariants for the pair
(X,L)
, reason why they are usually referred to as Gromov-Witten invariants.

The Lagrangian Floer superpotential is defined as

{\cal W}_{{}_{q}}^{{}^{\text{LF}}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \underset{\mu(\beta)=2}{\sum_{{}_{\beta\in\pi_{{}_{2}}(X,L)}}}n_{{}_{\beta}}Z_{{}_{\beta}},

(528)

where

Z_{{}_{\beta}}(L,\nabla)\ =\ \text{exp}\left(-\int_{{}_{\beta}}\omega_{{}_{q}}\right)\ \text{hol}_{{}_{\nabla}}(\partial\beta).

(529)

In general, (
528
) involves infinitely-many terms.

The semi-flat SYZ-transform

{\cal F}^{{}^{\text{semi-flat}}}:\ \Omega^{{}^{o}}(T)\ \longrightarrow\ \Omega^{{}^{n}}\left(T^{{}^{*}}\right)

(530)

is given by

{\cal F}^{{}^{\text{semi-flat}}}(\phi):\ \int_{{}_{T}}\phi\left(y^{{}^{1}},...,y^{{}^{n}}\right)\ e^{{}^{i\sum_{{}_{j}}\text{d}y_{{}_{j}}\wedge\text{d}y^{{}^{j}}}}

(531)

while the usual Fourier transform gives a function

\hat{\phi}:\ \Lambda^{{}^{\text{V}}}\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{C}

(532)

on the dual lattice
\Lambda^{{}^{\text{V}}}
by

\hat{\phi}\left(m_{{}_{1}},...,m_{{}_{n}}\right)\ =\ \int_{{}_{T}}\phi\left(y_{{}_{1}},...,y_{{}_{n}}\right)\ e^{{}^{i\sum_{{}_{j}}m_{{}_{j}}y^{{}^{j}}}}\text{d}y_{{}_{1}}\wedge...\wedge\text{d}y_{{}_{n}}.

(533)

Combining the two transformations to define the SYZ transformation,
{\cal F}^{{}^{\text{SYZ}}}
, turning differential forms on
T\times\Lambda
to those in
T^{{}^{*}}\times\Lambda^{{}^{\text{V}}}
. Here,
T\times\Lambda
denotes the space of geodesics or affine loops in
T
sitting inside the loop space of
T

T\times\Lambda\ =\ {\cal L}_{{}_{\text{geo}}}T\ \subset\ {\cal L}T.

(534)

For the case of compact toric Fano varieties,
{\cal F}^{{}^{\text{SYZ}}}
interchanges the symplectic and complex geometries, with quantum corrections included.

Consider the open dense subset

U_{{}_{o}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ X\backslash D,

(535)

which is the union of all Lagrangian torus fibers of the moment map.

U_{{}_{o}}\ \simeq\ T^{{}^{*}}\Delta^{{}^{o}}/\Lambda^{{}^{\text{V}}}

(536)

as symplectic manifolds.

\tilde{X}\ \overset{def}{=}\ U_{{}_{o}}\times\Lambda\ \subset\ {\cal L}X

(537)

On
\tilde{X}
, we have the quantum corrected symplectic structure

\tilde{\omega}\ =\ \omega+\Phi

(538)

implying
{\cal F}^{{}^{\text{SYZ}}}
can be constructed by composing
{\cal F}^{{}^{\text{semi-flat}}}
with fiberwise Fourier series.

Theorem

1.

The SYZ transforms interchange the quantum-corrected symplectic structure on
\tilde{X}
with the holomorphic volume form of the pair
(\hat{X},{\cal W})
, i.e. we have

{\cal F}^{{}^{\text{SYZ}}}\left(e^{{}^{i(\omega+\Phi)}}\right)\ =\ e^{{}^{\cal W}}\tilde{\Omega}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left({\cal F}^{{}^{\text{SYZ}}}\right)^{{}^{-1}}\ \left(e^{{}^{\cal W}}\tilde{\Omega}\right)\ =\ e^{{}^{i(\omega+\Phi)}}.

(539)

2.

The SYZ transformation induces an isomorphism

{\cal F}^{{}^{\text{SYZ}}}:\ {\cal Q}H^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(X,\omega_{{}_{q}}\right)\ \xrightarrow{\ }\text{Jac}\left({\cal W}_{{}_{q}}^{{}^{\text{LF}}}\right)

(540)

between the small quantum cohomology ring of
X
and the Jacobian ring of the Lagriangian Floer superpotential
{\cal W}_{{}_{q}}^{{}^{\text{LF}}}
.

13.2
Open Mirror Theorems

For semi-Fano toric Fano varieties, the mirror theorems of Givental and Lian-Liu-Yau imply the existence of the following isomorphism
12

{\cal Q}H^{{}^{\bullet}}\left(X,\omega_{{}_{q}}\right)\ \simeq\ \text{Jac}\left({\cal W}_{{}_{t(q)}}^{{}^{\text{LF}}}\right),

(541)

where
t(q)=\psi^{{}^{-1}}(q)
is the inverse of the toric mirror map

\displaystyle\psi:\ \hat{\cal M}_{{}_{B}}\ \longrightarrow\ {\cal M}_{{}_{A}}

(542)

\displaystyle t\ =\ \left(t_{{}_{1}},...,t_{{}_{r}}\right)\ \mapsto\ \psi(t)\ =\ \left(q_{{}_{1}}(t),...,q_{{}_{r}}(t)\right)

Instanton corrections can be realised using the genus 0 open Gromov-Witten invariants,
n_{{}_{\beta}}
. In terms of the Lagrangian Floer superpotential

{\cal W}_{{}_{q}}^{{}^{\text{LF}}}\ =\ \underset{\mu(\beta)=2}{\sum_{{}_{\beta\in\pi_{{}_{2}}(X,L)}}}\ n_{{}_{\beta}}Z_{{}_{\beta}}.

(543)

Conjecture

Let
X
be a semi-Fano toric manifold, namely, its anticanonical divisor,
-K_{{}_{X}}
is nef. Then,

{\cal W}_{{}_{t(q)}}^{{}^{\text{HV}}}\ =\ {\cal W}_{{}_{q}}^{{}^{\text{LF}}}

(544)

via the inverse mirror map
t(q)=\psi^{{}^{-1}}(q)
.

The semi-Fano varieties,
n_{{}_{\beta}}\neq 0
iff

\beta\ =\ \beta_{{}_{i}}+d,

(545)

with
\beta_{{}_{i}}
denoting a basic disk class, and
d\in H_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{\text{eff}}}(X;\mathbb{Z})
an effective curve class such that

c_{{}_{1}}(d)\ =\ c_{{}_{1}}(X)\cdot d\ =\ 0.

(546)

In particular, the Lagrangian Floer superpotential is a Laurent polynomial that can be expressed as

{\cal W}_{{}_{q}}^{{}^{\text{LF}}}\ =\ \sum_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{n}}\ C_{{}_{i}}\left(1+\delta_{{}_{i}}(q)\right)\ z^{{}^{v_{{}_{i}}}},

(547)

where

1+\delta_{{}_{i}}(q)\ =\ \underset{c_{{}_{1}}(\alpha)=0}{\sum_{{}_{\beta\in H_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{\text{eff}}}(X;\mathbb{Z})}}}\ \ n_{{}_{\beta_{{}_{i}}+\alpha}}q^{{}^{\alpha}}

(548)

is a generic function of genus 0 open GW invariants, with
C_{{}_{i}}\in\mathbb{C}
constants such that

\displaystyle q_{{}_{a}}\ =\ \prod_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{m}}C_{{}_{i}}^{{}^{D_{{}_{i}}\cdot\gamma_{{}_{a}}}}\ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ a=1,...,r

(549)

\displaystyle q\ =\ \left(q_{{}_{1}},...,q_{{}_{r}}\right)\ \in\ {\cal M}_{{}_{A}},

with
q
denoting the complexified K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler parameters.

The SYZ map

\displaystyle\phi:\ {\cal M}_{{}_{A}}\ \longrightarrow\ \hat{\cal M}_{{}_{B}}

(550)

\displaystyle q=\left(q_{{}_{1}},...,q_{{}_{r}}\right)\ \mapsto\ \phi(q)\ =\ \left(t_{{}_{1}}(q),...,t_{{}_{r}}(q)\right)

13.3
Mirror Symmetry for Toric CY varieties

Let
X=X_{{}_{\Sigma}}
be a toric CY variety of complex dimension
n
. By choosing a suitable basis of
N=\mathbb{Z}^{{}^{n}}
. One may then write

v_{{}_{i}}\ =\ \left(w_{{}_{i}},1\right)\ \in\ N\ =\ \mathbb{Z}^{{}^{n-1}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ w_{{}_{i}}\ \in\ \mathbb{Z}^{{}^{n-1}}.

(551)

Assuming
X
is smooth and the fan
\Sigma
has convex support. In such case, the CY is a crepant resolution of an affine toric variety (defined by the cone
|\Sigma|
) with Gorenstein canonical singularities, making
X
semi-projective.

13.3.1
Crepant resolutions of CY orbifolds

To construct a CY
13
13

13

A complex Kaehler manifold with trivial canonical bundle.
, one can start from a singular CY. Among the simplest singularities to be considered, we could use orbifold singularities, where an orbifold is a quotinet of a smooth CY manifold by a discrete group action featuring fixed points. Locally modelled on
\mathbb{C}^{{}^{n}}/G
, with
G
a finite subgroup of
SL(n,\mathbb{C})
.

A resolution
(X,\pi)
of
\mathbb{C}^{{}^{n}}/G
is a nonsignular complex manifold
X
of dimension
n
with proper biholomorphic map

\pi:\ x\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{n}}/G

(552)

inducing a biholomorphism between dense open sets.

X
is said to be a
crepant resolution
if the canonical bundles are isomorphic

K_{{}_{X}}\ \simeq\ \pi^{{}^{*}}\left(K_{{}_{\mathbb{C}^{{}^{n}}/G}}\right).

(553)

To obtain a CY structure on
X
, one must choose a crepant resolution of singularities.

The amount of information we can access about a crepant resolution of singularities of
\mathbb{C}^{{}^{n}}/G
heavily depends on the dimension
n
of the orbifold:

•

\boxed{n=2}
There is a unique crepant resolution. In this case, the Topology of the manifold is completely described by the finite group
G
.

•

\boxed{n=3}
A crepant resolution exists, but is not unique. The different possible resolutions are related by flops, and share the same Euler and Betti numbers.

•

\boxed{n\geq 4}
Very little is known in this case.

13.3.2
Example:
K_{{}_{\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}}}

Take
X=K_{{}_{\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}}}
, a toric CY 3-fold, whose fan
\Sigma
has rays generated by the lattice points

v_{{}_{0}}\ =\ (0,0,1)\ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ v_{{}_{1}}\ =\ (1,0,1)\ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ v_{{}_{2}}\ =\ (0,1,1)\ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ v_{{}_{3}}\ =\ (-1,-1,1)

(554)

v_{{}_{i}}\ \in\ N\ =\ \mathbb{Z}^{{}^{3}}.

(555)

Mirror symmetry, in this setting is called local mirror symmetry since it originates from mirror symmetry techniques applied to Fano surfaces, such as
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}
, contained inside compact CY manifolds, and could be recovered by using physical arguments from mirror symmetry for compact CY hypersurfaces in toric varieties by taking certain limits in the complexified K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler and complex moduli spaces.

The mirror of
X
defined as above is a family of affine hypersurfaces in

\hat{X}_{{}_{t}}\ =\ \left\{\left(u,v,z_{{}_{1}},...,z_{{}_{n-1}}\right)\ \in\ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}\times\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{\times}}\right)^{{}^{n-1}}:\ uv\ =\ \sum_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{m}}\hat{C}_{{}_{i}}z^{{}^{w_{{}_{i}}}}\right\},

(556)

where
\hat{C}_{{}_{i}}\ \in\ \mathbb{C}
are constants subject to constraints

t_{{}_{a}}\ =\ \prod_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{m}}\ \ \hat{C}_{{}_{i}}^{{}^{D_{{}_{i}}\cdot\gamma_{{}_{a}}}}\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ a=1,...,r,

(557)

with

t\ =\ \left(t_{{}_{1}},...,t_{{}_{r}}\right)

(558)

are coordinates on the mirror complex moduli

\hat{\cal M}_{{}_{B}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \mathbb{K}^{{}^{\text{V}}}\underset{\mathbb{Z}}{\otimes}\mathbb{C}^{{}^{\times}}\ \simeq\ \left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{\times}}\right)^{{}^{r}}.

(559)

The mirror dual
\hat{X}_{{}_{t}}
is noncompact with nowhere vanishing volume form

\hat{\Omega}_{{}_{t}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \text{Res}\left(\frac{\text{d}u\wedge\text{d}v\wedge\text{d}\log z_{{}_{1}}\ \wedge\ ...\ \wedge\text{d}\log z_{{}_{n-1}}}{uv-\sum_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{m}}\hat{C}_{{}_{i}}z^{{}^{w_{{}_{i}}}}}\right).

(560)

(
556
) for
X=K_{{}_{\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}}}
reads as follows

\hat{X}_{{}_{t}}\ =\ \left\{\left(u,v,z_{{}_{1}},z_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \in\ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}\times\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{\times}}\right)^{{}^{2}}:\ uv\ =\ 1+z_{{}_{1}}+z_{{}_{2}}+\frac{t}{z_{{}_{1}}z_{{}_{2}}}\right\},

(561)

with
t
being a coordinate on the mirror complex moduli space
\hat{\cal M}_{{}_{B}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{{}^{\times}}
.

Recalling the definition of local GW-invariants

N_{{}_{g,d}}(X)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \int_{{}_{\left[\overline{\cal M}_{{}_{g,0}}(X,d)\right]^{{}^{\text{vir}}}}}\mathbf{1}.

(562)

These invariants are, in general, hard to compute due to nontrivial obstructions. However, mirror symmetry can be used to calculate, at least, genus 0 invariants.

As previously outlined, performing such calculations requires a mirror map

\psi:\ \hat{\cal M}_{{}_{B}}\ \longrightarrow\ {\cal M}_{{}_{A}}

(563)

which is a local isomorphism from the complex moduli
\hat{\cal M}_{{}_{B}}
of the mirror
\hat{X}
to the complexified K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler moduli
{\cal M}_{{}_{A}}
of
X
. Traditionally, mirror maps are defined as quotients of period integrals

\int_{{}_{\Gamma}}\ \ \hat{\Omega},

(564)

with
\Gamma\in H_{{}_{3}}\left(\hat{X};\mathbb{Z}\right)
a middle-dimensional integral cocyle. Such integrals satisfy a system of Picard-Fuchs PDEs, whose solutions are explicit hypergeometric-type functions (this is the usual procedure through which mirror maps are usually calculated). For the specifics of the example in question, the PF system reduces to a single ODE

\left[\left(t\ \frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}t}\right)^{{}^{3}}+3t^{{}^{2}}\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}t}\left(3+t\ \frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}t}+1\right)\left(3t\ \frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}t}+2\right)\right]\ \Phi\ =\ =0

(565)

A basis of solutions to this equation is explicitly given by

\displaystyle\Phi_{{}_{0}}(t)\ =\ 1

(566)

\displaystyle\Phi_{{}_{1}}(t)\ =\ \log t+\sum_{{}_{k=1}}^{{}^{\infty}}\frac{(-1)^{{}^{k}}}{k}\frac{(3k)!}{(k!)^{{}^{3}}}\ t^{{}^{k}}

\displaystyle\Phi_{{}_{1}}(t)\ =\ \left(\log t\right)^{{}^{2}}+...

The mirror map is

q\ =\ \psi(t)\ =\ \text{exp}\left(\frac{\Phi_{{}_{1}}(t)}{\Phi_{{}_{0}}(t)}\right)\ =\ t\ (1+...)

(567)

and genus 0 GW-invariants
N_{{}_{0,d}}(X)
are predicted to appear as the coefficients of the Taylor series expansion of
\Phi_{{}_{2}}\left(\psi^{{}^{-1}}(q)\right)
around
q=0
.

Via the inverse mirror map

q\ \mapsto\ \psi^{{}^{-1}}(q)

(568)

and a coordinate change, the mirror of
K_{{}_{\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}}}
is

\hat{X}_{{}_{t}}\ =\ \left\{\left(u,v,z_{{}_{1}},z_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \in\ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}\times\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{\times}}\right)^{{}^{2}}:\ uv\ =\ f(q)+z_{{}_{1}}+z_{{}_{2}}+\frac{q}{z_{{}_{1}}z_{{}_{2}}}\right\},

(569)

with

f(q)\ =\ \left(\frac{\psi(t)}{t}\right)^{{}^{\frac{1}{3}}}\ =\ 1-2q+5q^{{}^{2}}-32q^{{}^{3}}+286q^{{}^{4}}-3038q^{{}^{5}}+...

(570)

Later on, we will see that the coefficients featuring in
f(q)
constitute virtual counts of holomorphic disks.

SYZ construction

For the case of a toric CY manifold (or, more generically, an orbifold),
X
, a special Lagrangian torus fibration on
X
is needed, [Goldstein] [Gross].

Setup

Take a lattice point
u
, defining the hyperplane containing all the generators
v_{{}_{i}}
, corresponding to the holomorphic function

\chi^{{}^{u}}:\ X\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{C}

(571)

with simple zeros along each toric prime divisor
D_{{}_{i}}\subset X
. Equipping
X
by a K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler structure
\omega
, and considering the action by the subtorus

T^{{}^{n-1}}\ \subset\ T_{{}_{N}}\ \simeq\ T^{{}^{n}}

(572)

preserving
\chi^{{}^{u}}
, therefore also preserving the canonical holomorphic volume form
\Omega
on
X
.

Let

\rho_{{}_{o}}:\ X\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{R}^{{}^{n-1}}

(573)

be the corresponding moment map which is given by composing the
T_{{}_{N}}
-moment map with the projection along the ray in
M_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}
spanned by
u
.

Proposition
[Goldstein] [Gross] For any nonzero constant
\epsilon\ \in\ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{\times}}
, the map defined by

\rho\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left(\rho_{{}_{o}},\left|\chi^{{}^{u}}-\epsilon\right|\right):\ X\ \longrightarrow\ B\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \mathbb{R}^{{}^{n-1}}\times\mathbb{R}_{{}_{\geq 0}}.

(574)

The base of the Gross fibration on
K_{{}_{\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}}}
, is an upper-half space in
\mathbb{R}^{{}^{3}}
.
\rho
is a special Lagrangian torus fibration, where the fibers are special w.r.t. themeromorphic volume form

\Omega_{{}_{\epsilon}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \frac{\Omega}{\chi^{{}^{u}}-\epsilon}

(575)

with
\rho
the Gross fibration, which is non-toric due to the lack of a
T_{{}_{N}}
-moment map. A fiber of
\rho
is non-regular iff, either:

1.

When it intersects nontrivially with the hypersurface
D_{{}_{\epsilon}}\subset X
defined by

\chi^{{}^{u}}\ =\ \epsilon

(576)

is then mapped to the boundary

\partial B\ =\ \mathbb{R}^{{}^{n-1}}\times\{0\}.

(577)

2.

Or, when it contains a point where the
T^{{}^{n-1}}
-action is not free, i.e. if when at least two of the homogeneous coordinates on
X
vanish, in which case the fiber is mapped to the image
\Gamma
of the codimension-2 subvariety

\bigcup_{{}_{i\neq j}}\ \ D_{{}_{i}}\ \cap\ D_{{}_{j}}

(578)

under
\rho
.

In the above,
B
denotes a tropical affine manifold with boundary
\partial B
and singularities
\Gamma
, the latter being a codimension-2 surface in
B
.

\bullet\text{{\ \ \ STEPS (STZ prescription) [Auroux]}}

Starting with a special Lagrangian torus fibration

\rho:\ X\ \longrightarrow\ B

(579)

on a CY manifold, the SYZ mirror construction proceeds in several steps:

Step 1
Over the smooth locus

B_{{}_{o}}\ \equiv\ B\backslash(\partial B\cup\ \Gamma),

(580)

the pre-image

X_{{}_{o}}\ \equiv\ \rho^{{}^{-1}}(B_{{}_{o}})

(581)

can be identified with the quotient

T^{{}^{*}}B_{{}_{o}}/\Lambda^{{}^{\text{V}}}.

(582)

Step 2
Define the semi-flat mirror,
\hat{X}_{{}_{o}}
as
TB_{{}_{o}}/\Lambda
(not the correct mirror since the complex structure on
\hat{X}_{{}_{o}}
cannot be extended further to any (partial) compactification as the monodromy of the tropical affine structure around the discriminant locus
\Gamma
is nontrivial).

Step 3
To obtain the correct and (partially) compactified mirror
\hat{X}
, we need to modify the complex structure on
\hat{X}_{{}_{o}}
by instanton corrections, which should come from holomorphic disks in
X
bounded by Lagrangian torus fibers on the fibration
\rho
.

Inside a base
B
, one can define a
wall
,
H
, inside the base of a Lagrangian torus fibration to be the union of the loci of regular fibers which nonconstant holomorphic or stable disks of Maslov index 0 in
X
.

For the Gross fibration on a toric CY manifold, there is only one wall given by the hyperplane

H\ =\ \mathbb{R}^{{}^{n-1}}\times\left\{|\epsilon|\right\}\ \subset\ B\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ H\ //\ \partial B,

(583)

containing the discriminant locus
\Gamma
as a tropical hypersurface, and it divides the base
B
into two chambers:

B_{{}_{+}}\ \equiv\ \mathbb{R}^{{}^{n-1}}\times\left(|\epsilon|,+\infty\right)\ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ B_{{}_{-}}\ \equiv\ \mathbb{R}^{{}^{n-1}}\times\left(0,|\epsilon|\right)

(584)

over which the Lagrangian torus fibers behave differently in a Floer-theoretic sense.

Figure 12:
H
is the wall between
B_{{}_{+}}
and
B_{{}_{-}}
, whereas the graph
\Gamma
is the discriminant locus, contained in the hyperplane
H//\partial B
. The base of the Gross fibration on
K_{{}_{\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}}}
is an upper-half space in
\mathbb{R}^{{}^{3}}
.

Genus 0 open GW-invariants correspond to the virtual counts of Maslov index 2 stable disks
14
14

14

The geometric meaning of the Maslov index 2 here means that the stable disks intersect with the hypersurface
D_{{}_{\epsilon}}
at only 1 point with multiplicity 1.
in
X
bounded by fibers of
\rho
. As one moves from one chamber to the other by crossing the wall
H
, the virtual counts of Maslov index 2 disks bounded by the corresponding Lagrangian torus fiber would jump, exhibiting a wall-crossing phenomenon. Fibers over the chamber
B_{{}_{-}}
bound only one family of Maslov index 2 disks, such that the corresponding generating function of open GW-invariants has just one term with coefficient 1.

On the other hand, fibers over
B_{{}_{+}}
bound as many Maslov index 2 disks as a moment map Lagrangian torus fiber, such that the corresponding generating function of open GW-invariants has (possibly) infinitely-many terms.

The resulting wall-crossing factor is exactly what we need to get the correct mirror

{\cal W}_{{}_{q}}^{{}^{\text{LF}}}\ =\ \begin{cases}&z_{{}_{o}}\sum_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{m}}\ \left(1+\delta_{{}_{i}}(q)\right)\ C_{{}_{i}}z^{{}^{w_{{}_{i}}}},\ \text{over}\ \ B_{{}_{+}}\\
&u\ \ \ \ ,\ \text{over}\ \ B_{{}_{-}}\\
\end{cases}

(585)

where

1+\delta_{{}_{i}}(q)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{\alpha\in H_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{eff}}(X;\mathbb{Z})}}n_{{}_{\beta_{{}_{i}}+d}}q^{{}^{d}}

(586)

is a generating function of genus-0 GW-invariants.

z_{{}_{o}},u
denote the coordinates associated to the lattice point

(0,...,0,1)\ \in\ \mathbb{Z}^{{}^{n-1}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}

(587)

over
B_{{}_{+}}
and
B_{{}_{-}}
, respectively, and

z^{{}^{w}}\ =\ z_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{w^{{}^{1}}}}...z_{{}_{n-1}}^{{}^{w^{{}^{n-1}}}}\ \ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ w\ =\ \left(w^{{}^{1}},...,w^{{}^{n-1}}\right)\ \in\ \mathbb{Z}^{{}^{n-1}}

(588)

q^{{}^{d}}\ =\ \exp\left(-\int_{{}_{d}}\omega_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}\right).

(589)

The semi-flat mirror

\hat{X}_{{}_{o}}\ =\ TB_{{}_{o}}/\Lambda

(590)

can be viewed as the gluing of two complex charts

\hat{X}_{{}_{+}}\ =\ TB_{{}_{+}}/\Lambda\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \hat{X}_{{}_{-}}\ =\ TB_{{}_{-}}/\Lambda

(591)

associated to the chambers
B_{{}_{+}}
and
B_{{}_{-}}
, respectively. Nontrivial monodromy of the topological affine structure on
B_{{}_{o}}
around the discriminant locus
\Gamma
prevents the complex structure on
\hat{X}
from extending any further.

[Auroux] showed that, following Floer theory,
{\cal W}_{{}_{q}}^{{}^{\text{LF}}}
is an analytic function, hence one should modify the gluing between the complex charts,
\hat{X}_{{}_{\pm}}
by the wall-crossing factors

\sum_{{}_{1=1}}^{{}^{m}}\left(1+\delta_{{}_{i}}(q)\right)C_{{}_{i}}z^{{}^{w_{{}_{i}}}}

(592)

which cancels the nontrivial monodromy of the complex structure around
\Gamma\subset B
, and gives an analytic superpotential on the corrected mirror,
\hat{X}
, leading to the following

Theorem

The SYZ mirror for the toric CY manifold,
X
is given by the family of affine hypersurfaces

\hat{X}_{{}_{q}}\ =\ \left\{\left(u,v,z_{{}_{1}},...,z_{{}_{n-1}}\right)\ \in\ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}\times\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{\times}}\right)^{{}^{n-1}}:\ uv\ =\ \sum_{{}_{1=1}}^{{}^{m}}\left(1+\delta_{{}_{i}}(q)\right)C_{{}_{i}}z^{{}^{w_{{}_{i}}}}\right\}

(593)

entirely written in terms of the complexified K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler parameters and disk-counting invariants of
X
.

Application of the Theorem

The SYZ mirror of
X=K_{{}_{\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}}}
is given by

\hat{X}\ =\ \left\{\left(u,v,z_{{}_{1}},...,z_{{}_{n-1}}\right)\ \in\ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}\times\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{\times}}\right)^{{}^{n-1}}:\ uv\ =\ 1+\delta_{{}_{o}}(q)+z_{{}_{1}}+z_{{}_{2}}+\frac{q}{z_{{}_{1}}z_{{}_{2}}}\right\},

(594)

where
q
is the K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler parameter measuring the symplectic area of a projective line contained inside the nonzero section of
K_{{}_{\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}}}
over
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}
, and

1+\delta_{{}_{o}}(q)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{k=0}}^{{}^{\infty}}n_{{}_{\beta_{{}_{o}}+kl}}q^{{}^{k}}

(595)

being the generating function of genus-0 open GW-invariants,

l\ \in\ H_{{}_{2}}\left(K_{{}_{\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}}};\mathbb{Z}\right)\ =\ H_{{}_{2}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}};\mathbb{Z}\right)

(596)

denotes the hyperplane class in
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}
,

q\ =\ \exp(-t)

(597)

\beta_{{}_{o}}
is the basic disk class, corresponding to the 0-section

\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}\ \subset\ K_{{}_{\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}}},

(598)

which is the only compact toric divisor in
K_{{}_{\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}}}
.

Applying the open/closed formula and a flop formula for closed GW-invariants, the genus-0 open GW-invariants
n_{{}_{\beta_{{}_{o}}+kl}}q^{{}^{k}}
can be expressed in terms of local BPS invariants of
K_{{}_{\mathbb{F}}}
, where
\mathbb{F}_{{}_{1}}
is the blowup of
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}
at a point. If

e,f\ \in\ H_{{}_{2}}\left(K_{{}_{\mathbb{F}_{{}_{1}}}};\mathbb{Z}\right)\ =\ H_{{}_{2}}\left(\mathbb{F}_{{}_{1}};\mathbb{Z}\right)

(599)

are the classes represented by the exceptional divisor and fiber of the blowup

\mathbb{F}_{{}_{1}}\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}

(600)

respectively, then

n_{{}_{\beta_{{}_{o}}+kl}}q^{{}^{k}}\ =\ N^{{}^{K_{{}_{\mathbb{F}_{{}_{1}}}}}}_{{}_{0,0,kf+(k-1)e}}

(601)

where the RHS denotes the local BPS invariants for the class

kf+(k-1)e\ \in\ H_{{}_{2}}\left(K_{{}_{\mathbb{F}_{{}_{1}}}};\mathbb{Z}\right).

(602)

Direct calculation of the latter was performed by [Chiang, Klemm, Yau, Zaslow].

The first explicit values read

\displaystyle n_{{}_{\beta_{{}_{o}}+l}}\ =

\displaystyle-2\ \ \ ,\ \ \ n_{{}_{\beta_{{}_{o}}+2l}}\ =\ 5\ \ \ ,\ \ \ n_{{}_{\beta_{{}_{o}}+3l}}\ =\ -32

(603)

\displaystyle n_{{}_{\beta_{{}_{o}}+4l}}\ =\ 286\ \ \ ,\ \ \ n_{{}_{\beta_{{}_{o}}+5l}}\ =\ -3038\ \ \ ,\ \ \ .\ .\ .

Upon substituting them in the generating function, we get

1+\delta_{{}_{o}}(q)\ =\ 1-2q+5q^{{}^{2}}-32q^{{}^{3}}+286q^{{}^{4}}-3038q^{{}^{5}}+\ ...

(604)

When compared with the expression for
f(q)
, (
570
),

f(q)\ =\ \left(\frac{\psi(t)}{t}\right)^{{}^{1/3}},

(605)

it follows that the coefficients of
f(q)
are exactly the virtual counts
n_{{}_{\beta_{{}_{o}}+kl}}
of holomorphic disks.

Note that one could also perform the inverse construction, namely starting from a conic bundle to construct a Lagrangian torus fibration, thereby proving the involutive property of the SYZ mirror construction.

13.4
Non-Fano cases: Hirzebruch surfaces

Hirzebruch surfaces are defined as follows

\mathbb{F}_{{}_{n}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \mathbb{P}\left({\cal O}_{{}_{\mathbb{P}^{{}^{1}}}}\oplus{\cal O}_{{}_{\mathbb{P}^{{}^{1}}}}(n)\right).

(606)

13.4.1
The Chekarov Torus

Taken from ”An introduction to the Chekarov torus” (JOe’ Brendel), 2021

The Chekarov torus in
\mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}
is the first example of a Lagrangian torus in
(\mathbb{R}^{{}^{4}},\omega_{{}_{o}})
that cannot be mapped to a product torus by symplectomorphisms. Highlighting the relationship with Hamiltonian torus actions and symplectic reduction.

Construction of the Chekarov Torus in
\mathbb{R}^{{}^{4}}

One key element is the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle F:\ \ \ \ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}\ \ \ \longrightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \mathbb{R}

(607)

\displaystyle(z_{{}_{1}},z_{{}_{2}})\ \mapsto\ \pi\left(|z_{{}_{1}}|^{{}^{2}}-|z_{{}_{1}}|^{{}^{2}}\right)

inducing the following Hamiltonian circle action

\psi_{{}_{\theta}}(z_{{}_{1}},z_{{}_{2}})\ =\ \left(e^{{}^{2\pi i\theta}}z_{{}_{1}},e^{{}^{-2\pi i\theta}}z_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \theta\ \in\ S^{{}^{1}}\ =\ \mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}

(608)

Level sets of
F
are

Z_{{}_{C}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ F^{{}^{-1}}(c)\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \forall\ c\ \in\ \mathbb{R}.

(609)

For
c\neq 0
,
Z_{{}_{C}}
is smooth, implying it is diffeomorphic to
S^{{}^{1}}\times\mathbb{C}
, the only critical value being
c=0
, with
0\in\mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}
being the corresponding critical point.

Figure 13:

Figure 14:

Figure 15:
The Chekarov torus (in red) is obtained from lifting a Lagrangian circle in
\mathbb{C}
past the singular point
M_{{}_{o}}
, corresponding to the tip of the cone.

Proposition

The symplectic quotient

M_{{}_{C}}\ =\ F^{{}^{-1}}(c)/S^{{}^{1}}\ =\ Z_{{}_{C}}/S^{{}^{1}}.

(610)

The moment map of the standard toric structure on
\mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}
is

\displaystyle\mu:

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}\ \ \ \longrightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \mathbb{R}_{{}_{\geq 0}}^{{}^{2}}

(611)

\displaystyle(z_{{}_{1}},z_{{}_{2}})\ \mapsto\ \left(\pi|z_{{}_{1}}|^{{}^{2}},\pi|z_{-2}|^{{}^{2}}\right)

The Chekarov torus is obtained by lifting circles from the quotient spaces
M_{{}_{C}}
to tori in
\mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}
via a 1-parameter family of quotients
15
15

15

That is not well-defined for
c=0
.

(\mathbb{C},\omega_{{}_{o}})\ \simeq\ (M_{{}_{c}},\omega_{{}_{c}})\ \xleftarrow{\ \ \pi_{{}_{c}}\ \ }\ Z_{{}_{c}}\ =\ F^{{}^{-1}}(c)\ \hookrightarrow\ \left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}},\omega_{{}_{o}}\right).

(612)

Circles in the plane are Lagrangian, and therefore, their corresponding tori are automatically Lagrangian. The Chekarov torus of area parameter
a>0
is defined as the lift

T_{{}_{\text{Ch}}}(a)\ =\ \pi_{{}_{o}}^{{}^{-1}}\left(S_{{}_{\text{Ch}}}^{{}^{1}}(a)\right)\ \subset\ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}

(613)

importantly, there are two kinds of circles in the reduced space
M_{{}_{o}}=\mathbb{C}
, those of standard type
S^{{}^{1}}(a)
(enclosing the origin) and those of Chekarov type
S_{{}_{\text{Ch}}}^{{}^{1}}(a)
(not enclosing the origin). Both types come in a 1-parameter family indexed by the area they contain. A smooth circle that passes through the origin lifts to a pinched Lagrangian torus in
\mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}
.

Figure 16:
Lagrangian torus fibration as the lift of circle Lagrangians on the complex plane.

One can define the sequence of Lagrangian tori

L_{{}_{n}}\ =\ \pi^{{}^{-1}}_{{}_{1/n}}\left(S_{{}_{\text{Ch}}}^{{}^{1}}(a)\right),

(614)

which converges to the Chekarov torus
T_{{}_{\text{Ch}}}(a)
.

13.4.2
\mathbb{F}_{{}_{2}}

Take a family of smooth quadratic surfaces

\chi\ \subset\ \mathbb{CP}^{{}^{3}}\ \times\ \mathbb{C}

(615)

\chi\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \{x_{{}_{0}}x_{{}_{1}}\ =\ x_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{2}}-t^{{}^{2}}x_{{}_{3}}^{{}^{2}}\}.

(616)

1.

For
t\neq 0

X_{{}_{t}}\ =\ \left\{x_{{}_{0}}x_{{}_{1}}\ =\ (x_{{}_{2}}+tx_{{}_{3}})(x_{{}_{2}}-tx_{{}_{3}})\right\}\ \subset\ \mathbb{CP}^{{}^{3}}

(617)

is a smooth quadric, and can be identified with the image of the embedding of

\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}\ \times\ \mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}.

(618)

2.

For
t=0

X_{{}_{t}}\ =\ \left\{x_{{}_{0}}x_{{}_{1}}\ =\ x_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{2}}\right\}\ \subset\ \mathbb{CP}^{{}^{3}}

(619)

is a cone with vertex at the point

(0:0:0:1)

(620)

where the 3-fold,
\chi
, presents as an ordinary double point singularity. Denote by

\pi:\ \chi^{{}^{\prime}}\ \longrightarrow\ \chi

(621)

a small resolution. Composing it with the projection to
\mathbb{C}
, we obtain a family of surfaces
X_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\prime}}
, such that

X_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\prime}}\ \simeq\ X_{{}_{t}}

(622)

for
t\neq 0
, while
X_{{}_{o}}^{{}^{\prime}}
is the blowup of
X_{{}_{o}}
, namely

X_{{}_{o}}^{{}^{\prime}}\ \simeq\ \mathbb{F}_{{}_{2}}.

(623)

Consider the family of anticanonical divisors

D_{{}_{t}}\ =\ \{x_{{}_{0}}=0\ ,\ x_{{}_{2}}=tx_{{}_{3}}\}\ \cup\ \{x_{{}_{1}}=0\ ,\ x_{{}_{2}}=tx_{{}_{3}}\}\ \cup\ \{x_{{}_{3}}\ =\ -tx_{{}_{2}}\}\ \subset\ X_{{}_{t}},

(624)

and equip
X_{{}_{t}}
with a holomorphic volume form
\Omega_{{}_{t}}
with poles along
D_{{}_{t}}
.

For
t=1
, the pair
(X_{{}_{t}},D_{{}_{t}})
corresponds to the toric anticanonical divisors in
\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}\times CP^{{}^{1}}
, whereas for arbitrary
t
, the geometry of

(X_{{}_{t}},D_{{}_{t}},\Omega_{{}_{t}})

(625)

closely resembles that of

X\ =\ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \Omega\ =\ \frac{dx\wedge dy}{xy-\epsilon}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ d=\{xy=\epsilon\}

(626)

with corresponding torus fibration

T_{{}_{r,\lambda}}\ =\ \{(x,y)\ \in\ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}},\ |xy-\epsilon|=r,\mu_{{}_{S^{{}^{1}}}}(x,y)\ =\ \lambda\}

(627)

The only difference being that
\mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}
is now compactified to
\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}\times CP^{{}^{1}}
.

D_{{}_{o}}\ \subset\ X_{{}_{o}}
is the toric anticanonical divisor consisting of the lines

x_{{}_{o}}=0\ \ \ ,\ \ \ x_{{}_{1}}=0\ \ \ ,\ \ \ x_{{}_{3}}=0.

(628)

(X_{{}_{t}},D_{{}_{t}},\Omega_{{}_{t}})

(629)

are preserved under the
S^{{}^{1}}
-action:

(x_{{}_{o}}:x_{{}_{1}}:x_{{}_{2}}:x_{{}_{3}})\ \mapsto\ \left(x_{{}_{o}}e^{{}^{i\theta}}:x_{{}_{1}}e^{{}^{-i\theta}}:x_{{}_{2}}:x_{{}_{3}}\right).

(630)

The
S^{{}^{1}}
-action lifts to the small resolution, and the lifted divisor

D_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\prime}}\ =\ \pi^{{}^{-1}}\left(D_{{}_{t}}\right)\ \subset\ X_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\prime}}

(631)

and holomorphic volume form

\Omega_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\prime}}\ \pi^{{}^{*}}\Omega_{{}_{t}}

(632)

that are also
S^{{}^{1}}
-invariant.

At
t=1
and
t=0
, the anticanonical divisors are the toric ones for
\mathbb{F}_{{}_{0}}
and
\mathbb{F}_{{}_{2}}
, respectively. Deforming away from these values, one partially smooths the toric anticanonical divisor, which, in the case of
\mathbb{F}_{{}_{2}}
requires a simultaneous deformation of the complex structure because the exceptional curve is rigid. The deformation from
t=1
to
t=0
is depicted in Figure
17
.

Figure 17:
Deforming
\mathbb{F}_{{}_{0}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{}_{2}}
.

For
t=1
, the mirror superpotential is that of the mirror dual to a Fano variety (namely an
\mathbb{F}_{{}_{0}}
), and simply reads

{\cal W}\ =\ z_{{}_{1}}+z_{{}_{2}}+e^{{}^{-A}}z_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{-1}}+e^{{}^{-B}}z_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{-1}},

(633)

with
A
and
B
denoting the symplectic area of the two
\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}
-factors. In particular, the first two terms correspond to the disks contained in the affine chart, whereas the remaining terms correspond to the disks hitting the lines at
\infty
.

Deforming to generic
t

X_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\prime}}\left\backslash D_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\prime}}\right.\ \simeq\ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}}\left\backslash\left\{xy\ =\ \frac{1-t^{{}^{2}}}{1+t^{{}^{2}}}\right\}\right.

(634)

Even though the K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler form
\omega_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\prime}}
is not toric, the construction of an
S^{{}^{1}}
-invariant special Lagrangian fibration proceeds as in the Fano case with one modification.

T_{{}_{r,\lambda}}\ \subset\ \mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}\times\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}\

(635)

with
T_{{}_{r,\lambda}}
now being a submanifold bounding additional families of Maslov index-2 holomorphic disks (intersecting the lines at
\infty
).

From (
634
), it follows that there are two main chambers

•

In the chamber
r>\left|\frac{1-t^{{}^{2}}}{1+t^{{}^{2}}}\right|
, deforming
T_{{}_{r,\lambda}}
to a product torus shows that it bounds 4 families of Maslov index-2 disks, and the superpotential is given by (
633
).

•

On the other hand, in the chamber
r<\left|\frac{1-t^{{}^{2}}}{1+t^{{}^{2}}}\right|
, deforming
T_{{}_{r,\lambda}}
to the Chekarov torus shows that it bounds 5 families of Maslov index-2 holomorphic disks. The superpotential now reads

{\cal W}\ =\ u+\frac{e^{{}^{-A}}}{uw}+\frac{e^{{}^{-B}}(1+w)}{u},

(636)

where the first term comes from the disks passing through the conic
xy=\frac{1-t^{{}^{2}}}{1+t^{{}^{2}}}
and avoiding all toric divisors, whereas the remaining terms correspond to sections intersecting exactly one of the two lines at
\infty
. There are four different possibilities, giving rise to one of the following families

\left[\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}\times\{\text{pt}\}\right]-\beta_{{}_{o}}-\alpha\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \left[\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}\times\{\text{pt}\}\right]-\beta_{{}_{o}}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \left[\{\text{pt}\}\times\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}\right]-\beta_{{}_{o}}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \left[\{\text{pt}\}\times\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}\right]-\beta_{{}_{o}}+\alpha,

(637)

where
\beta_{{}_{o}},\alpha
are classes in
\pi_{{}_{2}}\left(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{2}},T_{{}_{r,\lambda}}\right)
.

The two expressions (
633
) and (
636
) are related by the following change of variables

w\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \frac{z_{{}_{1}}}{z_{{}_{2}}}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ u\ \overset{def.}{=}\ z_{{}_{1}}+z_{{}_{2}},

(638)

thereby leading to the instanton-corrected gluing between the two chambers.

13.4.3
\mathbb{F}_{{}_{3}}

The corrected superpotential reads

{\cal W}(x,y)\ =\ x+y+\frac{e^{{}^{-[\omega]\cdot\left[S_{{}_{+3}}\right]}}}{xy^{{}^{3}}}+\frac{e^{{}^{-[\omega]\cdot\left[F\right]}}}{y}+\frac{2e^{{}^{-[\omega]\cdot\left(\left[S_{{}_{-3}}\right]+2\left[F\right]\right)}}}{y^{{}^{2}}}+\frac{e^{{}^{-[\omega]\cdot\left(\left[S_{{}_{-3}}\right]+\left[F\right]\right)}}x}{y}.

(639)

The deformation to be performed is as shown in Figure
18
.

Figure 18:
Deforming
\mathbb{F}_{{}_{1}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{}_{3}}
.

Starting from the family
\chi^{{}^{\prime}}
defined in the
\mathbb{F}_{{}_{2}}
case, let
C^{{}^{\prime}}\subset\chi^{{}^{\prime}}
be the proper transform of the curve

C\ =\ \left\{x_{{}_{o}}=x_{{}_{1}}=0,x_{{}_{2}}=tx_{{}_{3}}\right\}\ \subset\ \chi,

(640)

and let
\chi^{{}^{\prime}}
be the blowup of
\chi
along
C^{{}^{\prime}}
.

Let
\hat{Z}\subset\chi^{{}^{\prime}}
be the proper transform of the surface

Z\ =\ \left\{x_{{}_{1}}=0,x_{{}_{2}}=tx_{{}_{3}}\right\}\ \subset\ \chi.

(641)

Then, defining
\chi^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
as the family of surfaces
X_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}
, obtained from
X_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\prime}}
by first blowing up a point, then blowing down the proper transform of the line
x_{{}_{1}}=0,x_{{}_{2}}=tx_{{}_{3}}
, one can check that

X_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\ \simeq\ \mathbb{F}_{{}_{1}}\ \ \ \text{for}\ \ \ t\neq 0

(642)

X_{{}_{o}}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\ \simeq\ \mathbb{F}_{{}_{3}}.

(643)

Moreover, the divisors,
D_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\prime}}\ \subset X_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\prime}}
transform naturally under the biratiopnal transformation defined above, and yield a family of anticanonical divisors

D_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\ \subset\ X_{{}_{t}}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}.

(644)

For
t=0
and
t=1
, these are precisely the toric anticanonical divisors in
X_{{}_{o}}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=\mathbb{F}_{{}_{3}}
and
X_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=\mathbb{F}_{{}_{1}}
.

Once more, for intermediate values of
t
, we distinguish in between two chambers:

•

For
r>\left|\frac{1-t^{{}^{2}}}{1+t^{{}^{2}}}\right|
,
T_{{}_{r,\lambda}}
are isotoped to product tori, and the superpotential reads as follows

{\cal W}\ =\ z_{{}_{1}}+z_{{}_{2}}+\frac{e^{{}^{-(A+B)}}}{z_{{}_{1}}z_{{}_{2}}}+\frac{e^{{}^{-B}}}{z_{{}_{2}}}

(645)

where
A
denotes the area of the exceptional curve, whereas
B
denote sthe area of the ruling fiber in
\mathbb{F}_{{}_{1}}
.

•

For
r<\left|\frac{1-t^{{}^{2}}}{1+t^{{}^{2}}}\right|
, instead,
T_{{}_{r,\lambda}}
are isotoped to CHekarov tori, and the corresponding superpotential reads

{\cal W}\ =\ u+\frac{e^{{}^{-(A+B)}}(1+w)^{{}^{2}}}{u^{{}^{2}}w}+\frac{e^{{}^{-B}}(1+w)}{u}.

(646)

This time, the instanton-corrected gluing between the two chambers is given by the following change of variables

u\ =\ z_{{}_{1}}+z_{{}_{2}}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ w\ =\ \frac{z_{{}_{1}}}{z_{{}_{2}}}.

(647)

The rest of the proof to recover (
639
) is very similar to the
\mathbb{F}_{{}_{2}}
case.

14
Mirror Symmetry for Abelian Varieties

In physical terms, HMS is a correspondence in between A- and B-type D-branes. These are equipped with an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-category structure, encoding the properties of correlation functions in Topological Open String Theory.

For

\text{dim}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}X\ =\ \text{dim}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}\hat{X}\ =\ 1,

(648)

namely for the elliptic curve, the corresponding
{\cal N}=2
SCVA is known, and therefore one knows the precise conditions under which
(X,G,B)
is mirror to
(X^{{}^{\prime}},G^{{}^{\prime}},B^{{}^{\prime}})
. Furthermore, all objects and morphisms in the Fukaya category can be explicitly described.

For the case of algebraic tori of
\text{dim}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}>1
, the original HMS correspondence by Kontsevich does not hold in general anymore. The main reason is that, both,
D^{{}^{b}}
Coh and
D^{{}^{b}}
Fuk do not depend on the
B
-field, whereas for Physicists’ formulation of Mirror Symmetry, the
B
-field plays an essential role. This requires a certain modification of the HMS correspondence.

14.1
Functors between categories of coherent sheaves

Take
X
an algebraic variety over an arbitrary field
\mathbb{k}
.
{\cal O}_{{}_{X}}
the structure sheaf on
X
. Coh
(X)
the category of (quasi)-coherent sheaves on
X
, namely admitting a finite presentation in terms of
{\cal O}_{{}_{X}}
-modules.

Turning to derived categories of Coh
(X)
,
D^{{}^{b}}(X)
, they admit a triangulated categorical structure, equipped with the following properties:

1.

There is a translation functor

[1]:\ D\ \longrightarrow\ D

(649)

that is an additive autoequivalence.

2.

A class of distinguished (or exact) triangles

X\ \xrightarrow{u}\ Y\ \xrightarrow{v}\ Z\ \xrightarrow{w}\ X[1]

(650)

such that certain axioms are satisfied.

An additive functor between categories

F:\ D\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{\prime}}

(651)

is exact if it commutes with the translation functors, and takes every distinguished triangle in
D
to a distinguished triangle in
D^{{}^{\prime}}
. One such example is provided by algebraic varieies

f:\ X\ \longrightarrow\ Y

(652)

\Rightarrow\ \ f_{{}_{*}}:\ \text{QCoh}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{QCoh}(Y)

(653)

A category
\text{QCoh}(X)
admits enough injectives, implying there is a right derived functor

Rf_{{}_{*}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}\left(\text{Coh}(X)\right)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}\left(\text{Coh}(Y)\right).

(654)

Focusing on smooth projective varieties, every map

f:\ X\ \longrightarrow\ Y

(655)

induces two functors

Rf_{{}_{*}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(Y)

(656)

and

Lf^{{}^{*}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(Y)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(X).

(657)

Furthermore, each complex

{\cal F}\ \in\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)

(658)

induces an exact functor

\overset{L}{\bigotimes}\ {\cal F}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)

(659)

Making use of these functors, we can introduce a larger class of exact functors. Let
X
and
Y
be smooth projective varieties of dimension
n
and
m
, respectively.

Consider the projections

X\ \xleftarrow{p}\ X\times Y\ \xrightarrow{q}\ Y

(660)

Every object

{\cal E}\ \in\ D^{{}^{b}}(X\times Y)

(661)

defines an exact functor

\Phi_{{}_{\cal E}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(Y)

(662)

by the following formula

\Phi_{{}_{\cal E}}(.)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ Rq_{{}_{*}}\left({\cal E}\ \overset{L}{\otimes}\ p^{{}^{*}}(.)\right)

(663)

The same object defines another functor

\Psi_{{}_{\cal E}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(Y)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)

(664)

by the similar formula

\Psi_{{}_{\cal E}}(.)\ \equiv\ Rp_{{}_{*}}\left({\cal E}\ \overset{L}{\otimes}\ q^{{}^{*}}(.)\right).

(665)

The functor
\Phi_{{}_{\cal E}}
admits left- and right-adjoint functors
\Phi_{{}_{\cal E}}^{{}^{*}}
and
\Phi_{{}_{\cal E}}^{{}^{!}}
, respectively, defined as follows:

\Phi^{{}^{*}}_{{}_{\cal E}}\ \simeq\ \Psi_{{}_{{\cal E}^{{}^{V}}\otimes\ q^{{}^{*}}K_{{}_{Y}}[m]}}\ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \Phi^{{}^{!}}_{{}_{\cal E}}\ \simeq\ \Psi_{{}_{{\cal E}^{{}^{V}}\otimes\ p^{{}^{*}}K_{{}_{X}}[m]}}

(666)

where
K_{{}_{X}}
and
K_{{}_{Y}}
are canonical sheaves on
X
and
Y
, respectively, and
{\cal E}^{{}^{V}}
denotes RHom
\left({\cal E},{\cal O}_{{}_{X\times Y}}\right)
.

Example 1

Let

f:\ X\longrightarrow\ Y

(667)

be a morphism of varieties.

\Gamma(f)
defines a subvariety of
X\times Y
that is a graph of
f
. It is clear that the functor

Rf_{{}_{*}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(Y)

(668)

is isomorphic to
\Phi_{{}_{{\cal O}_{{}_{\Gamma(f)}}}}
, where
{\cal O}_{{}_{\Gamma(f)}}
is the structure sheaf of the graph
\Gamma(f)
. In the same way, the functor

Lf^{{}^{*}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(Y)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)

(669)

is isomorphic to
\Psi_{{}_{{\cal O}_{{}_{\Gamma(f)}}}}
.

Example 2

Let

\delta:\ \Delta\ \hookrightarrow\ X\ \times\ X

(670)

be the diagonal embedding.
\Delta
is the graph of the identity morphism

\text{Id}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)

(671)

is represented by the structure sheaf
{\cal O}_{{}_{\Delta}}
. For any object
{\cal F}\in D^{{}^{b}}(X)
, the functor

\overset{L}{\bigotimes}\ {\cal F}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)

(672)

is isomorphic to
\Phi_{{}_{\delta_{{}_{*}}{\cal F}}}
.

There is a large class of exact functors between derived categories of smooth projective varieties consisting of functors admitting the form
\Phi_{{}_{\cal E}}
for some complex
{\cal E}
pn the product. This class is closed under the composition of functors. Let
X,Y,Z
be three smooth projective varieties, and let

\Phi_{{}_{I}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(Y)\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \Phi_{{}_{J}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(Y)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(Z)

(673)

be two functors, where
I
and
J
are objects of
D^{{}^{b}}(X\times Y)
and
D^{{}^{b}}(Y\times Z)
, respectively.

By further denoting with

p_{{}_{XY}}\ ,\ p_{{}_{YZ}}\ ,\ p_{{}_{XZ}}

(674)

the projections of
X\times Y\times Z
on the corresponding pair of factors.

Lemma

The composition

\Phi_{{}_{J}}\ \circ\ \Phi_{{}_{I}}\ \simeq\ \Phi_{{}_{K}}

(675)

K\ \simeq\ R_{{}_{p_{{}_{XZ*}}}}\left(p^{{}^{*}}_{{}_{YZ}}(J)\ \otimes\ p^{{}^{*}}_{{}_{XY}}(I)\right).

(676)

This is conjectured to encode all exact functors between
D^{{}^{b}}
Coh on smooth projective varieties.

Theorem

Let
X
and
Y
be smooth projective varieties. Suppose

F:\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)\ \xrightarrow{\ \ \ \sim\ \ \ }\ D^{{}^{b}}(Y)

(677)

is an exact equivalence. Then, there is a unique object (up t oisomorphisms)

{\cal E}\ \in\ D^{{}^{b}}(X\times Y)

(678)

such that the functors
R
and
\Phi_{{}_{\cal E}}
are isomorphic.

Defining the group of isomorphism classes of exact equivalences

F:\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)\ \xrightarrow{\ \ \ \sim\ \ \ }\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)

(679)

is called the group of autoequivalences of
D^{{}^{b}}(X)
, denoted by Auteq
\left(D^{{}^{b}}(X)\right)
.

Assume that
\mathbb{k}\equiv\mathbb{C}
. For every element

\xi\ \in\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(X\times Y,\mathbb{Q})

(680)

define the following maps

v_{{}_{\xi}}:\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(X,\mathbb{Q})\ \longrightarrow\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ w_{{}_{\xi}}:\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\ \longrightarrow\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(X,\mathbb{Q})

(681)

v_{{}_{\xi}}(.)\ =\ q_{{}_{*}}\left(\xi\cup p^{{}^{*}}(.)\right)\ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ w_{{}_{\xi}}(.)\ =\ p_{{}_{*}}\left(\xi\cup q^{{}^{*}}(.)\right).

(682)

From the K
\ddot{\text{u}}
nneth formula, it follows that any linear map between cohomologies has this form for some
\xi
. The composition formula for cohomological correspondence is well-known and similar to the composition of functors. Given three varieties,
X,Y,Z
,
\xi,\eta
elements of
H^{{}^{\bullet}}(X\times Y)
and

Lemma

The composition

(683)

v_{{}_{\zeta}}
, where

is given by the formula

\zeta\ =\ p_{{}_{XZ*}}\left(p^{{}^{*}}_{{}_{YZ}}(\eta)\ \cup\ p^{{}^{*}}_{{}_{XY}}(\xi)\right).

(684)

There is a natural correspondence that attaches to a functor

\Phi_{{}_{\xi}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(Y)

(685)

a linear map of vector spaces

\phi_{{}_{\cal E}}:\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(X,\mathbb{Q})\ \longrightarrow\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(Y,\mathbb{Q}).

(686)

Each exact functor

(689)

For every object

{\cal E}\ \in\ D^{{}^{b}}(X\ \times\ Y)

(690)

\displaystyle\phi_{{}_{\cal E}}

\displaystyle\overset{def.}{=}\ v_{{}_{\text{ch}({\cal E})\ \cup\ p^{{}^{*}}\left(\text{td}_{{}_{X}}\right)}}(.)

(691)

\displaystyle=\ q_{{}_{*}}\left(\text{ch}({\cal E})\ \cup\ p^{{}^{*}}\left(\text{td}_{{}_{X}}\right)\ \cup\ p^{{}^{*}}(.)\right)

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\cal E}}

\displaystyle\overset{def.}{=}\ w_{{}_{\text{ch}({\cal E})\ \cup\ q^{{}^{*}}\left(\text{td}_{{}_{X}}\right)}}(.)

(692)

\displaystyle=\ p_{{}_{*}}\left(\text{ch}({\cal E})\ \cup\ q^{{}^{*}}\left(\text{td}_{{}_{X}}\right)\ \cup\ q^{{}^{*}}(.)\right)

with td
{}_{{}_{X}}
, td

defining the Todd classes of

and

, respectively.

Lemma

For given
{\cal E}\ in\ D^{{}^{b}}(X\times Y)
, the following diagram is commutative

\displaystyle K(X)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \xrightarrow{\overline{\Phi}_{{}_{\cal E}}}\ \ \ \ \ \ K(Y)

(693)

\displaystyle\text{ch}\ \downarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \downarrow\text{ch}\

\displaystyle H^{{}^{\bullet}}(X,\mathbb{Q})\ \ \ \ \xrightarrow{\overline{\phi}_{{}_{\cal E}}}\ \ \ \ \ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(Y,\mathbb{Q})

Assume that a functor

(694)

(695)

for

(699)

Both Lemmas follow from the Riemann-Roch-Grothendieck Theorem.

Example 3

14.2
The G-construction

Given an abelian variety,
A
, of complex dimension
n
. The multiplication morphism,
9
,

m:\ A\ \times\ A\ \longrightarrow\ A

(729)

the multiplication morphism
\forall a\ \in\ A
, the translation morphism

T_{{}_{a}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ m(.,a):\ A\ \longrightarrow\ A.

(730)

Denoting by
\hat{A}
the dual abelian variety, which is canonically isomorphic to Pic
{}^{{}^{0}}(A)
, there is a unique line bundle,
P
, (the Poincare’ line bundle) on the product
A\times\hat{A}
such that, for every point
\alpha\in\hat{A}
, the restriction
P_{{}_{\alpha}}
on
A\times\{\alpha\}
represents an element of Pic
{}^{{}^{0}}(A)
, and the restriction
P\bigg|_{{}_{\{0\}\times\hat{A}}}
should be trivial. Notice that
A
can be identified with
\hat{\hat{A}}
.

Considering the projections

A\ \overset{p}{\longleftarrow}\ A\times\hat{A}\ \overset{q}{\longrightarrow}\ \hat{A}

(731)

and the functor

\Phi_{{}_{p}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(A)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}\left(\hat{A}\right)

(732)

\Rightarrow\ \ \ \ \Phi_{{}_{p}}(.)\ =\ Rq_{{}_{*}}\left(P\otimes p^{{}^{*}}(.)\right).

(733)

Proposition

Let
P
be a Poincare’ line bundle on
A\times\hat{A}
. Then, the functor

\Phi_{{}_{p}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(A)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}\left(\hat{A}\right)

(734)

is an exact equivalence, and there is an isomorphism of functors
