14.2.1
The Mukai-Polishchuk group and the Spinorial group

[Mukai] defined a discrete group
U(A\times\hat{A})
, as the group of autoequivalences
\text{Auteq}(D^{{}^{b}}(A))
an abelian variety
A
. [Polishchuk] considered the same group, SL
{}_{{}_{2}}(A)
, and proved that it acts naturally on
D^{{}^{b}}(A)
up to the shift functor. For the case of the complex tori,
A
, the Mukai-Polishchuk group,
U(A)
, reads

U(A)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left\{g=\left(\begin{matrix}&a\ \ b\\
&c\ \ d\\
\end{matrix}\right)\ \in\ \left(\begin{matrix}&\text{End}(A)\ \ \text{Hom}(\hat{A},A)\\
&\text{Hom}(\hat{A},A)\ \ \text{End}(\hat{A})\\
\end{matrix}\ \right)\bigg|\ g^{{}^{-1}}\ =\ \left(\begin{matrix}&a\ \ b\\
&c\ \ d\\
\end{matrix}\ \right)\right\}

(772)

where

U(A)\ \subseteq\ \text{GL}\left(\Gamma_{{}_{A}}\oplus\hat{\Gamma}_{{}_{A}}\right).

(773)

Consider the canonical bilinear form
Q
on

\Lambda\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \Gamma_{{}_{A}}\oplus\Gamma_{{}_{\hat{A}}}.

(774)

Then, we can equivalently describe the group
U(A)
as follows

U(A)\ =\ O(\Lambda,Q)\ \cap\ \text{Aut}(A\times\hat{A})\ =\ SO(\Lambda,Q)\ \cap\ \text{Aut}(A\times\hat{A})

(775)

of subgroups of GL
(\Lambda)
.

Proposition

The image of the homomorphism

\gamma_{{}_{A}}:\ \text{Auteq}\left(D^{{}^{b}}(A)\right)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Aut}(A\times\hat{A})

(776)

coincides with the Mukai-Polishchuk group
U(A)
. Moreover, there is an exact sequence of groups

0\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{Z}\times A\hat{A}\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Auteq}\left(D^{{}^{b}}(A)\right)\ \longrightarrow\ U(A)\ \longrightarrow\ 1.

(777)

The correspondence

\Phi_{{}_{\cal E}}\ \mapsto\ \phi_{{}_{\cal E}}

(778)

induces a homomorphism

\rho_{{}_{A}}:\ \text{Auteq}\left(D^{{}^{b}}(A)\right)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{GL}(H^{{}^{\bullet}}(A,\mathbb{Q}))

(779)

preserving the integral cohomology lattice.

Proposition

For any equivalence

\Phi_{{}_{\cal E}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(A)\ \xrightarrow{\ \ \sim\ \ }\ D^{{}^{b}}(B)

(780)

the linear map
\phi_{{}_{\cal E}}
preserves the integral cohomology, i.e.

\phi_{{}_{\cal E}}(H^{{}^{\bullet}}(A,\mathbb{Z}))\ =\ H^{{}^{\bullet}}(B,\mathbb{Z})

(781)

Lemma

For every line bundle,
L
, on an abelian variety,
A
, the Chern character ch
(L)
is integral, i.e. it belongs to
H^{{}^{\bullet}}(A,\mathbb{Q})
.

The, the correspondence

\Phi_{{}_{\cal E}}\ \mapsto\ \phi_{{}_{\cal E}}

(782)

gives the homomorphism

\rho_{{}_{A}}:\ \text{Auteq}(D^{{}^{b}}(A))\ \longrightarrow\ \text{GL}(H^{{}^{\bullet}}(A,\mathbb{Z})).

(783)

The subgroup of
\text{GL}(H^{{}^{\bullet}}(A,\mathbb{Z}))
that is the image of
\rho_{{}_{A}}
will bereferred to as the spinor group of
A
, Spin
(A)
. This follows from the fact that the Clifford algebra
Cl(\Lambda,Q)
and the algebra homomorphism

\text{cor}_{{}_{A}}:\ Cl(\Lambda,Q)\ \longrightarrow\ (H^{{}^{\bullet}}(A,\mathbb{Z}))\ \simeq\ (H^{{}^{\bullet}}(A\times A,\mathbb{Z}).

(784)

Define on
\Lambda\ \subset\ Cl(\Lambda,Q)
by

\text{cor}_{{}_{A}}((l,x))(.)\ =\ l(.)+x\cup(.)

(785)

(l,x)\ \in\ \Gamma_{{}_{A}}\oplus\Gamma_{{}_{A}}^{{}^{*}}\ =\ \Lambda

(786)

where
l(.)
denotes the convolution with
l

l(\alpha)\ =\ \sum(-1)^{{}^{i-1}}l(x_{{}_{i}})x_{{}_{1}}\cup...\cup x_{{}_{i-1}}\cup x_{{}_{i+1}}\cup...\cup x_{{}_{k}}

(787)

whereas cor
{}_{{}_{A}}
sends
Cl^{{}^{+}}(\Lambda,Q)
to
H^{{}^{ev}}(A\times A,\mathbb{Z})
and induces the action of the group Spin
(\Lambda,Q)
on the cohomology lattice
H^{{}^{\bullet}}(A,\mathbb{Z})
, implying that

\text{Spin}(\Lambda,Q)\ \subseteq\ \text{GL}(H^{{}^{\bullet}}(A,\mathbb{Z}))

(788)

w.r.t. cor
{}_{{}_{A}}
.

14.3
Mirror pair construction

Given an algebraic pair
(A,\omega_{{}_{A}})
, we can use the following two Lemmas:

Lemma 1

Let
(A,\omega_{{}_{A}})
be an algebraic pair, and
B
a complex torus. Let

\alpha:\ \Gamma_{{}_{A}}\ \oplus\ \Gamma_{{}_{\hat{A}}}\ \xrightarrow{\ \ \sim\ \ }\ \Gamma_{{}_{B}}\ \oplus\ \Gamma_{{}_{\hat{B}}}

(789)

be an isomorphism which identifies the forms
Q_{{}_{A}}
and
Q_{{}_{B}}
, and

\alpha_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}\circ I_{{}_{\omega_{{}_{A}}}}\ =\ J_{{}_{B\times\hat{B}}}\ \circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}

(790)

implying there exists
\omega_{{}_{B}}\ \in\ C_{{}_{B}}
such that

\alpha_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}\ \circ I_{{}_{\omega_{{}_{A}}}}\ =\ J_{{}_{B\times\hat{B}}}\ \circ\ \alpha_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}

(791)

\alpha
therefore established the mirror symmetry of algebraic pairs
(A,\omega_{{}_{A}}),(B,\omega_{{}_{B}})
.

Lemma 2

Let

\Lambda\ =\ \Gamma\ \oplus\ \Gamma^{{}^{*}}

(792)

be a lattice with canonical symmetric bilinear form
Q
. Let there be an isotropic decomposition

\Lambda\ =\ \Lambda_{{}_{1}}\ \oplus\ \Lambda_{{}_{2}}.

(793)

Suppose
I\ \in\ \text{GL}\left(\Lambda_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}\right)
is a complex structure on
\Lambda_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}
, i.e.
I^{{}^{2}}=-1
, such that

•

I(\Lambda_{{}_{i}},\mathbb{R})\ =\ \Lambda_{{}_{i,\mathbb{R}}},i=1,2

•

I\in O(\Lambda_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}},Q_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}})

Then, the complex tori

B_{{}_{1}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left(\Lambda_{{}_{1,\mathbb{R}}}/\Lambda_{{}_{1}},I_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ B_{{}_{2}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left(\Lambda_{{}_{2,\mathbb{R}}}/\Lambda_{{}_{2}},I_{{}_{2}}\right)

(794)

where
I_{{}_{i}}
denotes the restriction of
I
on
\Lambda_{{}_{i,\mathbb{R}}}
, and are dual to each other, namely

B_{{}_{2}}\ \simeq\ \hat{B}_{{}_{1}}.

(795)

Putting Lemma 1 and 2 together,

\Lambda\ =\ \Gamma_{{}_{A}}\ \oplus\ \Gamma_{{}_{\hat{A}}}

(796)

it suffices to find a
Q_{{}_{A}}
-isotropic decomposition

\Lambda\ =\ \Lambda_{{}_{1}}\ \oplus\ \Lambda_{{}_{2}}

(797)

such that
\Lambda_{{}_{1,\mathbb{R}}},\Lambda_{{}_{2,\mathbb{R}}}
are preserved by
I_{{}_{\omega_{{}_{A}}}}
. Then, by Lemma 2, the tori

B\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left(\Lambda_{{}_{1,\mathbb{R}}}/\Lambda_{{}_{1}},I_{{}_{\omega_{{}_{A}}}}\right)\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ B_{{}_{2}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left(\Lambda_{{}_{2,\mathbb{R}}}/\Lambda_{{}_{2}},I_{{}_{\omega_{{}_{A}}}}\right)

(798)

are dual to each other.

Moreover,

\alpha\ =\ \text{id}:\ \Gamma_{{}_{A}}\ \oplus\ \Gamma_{{}_{\hat{A}}}\ \xrightarrow{\ \ \sim\ \ }\ \Gamma_{{}_{B}}\ \oplus\ \Gamma_{{}_{\hat{B}}},

(799)

(
\Gamma_{{}_{B}}=\Lambda_{{}_{1}},\Gamma_{{}_{\hat{B}}}=\Lambda_{{}_{2}}
), identifying the forms
Q_{{}_{A}}
and
Q_{{}_{B}}
, thereby implying
17
17

17

By means of Lemma 1.
that there exists
\omega_{{}_{B}}\subset C_{{}_{B}}
such that
(A,\omega_{{}_{A}})
and
(B,\omega_{{}_{B}})
are mirror pairs.

Note that not every pair
(A,\omega_{{}_{A}})
admits a mirror pair. In particular, there might not be a mirror dual if the group
U_{{}_{A,\mathbb{Q}}}
is too big and
\omega_{{}_{A}}\ \in\ C_{{}_{A}}
is too general.

The G-construction

A weak pair
A_{{}_{J(q),\omega}})
is obtained by G-construction if the complex torus
A_{{}_{J(q)}}
is

\left(\Gamma_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}/\Gamma,J(q)\right)

(800)

where
\Gamma
satisfies a set of conditions, specified in what follows,

q\ \in\ S(C)\ \otimes\ C

(801)

and the form of
omega
belongs to

i_{{}_{S}}(S(D))\ \otimes\ \mathbb{C}.

(802)

A weak pair
(A,\omega_{{}_{A}})
is well-becoming iff it can be obtained by the G-construction.

Claim:
Any well-becoming pair has a mirror symmetric pair.

Properties that
\Gamma
should satisfy for a weak pair to be obtained by G-construction:

1.

\Gamma
is a lattice of even dimension, i.e.

\Gamma\ =\ \mathbb{Z}^{{}^{2n}}

(803)

is an abelian group.

2.

\Gamma
is a faithful module, namely

R\ \longrightarrow\ \text{End}_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}}}(\Gamma)

(804)

is an embedding.

3.

The order

O(\Gamma)\ =\ \{a\in D|a(\Gamma)\subset\Gamma\}

(805)

coincides with
R
.

4.

\Gamma
is a direct sum of
R
-modules
\Gamma_{-1}
and
\Gamma_{{}_{2}}
such that there is an isomorphism

e:\ M_{{}_{1}}\ \xrightarrow{\sim}\ M_{-2}

(806)

of D-modules

M_{{}_{i}}\ =\ \Gamma_{{}_{i}}\underset{\mathbb{Z}}{\otimes}\mathbb{Q}\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ i=1,2.

(807)

5.

There is a rational symmetric form

g:\ M\times M\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{Q}

(808)

such that the algebra
D
is invariant w.r.t. an anti-involution,
\sim
, on End
{}_{{}_{\mathbb{Q}}}(M)
, defined as follows

\tilde{\alpha}\ =\ g^{{}^{-1}}\alpha^{{}^{t}}g.

(809)

Let
\Gamma\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{{}^{2n}}
be a lattice,

V\ =\ \Gamma\ \otimes\ \mathbb{R}\ \simeq\ \mathbb{R}^{{}^{2n}}

(810)

and complex structure

J\ \in\ \text{End}(V)\ \ \ ,\ \ \ J^{{}^{2}}=-1.

(811)

This way, we obtain an n-dimensional complex torus

A\ =\ \left(V_{{}_{A}}/\Gamma_{{}_{A}},J_{{}_{A}}\right).

(812)

Consider the canonical isomorphisms

\Gamma_{{}_{A}}\ =\ H_{{}_{1}}(A,\mathbb{Z})\ \ \ ,\ \ \ V_{{}_{A}}\ =\ H_{{}_{1}}(A,\mathbb{R}).

(813)

Given another complex torus

B\ =\ \left(V_{{}_{B}}/\Gamma_{{}_{B}},J_{{}_{B}}\right),

(814)

the group Hom
(A,B)
consists of homorphisms

f:\ \Gamma_{{}_{A}}\ \longrightarrow\ \Gamma_{{}_{B}}

(815)

such that

J_{{}_{B}}\cdot f_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}\ =\ f_{{}_{\mathbb{R}}}\cdot J_{{}_{A}}:\ V_{{}_{A}}\ \longrightarrow\ V_{{}_{B}}

(816)

\Rightarrow\ \ \ \text{Hom}(A,B)\ \subset\ \text{Hom}\left(\Gamma_{{}_{A}},\Gamma_{{}_{B}}\right).

(817)

14.4
The Hodge group, Hdg
{}_{{}_{A,\mathbb{Q}}}

Let
A=(V/\Gamma,J)
be a complex torus. Consider the homomorphism of algebraic
\mathbb{R}
-groups

h_{{}_{A}}:\ S^{{}^{1}}\ \longrightarrow\ \text{GL}(V)\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ h_{{}_{A}}\left(e^{{}^{i\theta}}\right)\ =\ \cos\theta\mathbb{1}+\sin\theta J

(818)

such that

h_{{}_{A}}\left(e^{{}^{i\pi/2}}\right)\ =\ J.

(819)

This defines a representation of
S^{{}^{1}}
on the exterior algebra
\bigwedge H_{{}_{1}}(A,\mathbb{R})
, which is, in turn, inherited by the (co)-homology of
A
. Decomposing the representation of
S^{{}^{1}}
on the k
{}^{{}^{th}}
-cohomology space w.r.t. the characteristics of
S^{{}^{1}}

\displaystyle S^{{}^{1}}\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{C}^{{}^{*}}

(820)

\displaystyle e^{{}^{i\theta}}\ \mapsto\ e^{{}^{i(q-p)\theta}}\ \ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ p+q=k

the Hodge decomposition reads

H^{{}^{k}}(A,\mathbb{C})\ =\ \bigoplus_{{}_{p+q=k}}H^{{}^{p,q}}(A,\mathbb{C}).

(821)

The Hodge group (a.k.a. the special Mumford-Tate group), Hdg
{}_{{}_{A,\mathbb{Q}}}
of
A
is defined as a minimal algebraic
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup
G
of GL
(H_{{}_{1}}(A,\mathbb{Q}))
, such that

h_{{}_{A}}\left(S^{{}^{1}}\right)\ \subset\ G(\mathbb{R}).

(822)

The group Hdg
{}_{{}_{A,\mathbb{Q}}}
acts naturally on
H_{{}_{k}}(A,\mathbb{Q})
, and trivially on
H^{{}^{p,p}}(A,\mathbb{Q})
.

Considering the dual torus,
\hat{A}
,

\text{Hdg}_{{}_{A,\mathbb{Q}}}\ =\ \text{Hdg}_{{}_{\hat{A},\mathbb{Q}}}\ =\ \text{Hdg}_{{}_{A\times\hat{A},\mathbb{Q}}}

(823)

14.5
Comparison with Kontsevich and SYZ

14.5.1
Comparison with Kontsevich

Assume that the algebraic pairs
\left(A,\omega_{{}_{A}}\right)
and
\left(B,\omega_{{}_{B}}\right)
are mirror symmetric. Let

\omega_{{}_{A}}\ =\ \eta_{{}_{1}}+i\eta_{{}_{2}}\ \in\ C_{{}_{A}}

(824)

Then,
\eta_{{}_{2}}
is a K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler form. The Hodge group
\text{Hdg}_{{}_{\hat{A},\mathbb{Q}}}
acts on
H_{{}_{1}}(A)
preserving the form
\eta_{{}_{2}}
.

\text{Hdg}_{{}_{A}}(\mathbb{Z})\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left\{g\in\text{Hdg}_{{}_{A,\mathbb{Q}}}\bigg|\ g\left(\Lambda_{{}_{A}}\right)\ \subset\ \Lambda_{{}_{A}}\right\}

(825)

acting by symplectomorphisms on the symplectic manifold
\left(A,\eta_{{}_{2}}\right)
, implying

\text{Hdg}_{{}_{A,\mathbb{Q}}}\ \subset\ \text{Auteq}F\left(A,\eta_{{}_{2}}\right).

(826)

But

\text{Hdg}_{{}_{A}}(\mathbb{Z})\ \subset\ U(B),

(827)

with
U(B)
denoting the group encoding the essential part of Auteq
D^{{}^{b}}(B)
, implying the original categorical equivalence by Kontsevich

F\left(A,\eta_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \simeq\ D^{{}^{b}}(B)

(828)

cannot be recovered.

14.5.2
Comparison with SYZ

Assume that the pair
\left(A,\omega_{{}_{A}}\right)
is obtained by the
G
-construction. Then, there is a real subtorus

T\ \subset\ A\times B\ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ n\ =\ \text{dim}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}A\ =\ \text{dim}_{{}_{\mathbb{C}}}B

(829)

of dimension
3n
, such that:

1.

The fibers of the projection of
T
onto
B
are special Lagrangian subtori of
A
of dimension
n
.

2.

The existence of such a correspondence is part of the geometric mirror symmetry by [Morrison].

3.

There is a natural complex line bundle,
L
on
T
such that its Chern class,
c_{{}_{1}}(L)
considered as an element in

H^{{}^{\bullet}}(A\times B)\ \simeq\ \text{Hom}\left(H^{{}^{\bullet}}(A),H^{{}^{\bullet}}(B)\right)

(830)

is the aforementioned isomorphism

\beta:\ H^{{}^{1}}(A,\mathbb{Z})\ \xrightarrow{\ \ \sim\ \ }\ H^{{}^{0}}(B,\mathbb{Z})\oplus H^{{}^{2}}(B,\mathbb{Z})

(831)

\beta:\ H^{{}^{0}}(A,\mathbb{Z})\oplus H^{{}^{2}}(A,\mathbb{Z})\ \xrightarrow{\ \ \sim\ \ }\ H^{{}^{1}}(B,\mathbb{Z}).

(832)

15
Comparison of mirror functors

Two different kinds of HMS for the elliptic curve,
10
:

1.

Categorical MS of POlishchuk-Zaslow, comparing
D^{{}^{b}}\text{Coh}(X)
and
D^{{}^{b}}F\left(T^{{}^{2}}\right)
, with
E
denoting the elliptic curve and
T^{{}^{2}}
the symplectic torus, respectively.

2.

Cho-Hong-Lau compared the symplectic torus
T^{{}^{2}}
(with
\mathbb{Z}/3
-symmetry), and its
\mathbb{Z}/3
-quotient orbifold,
\mathbb{P}_{{}_{3,3,3}}^{{}^{1}}
. The immersed Lagrangian in the orbifold, a.k.a. the Seidel Lagrangian, defines a LG mirror
\left(\Lambda^{{}^{3}},{\cal W}\right)
with an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-functor from the Fukaya category of
\mathbb{P}_{{}_{3,3,3}}^{{}^{1}}
to thedg-category of Matrix Factorisations.

To recover MS for the original symplectic torus,
T^{{}^{2}}
, one needs to take the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded version of this functor from the graded Fukaya category of the
T^{{}^{2}}
to the graded MF of MF
\mathbb{Z}({\cal W})
, implying there are 2 different ways of constructing the B-model of HMS of elliptic curves, namely as
D^{{}^{b}}
and as a MF.

BY Orlov’s Theorem, these two categories are equivalent. SPecifically, if
{\cal W}
is a (quasi)-homogeneous polynomial defining a smooth projective CY hypersurface
X
, then,

D^{{}^{b}}\text{Coh}(X)\ \simeq\ \text{HMF}_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}}}({\cal W})

(833)

also referred to as the LG/CY correspondence.

We now turn to explaining how to relate these exact functors between triangulated categories to each other.

Theorem
[Sangwook Lee]

There is a commutative diagram of exact functors

\displaystyle D^{{}^{\pi}}F\left(T^{{}^{2}}\right)\ \xrightarrow{S_{{}_{i}}}\ D^{{}^{\pi}}F\left(T^{{}^{2}}\right)

(834)

\displaystyle\Phi\ \downarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \downarrow\ {\cal LM}^{{}^{\mathbb{L}}}

\displaystyle D^{{}^{b}}\text{Coh}(X)\ \xrightarrow{{\cal G}_{{}_{i}}}\ \text{HMF}_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}}}({\cal W})

where
\Phi
is the mirror functor, and

S_{{}_{i}}\ =\ [-j]\circ\ \tau^{{}^{d}}\circ t_{{}_{(0,\frac{1}{2})}}\circ\left(\begin{matrix}&1\ \ 0\\
&-3i+2\ \ 1\\
\end{matrix}\right)

(835)

where

j=\lfloor-\frac{i}{3}\rfloor\ \ \ ,\ \ \ d=-i-3j,

(836)

\tau
denotes a rotation by
-\frac{2\pi}{3}
, whereas
t_{{}_{(0,\frac{1}{2})}}
denotes parallel transport by
\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
.

{\cal LM}^{{}^{\mathbb{L}}}
requires the choice of a character

\gamma:\ \mathbb{Z}/3\ \longrightarrow\ U(1)

(837)

Varying the choice of
\gamma
, the functors
S_{{}_{i}}
may also vary. The commuting diagram aboveassumes a choice has been made. Essentially, this Theorem states that two HMSs are equivalent after suitable geometric transformation
S_{{}_{i}}
and shift.

15.1
Graded Matrix Factorisation

Recap from the case of
\mathbb{CP}^{{}^{1}}
.. Let

R\ =\ \Lambda\left[x_{{}_{o}},...,x_{{}_{n}}\right]

(838)

be a graded ring with

\text{deg}(x_{{}_{i}})\ =\ d_{{}_{i}}.

(839)

Let
{\cal W}\ \in\ R
be a (quasi)-homogeneous polynomial of degree
d
. MF
{}_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}}}({\cal W})
is a dg-category whose object
(P,d_{{}_{P}})
is represented as a pair of global morphisms

p_{{}_{o}}:\ P_{{}_{0}}\ \longrightarrow\ P_{{}_{1}}\ \ \ \ \ \ p_{{}_{1}}:\ P_{{}_{1}}\ \longrightarrow\ P_{{}_{o}}(d),

(840)

with
P_{{}_{0},}P_{{}_{1}}
graded free
R
-modules, and

\displaystyle p_{{}_{0}}(d)\circ p_{{}_{1}}\ =\ {\cal W}\circ\text{id}\ :\ P_{{}_{1}}\ \longrightarrow\ P_{{}_{1}}(d)

(841)

\displaystyle p_{{}_{1}}\circ p_{{}_{0}}\ =\ {\cal W}\ \circ\ \text{id}\ :\ P_{{}_{0}}\ \longrightarrow\ P_{{}_{0}}(d).

Equivalently, one such object can also be expressed as a quasi-periodic
\infty
-sequence:

K^{{}^{\bullet}}:...\ \longrightarrow\ K^{{}^{i}}\ \xrightarrow{k^{{}^{i}}}\ K^{{}^{i+1}}\ \xrightarrow{k^{{}^{i+1}}}\ K^{{}^{i+2}}\ ...

(842)

where

K^{{}^{2i+1}}\ =\ P_{{}_{1}}(i\cdot d)\ \ ,\ \ K^{{}^{2i}}\ =\ P_{{}_{0}}(i\cdot d)

(843)

k^{{}^{2i}}\ =\ p_{{}_{0}}(i\cdot d)\ \ ,\ \ k^{{}^{2i+1}}\ =\ p_{{}_{1}}(i\cdot d).

(844)

Then,

\text{Hom}_{{}_{\text{MF}_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}(\cal W)}}}}^{{}^{i}}\left(K^{{}^{\bullet}},L^{{}^{\bullet}}\right)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left\{f^{{}^{\bullet}}:K^{{}^{\bullet}}\ \longrightarrow\ L^{{}^{\bullet+j}},\text{graded}\ \ |f^{{}^{i+2}}\ =\ f^{{}^{i}}(d)\right\}

(845)

and

\text{d}:\ \text{Hom}_{{}_{\text{MF}_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}(\cal W)}}}}^{{}^{j}}\left(K^{{}^{\bullet}},L^{{}^{\bullet}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Hom}_{{}_{\text{MF}_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}(\cal W)}}}}^{{}^{j+1}}\left(K^{{}^{\bullet}},L^{{}^{\bullet}}\right)

(846)

is defined by

(d(f^{{}^{\bullet}}))^{{}^{i}}\ \equiv\ l^{{}^{i+j}}\circ f^{{}^{i}}+(-1)^{{}^{j}}f^{{}^{i+1}}\circ k^{{}^{i}}

(847)

where

l^{{}^{n}}:\ L^{{}^{n}}\ \longrightarrow\ L^{{}^{n+1}}.

(848)

Given a MF,
K^{{}^{\bullet}}
, it is natural to define the shift,
K^{{}^{\bullet}}[1]
such that

K^{{}^{\bullet}}[1]^{{}^{i}}\ =\ K^{{}^{\bullet\ i+1}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ k^{{}^{\bullet}}[1]^{{}^{i}}\ =\ -k^{{}^{\bullet\ i+1}}.

(849)

Given a dg-category,
{\cal C}
, its cohomology category,
H^{{}^{0}}({\cal C})
, is defined by the same objects as those of
{\cal C}
, and morphism spaces are the 0
th
-cohomologies of

\text{d}:\ \text{Hom}^{{}^{j}}\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Hom}^{{}^{j+1}}.

(850)

Proposition

The cohomology category
H^{{}^{0}}\left(\text{MF}_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}}}({\cal W})\right)
is a triangulated category with exact triangles

K^{{}^{\bullet}}\ \xrightarrow{f}\ L^{{}^{\bullet}}\ \longrightarrow\ C^{{}^{\bullet}}(f)\ \longrightarrow\ K^{{}^{\bullet}}[1],

(851)

where the mapping cone of
f
is defiend as follows

C^{{}^{\bullet}}(f):\ ...\ \longrightarrow\ L^{{}^{i}}\oplus K^{{}^{i+1}}\ \xrightarrow{c^{{}^{i}}}\ L^{{}^{i+1}}\oplus K^{{}^{i+2}}\\
\xrightarrow{c^{{}^{i+1}}}\ L^{{}^{i+2}}\oplus K^{{}^{i+3}}

(852)

such that

c^{{}^{i}}\ =\ \left(\begin{matrix}&l^{{}^{i}}\ \ \ \ \ \ f^{{}^{i+1}}\\
&0\ \ -k^{{}^{i+1}}\end{matrix}\right).

(853)

The category of graded MFs of
{\cal W}
is tehreforedefiend as follows

\text{HMF}_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}}}({\cal W})\ \overset{def.}{=}\ H^{{}^{0}}\left(\text{MF}_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}}}({\cal W})\right).

(854)

Take
A=R/{\cal W}
. Given that
{\cal W}
is homogeneous, then
A
is a graded ring. Then, the category gr-
A
of finitely-generated graded
A
-modules is an abelian category. Defining Perf
-A
as the full subcategory of chain complexes of
A
-modules, then

Lemma

\text{HMF}_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}}}({\cal W})\ \simeq\ D_{{}_{sg}}^{{}^{gr}}(A)\ \simeq\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A)/\text{Perf}-A\ \ \ \ \ \ .

(855)

15.2
The LG/CY correspondence

Between
\text{HMF}_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}}}({\cal W})
and
D^{{}^{b}}\text{Coh}(X)
.

The idea of this correspondence comes from the fact that the two categories are both Verdier quotients of
D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A)
.

Let tors-
A
be the subcategory of gr-
A
of torsion modules that are finite-dimensional over
\Lambda
. From Serre’s Theorem,

D^{{}^{b}}\text{Coh}(X)\ \simeq\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{qgr}-A)\ \equiv\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A)/D^{{}^{b}}(\text{tors}-A)

(856)

\text{HMF}_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}}}({\cal W})\ \simeq\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A)/\text{Perf}-A\ \ \ \ \ .

(857)

If the quotient functors

\displaystyle\pi:\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{qgr}-A)

(858)

\displaystyle q:\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{gr}}_{{}_{sg}}(A)

had adjoint functors, then we could lift objects and morphisms in a quotient category to those in
D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A)
, and project them to the other quotient, thereby obtaining functors between two quotient categories. However,
q
does not admit ant adjoint functor, whereas
\pi
admits a right-adjoint. Considering the restrictions:

\displaystyle\pi_{{}_{i}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A_{{}_{\geq i}})\ \hookrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{qgr}-A)

(859)

\displaystyle q_{{}_{i}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A_{{}_{\geq i}})\ \hookrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{gr}}_{{}_{sg}}(A)\ \ \ \ \ \ .

Here,
\text{gr}-A_{{}_{\geq i}}
denote modules,
M
, such that

M_{{}_{p}}\ =\ 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for}\ \ \ \ p<i\ \ \ \ \ \ \ .

(860)

Now,
\pi_{{}_{i}}
admits right-adjoints, whereas
q_{{}_{i}}
admits left-adjoints.

Lemma

Defining

\mathbb{R}\omega_{{}_{i}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{qgr}-A)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A_{{}_{\geq i}})

(861)

by

\mathbb{R}\omega_{{}_{i}}(M)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \bigoplus_{{}_{k=1}}^{{}^{\infty}}\ \mathbb{R}\text{Hom}_{{}_{D^{{}^{b}}(\text{qgr}-A)}}(\pi A,M(k)).

(862)

Then,
\mathbb{R}\omega_{{}_{i}}
is fully-faithful, and
\mathbb{R}\omega_{{}_{i}}
is the right-adjoint of
\pi_{{}_{i}}
. All cohomologies
\mathbb{R}^{{}^{j}}\omega_{{}_{i}}(M)
are contained in tors-
A
for
j>0
.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\omega_{{}_{i}}(M)\ :\ D^{{}^{b}}\text{Coh}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A_{{}_{\geq i}})

(863)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\omega_{{}_{i}}(M)({\cal E})\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \bigoplus_{{}_{k=1}}^{{}^{\infty}}\ \mathbb{R}\text{Hom}_{{}_{D^{{}^{b}}\text{Coh}(X)}}({\cal O}_{{}_{X}},{\cal E}(k))\ \ \ \ \ \ .

A Gorenstein algebra,
A
, has finite injective dimension,
n
. If

D(K)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \mathbb{R}\text{Hom}_{{}_{A}}(K,A),

(864)

where

K\ \simeq\ A/(x_{{}_{o}},...,x_{{}_{n}}),

(865)

then, it is isomorphic to

K(a)[-n]

(866)

for some integer,
a
, called the Gorenstein parameter. If

A\ =\ R/{\cal W}

(867)

and
{\cal W}
defines a CY, then
a=0
. =

For later purposes, we now introduce the notion of
semiorthogonal decomposition
. Given a triangulated category,
{\cal C}
,

{\cal C}\ =\ <{\cal A},{\cal B}>

(868)

is a semi-orthogonal decomposition if:

1.

{\cal A,B}
are full triangulated subcategories

2.

\forall C\ \in\text{Ob}({\cal C})
, there is an exact triangle

\displaystyle A\ \xrightarrow{[1]}\ B

(869)

\displaystyle C

for some
A\ \in\text{Ob}({\cal A}),B\ \in\text{Ob}({\cal B})
. In such case,
A,B
are referred to as orthoginal projections of
C
onto
{\cal A}
and
{\cal B}
, respectively.

3.

\text{Hom}_{{}_{\cal C}}({\cal B},{\cal A})\ =\ 0\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \forall B\ \in\text{Ob}({\cal B}),A\ \in\text{Ob}({\cal A})\ \ \ \ \ .

(870)

Lemma

Let
S_{{}_{\geq i}}
be the triangular subcategory generated by
K(e)
, with
e\leq-i
, and
{\cal P}_{{}_{\geq i}}
the triangulated subcategory generated by
A(e)
,
e\leq-i
. Then,
\forall i\in\mathbb{Z}
, there are semi-orthogonal decompositions

D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A_{{}_{\geq i}})\ =\ <D_{{}_{i}},S_{{}_{\geq i}}>\ =\ <{\cal P}_{{}_{\geq i}},{\cal T}_{{}_{i}}>

(871)

where
D_{{}_{i}}
is equivalent to
D^{{}^{b}}(\text{qgr}-A_{{}_{\geq i}})
under the function
\mathbb{R}\omega_{{}_{i}}
, and
{\cal T}_{{}_{i}}
is equivalent to
D_{{}_{sg}}^{{}^{gr}}(A)
.

Main Theorem
[Orlov]

Let
a
be the Gorenstein parameter of
A
. Then,
D_{{}_{sg}}^{{}^{gr}}(A)
and
D^{{}^{b}}(\text{qgr}-A)
are related as follows:

1.

If
a>0
,
\forall i\in\mathbb{Z}
fully-faithful functors

\Phi_{{}_{i}}:\ D_{{}_{sg}}^{{}^{gr}}(A)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{qgr}-A)

(872)

and semi-orthogonal decomposition

D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A)\ =\ \left<\pi(A(-i-a+1),...,\pi A(-i),\Phi_{{}_{i}}D_{{}_{sg}}^{{}^{gr}}(A)\right>

(873)

where

\pi:\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{qgr}-A)

(874)

is the natural projection, and

\Phi_{{}_{i}}:\ D_{{}_{sg}}^{{}^{gr}}(A)\ \simeq\ {\cal T}_{{}_{i}}\ \hookrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A)\ \longrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{qgr}-A)\ \ \ \ .

(875)

2.

If
a<0,\forall i\in\mathbb{Z}
there are fully-faithful functors

{\cal G}_{{}_{i}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{qgr}-A)\ \longrightarrow\ D_{{}_{sg}}^{{}^{gr}}(A)

(876)

and semi-orthogonal decomposition

D_{{}_{sg}}^{{}^{gr}}(A)\ =\ \left<qk(-i),...,qk(-i+a+1),{\cal G}_{{}_{i}}D^{{}^{b}}(\text{qgr}-A)\right>,

(877)

with

q:\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A)\ \longrightarrow\ D_{{}_{sg}}^{{}^{gr}}(A)

(878)

is the natural projection, and

{\cal G}_{{}_{i}}:\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{qgr}-A)\ \simeq\ D_{{}_{i-a}}\ \hookrightarrow\ D^{{}^{b}}(\text{gr}-A)\ \xrightarrow{q}\ D_{{}_{sg}}^{{}^{gr}}(A)\ \ \ \ .

(879)

3.

If
a=0
,
D_{{}_{sg}}^{{}^{gr}}(A)
and
D^{{}^{b}}(\text{qgr}-A)
are equivalent under
\Phi_{{}_{i}}
combined with
{\cal G}_{{}_{i}}
.

For the case in which
X=\text{Proj}(A)
is a CY variety,
a=0
, implying

D^{{}^{b}}(\text{qgr}-A)\ \simeq\ D^{{}^{b}}\text{Coh}(X)

(880)

and

D_{{}_{sg}}^{{}^{gr}}(A)\ \simeq\ \text{HMF}_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}}}({\cal W})

(881)

are equivalent.

15.3
Polishchuk-Zaslow Mirror Symmetry: a brief recap

Given
T^{{}^{2}}
a symplectic torus,

T^{{}^{2}}\ \equiv\ \mathbb{C}/\left(\mathbb{Z}\oplus e^{{}^{2\pi i/3}}\mathbb{Z}\right).

(882)

Let
\alpha
be its symplectic area, and
q=T^{{}^{\alpha}}
in
\Lambda
.

Any unobstructed Lagrangian circle,
L
, is described by a straight line

(c,0)+t\vec{v}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ t\in\mathbb{R}\vec{v}\ =\ a+e^{{}^{2\pi i/3}}b,\ \ \ \ a,b\in\mathbb{Z},c\in[0,1).

(883)

L\ =\ L_{{}_{(a,b),c}}

(884)

Then, the categorical mirror symmetry formulation of [PL] is succinctly expressed in the following

Theorem

{\cal F}:\ D^{{}^{b}}\text{Coh}(X)\ \simeq\ D^{{}^{\pi}}F(E)

(885)

such that

\Phi\left({\cal O}_{{}_{X}}(np_{{}_{o}})\right)\ =\ L_{{}_{(1,-n)}}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \Phi\left({\cal O}_{{}_{X}}(p_{{}_{o}})\right)\ =\ L_{{}_{(0,-1),\frac{1}{2}}}\ \ \ \ ,

(886)

where
X
is a mirror elliptic curve, and
p_{{}_{o}}
is a point in
X
. The construction of
{\cal F}
follows from the identification of theta identities and morphisms between Lagrangians.

15.4
Localised mirror functors

Two versions:

1.

Ungraded.

2.

Graded.

Take
T^{{}^{2}}
equipped with
\mathbb{Z}/3
-action. A parallelogram whose sides are solid lines is a fundamental domain of
T^{{}^{2}}
. Let
\Omega=dz
be the holomorphic volume form on
T^{{}^{2}}
, with
z
being the complex coordinate of
\mathbb{C}
. The grading structure on
E
is not preserved under the
\mathbb{Z}/3
-action, thereby implying such action does not induce an action on the graded Fukaya category either, but there is an action up to shifts, i.e. a homeomorphism

\mathbb{Z}/3\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Auteq}\left(D^{{}^{\pi}}F(T^{{}^{2}})\right)/\mathbb{Z},

(887)

with
\mathbb{Z}
denoting the shift functor. For example, an oriented Lagrangian,
L
, is a line defined as follows

\frac{1+\xi}{2}+\sqrt{-1}\ t.

(888)

Constructing an immersed Lagrangian

\mathbb{L}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ L\ \cup\ \tau(L)\ \cup\ \tau^{{}^{2}}(L),

(889)

equipping each constituent with a pin structure. The phase of
\tau(L)
is
\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{2\pi}{3}
, and that of
\tau^{{}^{2}}(L)
is
\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{4\pi}{3}
, i.e.
\tau
is a rotation by
-\frac{2\pi}{3}
.

The union of 3 dashed lines (add picture here) is
\mathbb{L}
.
e_{{}_{1}},e_{{}_{2}},e_{{}_{3}}
represents fundamental classes of
L,\tau(L),\tau^{{}^{2}}(L)
., respectively, and

e\ =\ e_{{}_{1}}+e_{{}_{2}}+e_{{}_{3}}

(890)

is the unit in
\mathbb{L}
.

15.4.1
Ungraded localised mirror functor

Theorem
[Cho-Hong-Lau], 2015

Let
x,\ y,\ z
be immersed generators of
\overline{\mathbb{L}}\subset T^{{}^{2}}/(\mathbb{Z}/3)
,
X_{{}_{i}},Y_{{}_{i}},Z_{{}_{i}},i=1,2,3
be their lifting. Then,

b\ =\ x\left(X_{{}_{1}}+X_{{}_{2}}+X_{{}_{3}}\right)+y\left(Y_{{}_{1}}+Y_{{}_{2}}+Y_{{}_{3}}\right)+z\left(Z_{{}_{1}}+Z_{{}_{2}}+Z_{{}_{3}}\right)\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \forall x,\ y,\ z\ \in\ \Lambda

(891)

is a weak bounding cochain of
\mathbb{L}
.

Let

{\cal W}\ =\ \phi(x^{{}^{3}}-y^{{}^{3}}+z^{{}^{3}})-\psi xyz\ \equiv\ {\cal W}(\mathbb{L},b)

(892)

where
\phi,\psi
are power series of
q_{{}_{\alpha}}=T^{{}^{\Delta_{{}_{xyz}}}}
.

Suppose
L^{{}^{\prime}}
is an unobstructed Lagrangian submanifold.
CF((\mathbb{L},b),L^{{}^{\prime}}))
is generated by even and odd intersections. The number of strips lying between even and odd generators,
\mu_{{}_{1}}
, are such that

\mu_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{2}}\ =\ {\cal W}\ \cdot\ \text{id}_{{}_{n\times n}},

(893)

with
n
denoting the number of even and odd generators. The resulting matrix factorisation therefore reads

\text{MF}({\cal W})\ =\ \left(CF((\mathbb{L},b),\ \mu_{{}_{1}}),L^{{}^{\prime}}\right),

(894)

where

\text{rank}(\text{MF}({\cal W}))\ =\ n.

(895)

Theorem
[Cho-Hong-Lan], 2015

Let
{\cal C}
be an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-category, and
(A,b)
a weakly-unobstructed object with weak bounding cochain
b
. Let
{\cal C}_{{}_{o}}
be the
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-subcategory of unobstructed objects. Define a collection of maps

\left\{{\cal LM}_{{}_{\bullet}}^{{}^{(A,b)}}\right\}

(896)

with
{\cal C}_{{}_{o}}
as source category, such that:

•

{\cal LM}_{{}_{0}}^{{}^{(A,b)}}
sends an object
B
to

\text{MF}\ =\ \left(\text{Hom}((A,b),B),\mu_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \ \ \ .

(897)

•

{\cal LM}_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{(A,b)}}(x)
is defined as follows

{\cal LM}_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{(A,b)}}(x)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ (-1)^{{}^{\bullet}}\mu_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{b,0}}(.,x):\ \left(\text{Hom}((A,b),B),\mu_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ \left(\text{Hom}((A,b),B),\mu_{{}_{1}}\right)\ \ \ \ ,

(898)

with

x\ \in\ \text{Hom}\left(B_{{}_{1}},B_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \ \ .

(899)

•

{\cal LM}_{{}_{k}}^{{}^{(A,b)}}(x_{{}_{1}},...,x_{{}_{k}})\ \overset{def.}{=}\ (-1)^{{}^{\bullet}}\mu_{{}_{k+1}}^{{}^{b,0,...,0}}(y,x_{{}_{1}},...,x_{{}_{k}})

(900)

Then,

\left\{{\cal LM}_{{}_{\bullet}}^{{}^{(A,b)}}\right\}:\ {\cal C}_{{}_{o}}\ \longrightarrow\ \text{MF}({\cal W}(a,b))

(901)

is an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-functor where MF is the differential
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded category of MFs of
{\cal W}(A,b)
. (
901
) is therefore referred to as the
ungraded local
mirror functor.

15.4.2
Graded localised mirror functor

Adding a cyclic group action,

(M,\omega,\Omega)

(902)

defining a Cy manifold, with
\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z}
. Consider a
\frac{1}{d}
-grading on an immersed Lagrangian, i.e. defining a phase function

\theta_{{}_{L}}^{{}^{1/d}}:\ L\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{R}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{s.t.}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \Omega^{{}^{\otimes d}}(T_{{}_{p}}L)\ =\ e^{{}^{\pi i\theta_{{}_{L}}^{{}^{1/d}}(p)}}\ \ \ .

(903)

If such a phase function exists, then
L
is said to be
\frac{1}{d}
-graded. If the phase is constant, it is special Lagrangian.

Proposition

On the full
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-subcategory of
\frac{1}{d}
-graded Lagrangians, the
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-multiplication has degree
2-k
under
\frac{1}{d}
-grading.

The degree of a Lagrangian intersection,
p
, between
\frac{1}{d}
-graded Lagrangians is defined as follows

\text{deg}^{{}^{1/d}}(p)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \frac{1}{d}\left(\theta_{{}_{L_{{}_{2}}}}^{{}^{1/d}}(p)-\theta_{{}_{L_{{}_{1}}}}^{{}^{1/d}}(p)+\theta^{{}^{d}}\left(\hat{L_{{}_{1}}L_{{}_{2}}}\right)\bigg|_{{}_{p}}\right)\ \in\ \frac{1}{d}\mathbb{Z}

(904)

where
p
is considered as a morphism

p:\ L_{{}_{1}}\ \longrightarrow\ L_{{}_{2}}

(905)

and

\pi\theta^{{}^{d}}\left(\hat{L_{{}_{1}}L_{{}_{2}}}\right)\bigg|_{{}_{p}}

(906)

is the phase angle of the positive-definite path from
TL_{{}_{2}}
to
TL_{{}_{1}}
measured by
\Omega^{{}^{\otimes d}}(p)
.

Going back to the example of
T^{{}^{2}}
with
\mathbb{Z}/3
-action, the Seidel Lagrangian,
\mathbb{L}
can be turned into a special Lagrangian w.r.t.
\frac{1}{3}
-grading by defining a possible phase as

\theta_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}^{{}^{1/3}}\ =\ -\frac{1}{2}\ \ \ .

(907)

Then, each immersed point that is a morphism between any of
L,\tau(L),\tau^{{}^{2}}(L)
, has degree
\frac{1}{3}
.

Upon further fixing a character

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}/3\ \longrightarrow\ U(1)

(908)

\displaystyle-j\ \ \mapsto\ \ e^{{}^{-\pi i2j/3}},

we can define a pair

\left(\bigoplus_{{}_{p_{{}_{i}}\in CF((\mathbb{L},b),L)}}A_{{}_{g_{{}_{i}}}}[\text{deg}p_{{}_{i}}],\ \ \mu_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{(\mathbb{L},b),L}}\right)\ \ \ ,

(909)

where

p_{{}_{i}}\ \in\ L^{{}^{g_{{}_{i}}}}\ \cap\ L^{{}^{\prime}}

(910)

and deg
p_{{}_{i}}
denotes the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading by
\Omega
.

Theorem

The category whose ojects are of the form (
909
) is equivalent to the category of graded matrix factorisations of
{\cal W}
under the following correspondence

\left(\bigoplus_{{}_{i}}A_{{}_{g_{{}_{i}}}}[k_{{}_{i}}],\ \ \delta\right)\ \ \ \ \mapsto\ \ \ \left(...\ \rightarrow\ E_{{}_{0}}\ \rightarrow\ E_{{}_{1}}\ \rightarrow\ ...\right)\ \ \ ,

(911)

where

E_{{}_{0}}\ =\ \bigoplus_{{}_{K_{{}_{i}}:\text{even}}}\ R\left(-\frac{k_{{}_{i}}d}{2}-\frac{d}{2}\alpha_{{}_{g_{{}_{i}}}}\right)

(912)

E_{{}_{1}}\ =\ \bigoplus_{{}_{K_{{}_{i}}:\text{odd}}}\ R\left(-\frac{(1-k_{{}_{i}})d}{2}-\frac{d}{2}\alpha_{{}_{g_{{}_{i}}}}\right)

(913)

and structure maps

p_{{}_{i}}:\ E_{{}_{i}}\ \longrightarrow\ E_{{}_{i+1}}.

(914)

Proposition

The collection of maps

\left\{{\cal F}_{{}_{bullet}}^{{}^{\mathbb{L},b}}\right\}

(915)

becomes an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-functor,

{\cal LM}^{{}^{\mathbb{L,b}}}_{{}_{\text{gr}}}:\ \ \ Fu^{{}^{\mathbb{Z}}}(T^{{}^{2}})\ \longrightarrow\ TW_{{}_{\mathbb{Z}}}\left(R_{{}_{\cal W}}\mathbb{Z}/3\right),

(916)

called the
graded localised
mirror functor.

Importantly, this Proposition, has been shown to hold in more general setups, involving noncommutative Matrix Factorisations.

16
Noncommutative HMS for elliptic curves

Main techniques:

1.

HMS

2.

SYZ

3.

Kontsevich-Soibleman/Gross-Seibert

4.

CHL a formulation using weakly-unobstructed deformations of a Lagrangian immersion to construct the mirror geometry, which is a LG model. This is particularly important when the Lagrangian fibration is not known, or is too complicated in parctice. This way, we can always construct a natural functor from the Fukaya category t othe mirror B-model (namely MF for a LG model). The Theory can be applied to the orbifold
\mathbb{P}_{{}_{a,b,c}}^{{}^{1}}
to construct its mirror by using an immersed Lagrangian [Seidel], succesfully covering HMS for elliptic and hyperbolic orbifolds.

Essentially, the technique adopted by [CHL] consists in fixing a reference Lagrangian,
\mathbb{L}
, and a space of odd-degree weakly unobstructed formal deformations
b
of
\mathbb{L}
. The mirror functor corresponds to a family of curved Yoneda embedding, constructed by using curved Floer theory between the family
(\mathbb{L},b)
and the other Lagrangians in the Fukaya category. For Lagrangian fibrations, the singular fibers and wall-corossing phenomenons make it very difficult to construct the family Floer functor in an explicit way.

On the other hand, [CHL] allows to start from an immersed Lagrangian, with no wall-crossing taking place.

[Khovanov-Seidel] introduced the idea of applying the Yoneda functor to the study of Fukaya categories. From Fukaya’s construction, there is an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-algebra,
{|calA}
, associated to a fixed collection of Lagrangians

\mathbb{L}\ \equiv\ \left\{L_{{}_{1}},...,L_{{}_{N}}\right\}

(917)

and there is a functor

D^{{}^{b}}\text{Fuk}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ D({\cal A}).

(918)

[CHL], instead: the non-commutative algebras in this paper are obtained from Maurer-Cartan solutions rather than the whole Hom
(\mathbb{L,L})
space,
11
.

Furthermore, this formalism extends to non-compact Lagrangians. This occurs in the case of punctured Riemann surfaces. Importantly, to achieve mirrors i sto consider weakly-unobstructed odd-degree deformations. Then, need to consider curved
\mathbb{Z}_{{}_{2}}
-graded Fukaya categories and categories of Matrix Factorisations in the mirror.

In this section, we will be dealing with the extension of the [CHL] formalism to the deformation space by noncommutative deformations. The reason to do so is two-fold:

•

First of all, the fact that the solution space of the weak MAurer-Cartan equations associated to
\mathbb{L}
in
12
might be trivial (the 0-vector space), as is needed in the case for a generic Lagrangian,
\mathbb{L}
. Nontriviality requires a certain symmetry on the moduli of holomorphic disks bounded by
\mathbb{L}
. Specifically, it requires that the counting of holomorphic disks through a point
p\in\mathbb{L}
is independent of the choice of
p
. Nontrivial solutions are thereby achieved by using noncommutative deformations. Foor example, the Seidel Lagrangian in a 3-punctured sphere is generically non-symmetric about the equator. There is no non-zero commutative solution to the Maurer-Cartan equation. On the other hand, the noncommutative solutions form the space of Sklyanin algebras

\frac{\mathbb{C}<x,y,z>}{<xy-ayx,yz-azy,zx-axz>}

(919)

as mirrors to the elliptic orbifold
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{1}}_{{}_{3,3,3}}
using noncommutative deformations.

•

The second reason is that of globalising the mirror construction: the mirror functor is localised to the Lagrangian immersion,
\mathbb{L}
, fixed in the beginning. Applying the mirror construction to a family of Lagrangian immersions, one can capture more about the global geometry. For the collection (
917
) , the End-algebra is a path algebra of a directed graph, and is naturally noncommutative.

Collections of Lagrangians can be achieved by means of dimer models in 1D, or skeletons of coamoebas in arbitrary dimensions.

16.1
The construction

The odd-degree Floer-theoretical endomorphisms of
\mathbb{L}
are described by a directed graph
Q
(a.k.a. quiver). The path algebra
\Lambda Q
is regarded as the noncommutative space of formal deformations of
\mathbb{L}
. Each edge
e
of
Q
corresponds to an odd-degree Floer generator
X_{{}_{e}}
and a formal dual variable
x_{{}_{e}}\in\Lambda Q
. Consider the deformation

b\ =\ \sum_{{}_{e}}x_{{}_{e}}X_{{}_{e}}

(920)

The obstruction is given by

\mu_{{}_{0}}^{{}^{b}}\ =\ \mu\left(e^{{}^{b}}\right)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{k\geq 0}}\mu_{{}_{k}}(b,...,b)

(921)

The new thing is that extending the notion of weakly-unobstructedness by FOOO to the noncommutative setting; the corresponding weak Maurer-Cartan equation is

\mu_{{}_{0}}^{{}^{b}}\ =\ \sum_{{}_{i=1}}{\cal W}_{{}_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{{}_{L_{{}_{i}}}}

(922)

where
\mathbf{1}_{{}_{L_{{}_{i}}}}
denotes the Floer-theoretical unit corresponding to the fundamental class of
L_{{}_{i}}
, assuming the Fukaya
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-category is unital.

The solution space is given by a quiver algebra with relations

{\cal A}\ =\ \Lambda Q/R

(923)

where
R
is the 2-sided ideal generated by weakly-unobstructed relations.

The end product is a noncommutative LG model

\left({\cal A}\ =\ {\cal W}\ =\ \sum_{{}_{i}}{\cal W}_{{}_{i}}\right)

(924)

This is referred to as a generalised mirror of
X
, in the sense that there exists a natural functor

\text{Fuk}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{NCMF}({\cal A,W})

(925)

By
generalised
here, we really mean in the following sense:

1.

It is a generalisation of the SYZ program, where Lagrangian tori are replaced by immersions.

2.

The functor needs not be an equivalence, so
({\cal A,W})
needs not be a mirror of
X
in the original sense.

Theorem 1.1

There exists an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-functor

{\cal F}^{{}^{\mathbb{L}}}:\ \text{Fuk}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{MF}({\cal A,W})

(926)

which is injective on
H^{{}^{\bullet}}(\text{Hom}(\mathbb{L},U))\ \forall U
. An important feature is that the LG superpotential,
{\cal W}
, constructed this way is automatically a central element in
{\cal A}
. In particular, we can make sense of
{\cal A}/<{\cal W}>
as a hypersurface singularity defined by the zero set of
{\cal W}
.

Theorem 1.2

{\cal W}\ \in\ {\cal A}
is a central element.

The noncommutative formal space dual to an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-algebra uses constructions by [Kontsevich, Soibelman]. The quiver algebra with relations,
{\cal A}
constructed by [CHL] can be regarded as a subvariety in the formal space (restricting to weakly-unobstructed odd-degree deformations). Moreover, obtaining a LG model instead of a CY algebra. Weakly-unobstructedness is crucial for the construction of the mirror functor. The resulting mirror is a curved dg-algebra. However, it is very difficult to compute in practice, since it involves all higher
\mu_{{}_{k}}
operations. An interesting feature of this setup is that we can achieve multiple mirrors to a single symplectic geometry,
X
. The construction of a LG mirror
({\cal A}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}},{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}})
and the associated mirror functor

{\cal F}^{{}^{\mathbb{L}}}:\ \text{Fuk}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{MF}({\cal A}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}},{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}})

(927)

depends on the choice of
\mathbb{L}\ \subset\ X
.

For a different choice of
\mathbb{L}^{{}^{\prime}}
, the mirror
\left({\cal A}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}^{{}^{\prime}},{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}^{{}^{\prime}}\right)
would be different, even though their B-model categories MF
({\cal A}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}},{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}})
and MF
\left({\cal A}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}^{{}^{\prime}},{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}^{{}^{\prime}}\right)
can remain equivalent. A similar phenomenon occurs in SYZ mirror symmetry for multiple Lagrangian fibrations.

Later on, we will see how to construct a continuous family of LG models
\left({\cal A}_{{}_{t}},{\cal W}_{{}_{t}}\right)
that are mirror to a single elliptic orbifold (with a fixed K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler structure).
18
18

18

Albeit there being applications beyond Mirror Symmetry, they will not be dealt with in this course.
Most importantly, their construction provides an interesting link between Lagrangian Floer theory and deformation quantisation in the sense of Kontsevich: take
\mathbb{L}
. The mirror
quiver algebra
{\cal A}
can be obtained from an exceptional collection of a variety
\tilde{X}
. Then, a commutative mirror,
(\hat{X},{\cal W})
is equivalent to the noncommutative model
({\cal A,W})
.

Take a defomation family,
\mathbb{L}_{{}_{t}}
, with
\mathbb{L}_{{}_{0}}\ \equiv\ \mathbb{L}
. The deformations can be obtained by equipping
\mathbb{L}
with flat
U(1)
-connection, or by deforming
\mathbb{L}
using non-exact closed 1-forms on
\mathbb{L}
. The mirror construction gives a family of non-commutative LG models
({\cal A}_{{}_{t}},{\cal W}_{{}_{t}})
. The NC hypersurface
{\cal A}_{{}_{t}}/{\cal W}_{{}_{t}}
should give deformation-quantisation of the hypersurface

\left\{{\cal W}=0\right\}\ \subset\ \hat{X}

(928)

Examples of this are for mirrors of elliptic orbifolds
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{1}}_{{}_{3,3,3}}
and
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{1}}_{{}_{2,2,2,2}}
.

The elliptic orbifold (discussed last time),

X\ =\ E/\mathbb{Z}_{{}_{3}}\ =\mathbb{P}^{{}^{1}}_{{}_{3,3,3}}

(929)

has a noncommutative LG mirror
(\mathbb{C}^{{}^{3}},{\cal W})
constructed by [CHL] by using the immersed Lagrangian,
\mathbb{L}
given by Seidel.
\mathbb{L}
is symmetric under any anti-symplectic involution on
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{1}}_{{}_{3,3,3}}
. This symmetry can be broken, and one could consider a deformation family of immersed Lagrangians
\mathbb{L}_{{}_{h}}\subset X
, obtaining
({\cal A}_{{}_{h}},{\cal W}_{{}_{h}})
as the mirror, where
{\cal A}_{{}_{h}}
is a Sklyanin algebra, and
{\cal W}_{{}_{h}}
is its central element.

For
h=0

\left({\cal A}_{{}_{0}}/{\cal W}_{{}_{0}}\right)\ =\ \left(\mathbb{C}[x,y,z]/{\cal W}\right)

(930)

we recover the commutative mirror.

The hypersurface
{\cal A}_{{}_{h}}/{\cal W}_{{}_{h}}
gives a deformation quantisation of the affine del Pezzo surface

\mathbb{C}[x,y,z]/{\cal W}

(931)

h
here denotes a deformation parameter belonging to a compact manifold
(S^{{}^{1}})^{{}^{2}}
.

One of the most extensively-used NC algebras is the Sklyanin algebra

{\cal A}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \frac{\Lambda<x,y,z>}{(axy+byz+cz^{{}^{2}},ayz+bzy+cx^{{}^{2}},azx+bxz+cy^{{}^{2}})}

(932)

for fixed
a,b,c,\ \in\ \mathbb{C}
. For
a=-b,c=0
,
{\cal A}=\mathbb{C}[x,y,z]
. As a graded algebra,
{\cal A}
gives a NC deformation of
\mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}
.

16.2
Noncommutative Mirror from a single Lagrangian and the Centrality Theorem

The first step involves extending the base of an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-algebra,
A
, to a noncommutative space. A key theorem, by [CHL], is that the potential function
{\cal W}
is automatically a central element.

For the mirror construction, the
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-algebra is taken to be the Floer chain complex of a single (immersed) Lagrabgian in a K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler manifold,
X
. Then, the base space together with the potential
{\cal W}
they construct is regarded as a NCLG model for
X
in a generalised sense. Such construction, therefore relates symplectic geometry of
X
with a NC AG pf
{|calW}
. However, such functor is not necessarily a categorical equivalence. The above construction is algebraic, implying it can be applied to any
A_{{}_{|infty}}
-caategory, not necessarily Fukaya categories.

An upgrade of this is by using finitely many Lagrangians (rather than a single one), and the corresponding base space will form a quiver algebra with relations. This procedure makes the generated mirror more global, and thereby capable of capturing more general information of
X
.

Take an immersed Lagrangian,
\mathbb{L}
, in a K
\ddot{\text{a}}
hler manifold,
X
,

\iota:\ \mathbb{L}\ \longrightarrow\ X

(933)

For
\mathbb{L}
a compact, spin oriented, unobstructed Lagrangian immersion with self-intersection points.
12
is associated with an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-algebra whose underlying vector space is the space of cochains

\displaystyle CF^{{}^{\bullet}}(\mathbb{L,L})

\displaystyle\overset{def.}{=}\ CF^{{}^{\bullet}}(\mathbb{L}\underset{\iota}{\times}\mathbb{L})

(934)

\displaystyle=\ C^{{}^{\bullet}}(\mathbb{L})\oplus\bigoplus_{{}_{p}}\text{Span}\left\{(p_{{}_{-}},p_{{}_{+}}),(p_{{}_{+}},p_{{}_{-}})\right\}

where
p
runs over immersed points,
p_{{}_{\pm}}
are its preimages under
\iota
, whereas
(p_{{}_{-}},p_{{}_{+}}),(p_{{}_{+}},p_{{}_{-}})
constitute complementary immersed generators.

The vector space is
\mathbb{Z}_{{}_{2}}
-graded (
\mathbb{Z}
-graded when
X
is CY, and
\mathbb{L}\ \subset\ X
has Maslov class equal to
0
). Consider the NC deformation by the immersed generators; let
\{X_{{}_{e}}\}
be the set of all odd-degree immersed generators in
CF^{{}^{\bullet}}(\mathbb{L,L})
,
e
running in a finite label set
E
of immersed points.

One can also include odd degree generators other than the immersed generator, but in this course we will not be dealing with it.

\{x_{{}_{e}}\}
denote formal dual variables. Defining

K\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \Lambda_{{}_{0}}<<x_{{}_{e}}:\ e\ \in\ E>>\ \ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \ \ \tilde{A}\ =\ K\ \underset{\Lambda_{{}_{0}}}{\hat{\bigotimes}}\ A

(935)

with
\tilde{A}
defining an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-algebra.

The formal bounding cochain

b\ =\ \sum_{{}_{e}}x_{{}_{e}}X_{{}_{e}}

(936)

represents a formal smoothing deformation of
\mathbb{L}
at each intersection point,
e
. This
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-algebra can be defined with
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-operations
\{\mu_{{}_{k}}^{{}^{b}}\}
, where
\mu_{{}_{k}}^{{}^{b}}
denotes the deformed
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-operation defined as follows

\displaystyle\mu_{{}_{K}}^{{}^{b}}(v_{{}_{1}},...,v_{{}_{k}})

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{{}_{l_{{}_{o}},...,l_{{}_{k}}\geq 0}}\mu_{{}_{k+l_{{}_{0}}+...+l_{{}_{k}}}}\left(b^{{}^{l_{{}_{o}}}}v_{{}_{1}},b^{{}^{l_{{}_{1}}}},v_{{}_{2}},...,v_{{}_{k}},b^{{}^{l_{{}_{k}}}}\right)

(937)

\displaystyle=\mu\left(e^{{}^{b}},v_{{}_{1}},e^{{}^{b}},v_{{}_{2}},...,e^{{}^{b}},v_{{}_{k}},e^{{}^{b}}\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ \ \ \mu_{{}_{K}}^{{}^{b_{{}_{0}},...,b_{{}_{k}}}}(v_{{}_{1}},...,v_{{}_{k}})

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{{}_{l_{{}_{o}},...,l_{{}_{k}}\geq 0}}\mu_{{}_{k+l_{{}_{0}}+...+l_{{}_{k}}}}\left(b^{{}^{l_{{}_{o}}}}v_{{}_{1}},b^{{}^{l_{{}_{1}}}},v_{{}_{2}},...,v_{{}_{k}},b^{{}^{l_{{}_{k}}}}\right)

(938)

\displaystyle=\mu\left(e^{{}^{b}},v_{{}_{1}},e^{{}^{b}},v_{-2},...,e^{{}^{b}},v_{{}_{k}},e^{{}^{b}}\right)

for
b
a Maurer-Cartan element.

The worldsheet potential

{\cal W}\ =\ {\cal W}_{{}_{\cal L}}\ \subset\ {\cal A}

(939)

is a generalisation of
X
w.r.t. the choice of an immersed Lagrangian,
\mathbb{L}
.
\mu_{{}_{0}}
counts the holomorphic disks bounded by
\mathbb{L}
passing through a generic point.
{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}
counts holomorphic disks bounded in
\mathbb{L}
passing through a generic point with
b
-boundary insertions. Holomorphic sisks are allowed to turn corners at
X_{{}_{e}}
, and the names of the corners are recorded in the variables
x_{{}_{e}}
.

Theorem
[CHL] (Centrality of the potential)

The noncommutative potential
{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}\ \in\ {\cal A}
is central, meaning

{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}\ \cdot\ x_{{}_{e}}\ =\ x_{{}_{e}}\ \cdot\ {\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \forall\ x_{{}_{e}}

(940)

Proof

\mu_{{}_{0}}^{{}^{b}}\ =\ {\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}\mathbf{1}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}

(941)

over
{\cal A}
.

\hat{\mu}
denotes the coderivation induced from
\mu
, implying

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\ \mu\ \circ\ \hat{\mu}\left(e^{{}^{b}}\right)\

(942)

\displaystyle=\ \mu\left(e^{{}^{b}},\hat{\mu}_{{}_{e}}^{{}^{(b)}},e^{{}^{b}}\right)

\displaystyle=\ \mu_{{}_{2}}\left(b,{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}\mathbf{1}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}\right)+\mu_{{}_{2}}\left({\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}\mathbb{1}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}},b\right)

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{{}_{e}}\left({\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}\cdot x_{{}_{e}}-x_{{}_{e}}\cdot{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}\right)\ X_{{}_{e}}

following from the property of
\mathbb{1}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}
.

Centrality is also required to ensure that

\left(\mu_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{b,b}}\right)^{{}^{2}}\ =\ 0

(943)

The
spacetime superpotential
is defined as follows

\Phi\ =\ \Phi_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \sum_{{}_{k}}\ \frac{1}{k+1}<\mu_{{}_{k}}(b,...,b),b>\ \in\ K/[K,K]

(944)

such that

{\cal A}\ =\ \text{Jac}(\Phi)\ =\ \Lambda_{{}_{0}}\left<\left<x_{{}_{e}}:\ e\in E\right>\right>\bigg/\left(\partial_{{}_{x_{{}_{e}}}}\Phi:\ e\in E\right)

(945)

A triple

\left(\Lambda_{{}_{0}}\left<\left<x_{{}_{e}}:\ e\in E\right>\right>,\Phi,{\cal W}\right)

(946)

is referred to as a
noncommutative
LG model if the noncommutative Jacobi algebra

{\cal A}\ =\ \text{Jac}(\Phi)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \Lambda_{{}_{0}}\left<\left<x_{{}_{e}}:\ e\in E\right>\right>\bigg/\left(\partial_{{}_{x_{{}_{e}}}}\Phi\right)

(947)

has
{\cal W}
in its center.

Corollary

Under the assumption that
\partial_{{}_{x_{{}_{e}}}}\Phi,\ \forall e\ \in\ E
are weakly-unobstructed relations, implying

{\cal A}\ =\ \text{Jac}(\Phi)

(948)

the generalised mirror

\left(\Lambda_{{}_{0}}\left<\left<x_{{}_{e}}:\ e\in E\right>\right>,\Phi,{\cal W}\right)

(949)

associated to
\mathbb{L}
is a NC LG model.

Next step is to transform Lagrangian submanifolds in
X
to MF
({\cal W})
. Symplectic geometry corresponds to NCAG in the generalised mirror.

16.2.1
The mirror functor from a single Lagrangian

Take MF
({\cal A,W})
equipped with two projective
{\cal A}
-modules,
M_{{}_{0}},M_{{}_{1}}
, with maps

\delta_{{}_{0}}:\ M_{{}_{0}}\ \longrightarrow\ M_{{}_{1}}\ \ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \delta_{{}_{1}}:\ M_{{}_{1}}\ \longrightarrow\ M_{{}_{0}}

(950)

such that

\delta_{{}_{0}}\delta_{{}_{1}}\ =\ \delta_{{}_{1}}\delta_{{}_{0}}\ =\ {\cal W}\cdot\ \text{Id}

(951)

or as a
\mathbb{Z}_{{}_{2}}
-graded projective
{\cal A}
-module

M\ =\ M_{{}_{0}}\ \oplus\ M_{{}_{1}}

(952)

with an odd endomorphism

\delta\ =\ \delta_{{}_{0}}\ +\ \delta_{{}_{1}}

(953)

such that

\delta^{{}^{2}}\ =\ {\cal W}\ \cdot\ \text{Id}

(954)

The differential graded category of MF
({\cal A,W})
is defined as follows.

A morphism between MF
(M,\delta_{{}_{M}})
and MF
(N,\delta_{{}_{N}})
is an
{\cal A}
-module homomorphism

f:\ M\ \longrightarrow\ N

(955)

The morphism space is
\mathbb{Z}_{{}_{2}}
-graded and equipped with a differential
mu_{{}_{1}}
or d defined by

\mu_{{}_{1}}(f)\ =\ \delta_{{}_{N}}\circ f-(-1)^{{}^{\text{deg}(f)}}f\circ\delta_{{}_{M}}

(956)

\mu_{{}_{2}}
denotes the composition of morphisms.

Denoting by Fuk
(X)
the
\mathbb{Z}_{{}_{2}}
-graded Fukaya category of unobstructed Lagrangian submanifolds of
X
. For an object
U
of Fuk
(X)
, its mirror MF
({\cal A,W})
is defined as follows

{\cal F}(U)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left({\cal A}\ \underset{\Lambda_{{}_{0}}}{\hat{\bigotimes}}\ CF^{{}^{\bullet}}((\mathbb{L},b),U),\text{d}\ =\ (-1)^{{}^{\text{deg}(.)}}\mu_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{b,0}}(.)\right)

(957)

There are different ways of realising Lagrangian Floer theory and
CF^{{}^{\bullet}}((\mathbb{L},b),U)
depends on the choice.

12
showed that
U
can have clean intersections with
\mathbb{L}
, implying
CF^{{}^{\bullet}}((\mathbb{L},b),U)
is the direct sum of the spaces of singular chains (or differential forms) in the intersections.

[Seidel] showed that if
U
transversally-intersects
\mathbb{L}
, then
CF^{{}^{\bullet}}((\mathbb{L},b),U)
is spanned by the intersection points.

Fixing only one of the choices to carry out the constructions, the operation of
\mu_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{b,0}}
on

{\cal A}\ \underset{\Lambda_{{}_{0}}}{\otimes}\ CF^{{}^{\bullet}}((\mathbb{L},b),U)

(958)

is

\mu_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{b,0}}(p)\ =\ \sum_{{}_{k=0}}^{{}^{\infty}}\mu_{{}_{k+1}}(b,...,b,p)\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ p\ \in\ CF^{{}^{\bullet}}((\mathbb{L},b),U)

(959)

The mirror MF counts pseudoholomorphic strips bounded by
\mathbb{L}
and
U
, where an arbitrary number of boundary intersection
b
’s are allowed along the upper boundary mapped in
\mathbb{L}
.

The correspondence between objects defined above extends to be an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-functor

{\cal F}\ =\ \left({\cal F}_{{}_{k}}\right)_{{}_{k\geq 1}}^{{}^{\infty}}

(960)

as follows. Given an intesection point,
q
between two Lagrangians,
U_{{}_{1}},U_{{}_{2}}
, in Fuk
(X)
, which can be regarded as a morphism from
U_{{}_{1}}
to
U_{{}_{2}}
, we have the morphism

(-1)^{{}^{\text{deg}(q)-1)(\text{deg}(.)-1)}}\mu_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{b,0,0}}(.,q):\ {\cal F}(U_{{}_{1}})\ \longrightarrow\ {\cal F}(U_{{}_{2}})

(961)

Given an intersection point,
p\in\mathbb{L}\cap U_{{}_{1}}
,

(-1)^{{}^{(\text{deg}(q)-1)(\text{deg}(p)-1)}}\mu_{{}_{2}}^{{}^{b,0,0}}(p,q)\ =\ (-1)^{{}^{(\text{deg}(q)-1)(\text{deg}(p)-1)}}\sum_{{}_{k=0}}^{{}^{\infty}}\mu_{{}_{k+2}}(b,...,b,p,q)

(962)

defining an element in the underlying module of
{\cal F}(U_{{}_{2}})
. This defines a map

{\cal F}_{{}_{1}}^{{}^{\mathbb{L}}}:\ \text{Hom}(U_{{}_{1}},U_{{}_{2}})\ \longrightarrow\ \text{Hom}\left({\cal F}(U_{{}_{1}},{\cal F}(U_{{}_{2}})\right)

(963)

where

\left(q_{{}_{1}},...,q_{{}_{l}}\right)\ \in\ \text{Hom}\left(U_{{}_{1}},U_{{}_{2}}\right)\ \otimes\ ...\ \otimes\ \text{Hom}\left(U_{{}_{l}},U_{{}_{l+1}}\right)

(964)

sending

p\ \in\ \mathbb{L}\cap U_{{}_{1}}\ \mapsto\ (-1)^{{}^{\epsilon}}\mu_{{}_{l+1}}^{{}^{b,0,...,0}}\left(p,q_{{}_{1}},...,q_{{}_{l}}\right)

(965)

with

\epsilon\ =\ \left(\text{deg}p-1\right)\left(\sum_{{}_{i=1}}^{{}^{l}}\left(\text{deg}q_{{}_{i}}-1\right)\right)

(966)

To prove that
{\cal F}
is actually an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
functor, it is essential to take the underlying ring to be

{\cal A}\ =\ \Lambda_{{}_{0}}\left<\left<x_{{}_{e}}:\ e\in E\right>\right>\bigg/\left<P_{{}_{f}}:\ f\ \in\ F\right>

(967)

rather than
\Lambda_{{}_{0}}
. Over this ring,

\mu_{{}_{0,\mathbb{L}}}^{{}^{b}}\ =\ {\cal W}\ \cdot\ \mathbf{1}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}

(968)

{\cal F}_{{}_{l}}\left(q_{{}_{1}},...,q_{{}_{l}}\right)
defined above are truly
{\cal A}
-module homomorphisms, since

\mu_{{}_{k+l+1}}\left(b,...,b,g\cdot p,q_{{}_{1}},...,q_{{}_{l}}\right)\ =\ g\cdot\mu_{{}_{k+l+1}}\left(b,...,b,p,q_{{}_{1}},...,q_{{}_{l}}\right)

(969)

16.3
Elliptic curves and deformation quantisation

Applying the technique outlined above to the elliptic curve quotient

E\bigg/\mathbb{Z}_{{}_{3}}\ =\ \mathbb{P}^{{}^{1}}_{{}_{3,3,3}}

(970)

to achieve a family of noncommutative mirrors. Considering a family of reference Lagrangians,
\left(\mathbb{L}_{{}_{t}},\lambda\right)
producing the mirror functor whose target is the noncommutative LG model of 3D Skyanin algebras with central elements.

Theorem
[CHL]

There is a
T^{{}^{2}}
-family
(\mathbb{L},\lambda)
of Lagrangians decorated by flat
U(1)
-connections in
\mathbb{P}_{{}_{3,3,3}}^{{}^{1}}
for
(-1)\ \in\ \mathbb{R}/2\mathbb{Z}
and
\lambda\in U(1)
whose corresponding generated mirror

\left({\cal A}_{{}_{(\lambda,t)}},{\cal W}_{{}_{(\lambda,t)}}\right)

(971)

satisfies the following:

1.

The noncommutative algebras are Sklyanin algebras of the form

{\cal A}_{{}_{(\lambda,t)}}\ =\ \frac{\Lambda\ <x,y,z>}{(axy+byx+cz^{{}^{2}},ayz+bzy+cx^{{}^{2}},azx+bxz+cy^{{}^{2}})}

(972)

2.

{\cal W}_{{}_{(\lambda,t)}}
lies in the center of
{\cal A}_{{}_{(\lambda,t)}}\ \ \ \forall(\lambda,t)
.

3.

The coefficients
(a:b:c)
are given by theta-functions defining an embedding

E_{{}_{\tau}}\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}

(973)

onto the mirror elliptic curve

\hat{E}\ =\ \left\{(a:b:c)\ \in\ \mathbb{P}^{{}^{2}}\ \bigg|\ {\cal W}_{{}_{0}}(x,y,z)\ =\ 0\right\}

(974)

4.

\forall\ (\lambda,t)
, there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{{}_{2}}
-graded
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-functor

{\cal F}^{{}^{(\mathbb{L},t)}}:\ \text{Fuk}\left(\mathbb{P}_{{}_{3,3,3}}^{{}^{1}}\right)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{MF}\left({\cal A}_{{}_{(\lambda,t)}},{\cal W}_{{}_{(\lambda,t)}}\right)

(975)

5.

The graded noncommutative algebra

{\cal A}_{{}_{(\lambda,t)}}\bigg/\left<{\cal W}_{{}_{(\lambda,t)}}\right>

(976)

near
t=0,\lambda=-1
gives a quantisation of the affine del Pezzo surface defined by

{\cal W}_{{}_{0}}(x,y,z)\ =\ 0

(977)

in
\mathbb{C}^{{}^{3}}
.

Proposition
[CHL]

The noncommutative LG superpotential for
(\mathbb{L}_{{}_{t}},\lambda)

{\cal W}_{{}_{(\lambda,t)}}\ \in\ \text{Sky}(a(\lambda,t),b(\lambda,t),c(\lambda,t))

(978)

is given by

{\cal W}_{{}_{(\lambda,t)}}\ =\ -\frac{q_{{}_{0}}^{{}^{(3t+1)^{{}^{2}}}\lambda^{{}^{(1+3t)}/2}}}{6\pi\ i}\left(a^{{}^{\prime}}(u)(xyz+zxy+yzx)+b^{{}^{\prime}}(u)(zyx+xzy+yxz)+c^{{}^{\prime}}(u)\left(x^{{}^{3}}+y^{{}^{3}}+z^{{}^{3}}\right)\right)

(979)

where

q_{{}_{0}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \exp\left(-\omega_{{}_{\text{min}}}\right)

(980)

with
\omega_{{}_{\text{min}}}
denoting the symplectic area of the minimum triangle.

Corollary
[CHL]

{\cal W}_{{}_{(\lambda,t)}}
as given above is a central element of Sky
{}_{{}_{3}}(a,b,c)
.

16.3.1
Relation to quantisation of an affine del Pezzo surface

Elliptic curves and deformation quantisation

16.4
Noncommutative Mirror from multiple Lagrangians

Starting from a reference Lagrangian immersion

\mathbb{L}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left(L_{{}_{1}},...,L_{{}_{k}}\right)

(981)

e.g,
\mathbb{L}
could be a set of generators of the Fukaya category. The construction leads to a quiver
Q
with relations
R
, together with a central element
{\cal W}
of the quiver algebra with relations
\Lambda Q/R
.

Under certain conditions,
R
can be obtained as partial derivatives of a cyclic element
\Phi
in the path algebra
\Lambda Q
. Regarding

(\Lambda Q/{\cal W})

(982)

as a generalised mirror of
X
, in the sense that there exists a natural functor

\text{Fuk}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{MF}(\Lambda Q/{\cal W})

(983)

The quiver
Q\equiv Q^{{}^{\mathbb{L}}}
is a finite oriented graph, whose varieties correspond to elements in
\mathbb{L}
, and whose arrows correspond to odd-degree immersed generators of
\mathbb{L}
.

The path algebra of
Q
can be taken to be the base of the Fukaya subcategory,
\mathbb{L}
. The counting of holomorphic disks passing through a generic marked point on each member of
\mathbb{L}
defines
\{{\cal W}_{{}_{i}}\}

{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \sum_{{}_{i}}{\cal W}_{{}_{i}}\ \in\ \Lambda Q/R

(984)

As we will see,
{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}
lies in the center of
\Lambda Q/R
.

Path algebra and the semi-simple ring

Assuming any two Lagrangians intersect transversally, and that

\bigcup_{{}_{1\leq i\leq\ k}}\ L_{{}_{i}}

(985)

to be an immersed Lagrangian.

Denoting by

Q\ =\ Q^{{}^{\mathbb{L}}}

(986)

a graph such that each vertex
v_{{}_{i}}
of
Q
corresponds to a Lagrangian
L_{{}_{i}}\in\mathbb{L}
,

Q_{{}_{0}}^{{}^{\mathbb{L}}}\ \equiv\ \{v_{{}_{1}},...,v_{{}_{k}}\}

(987)

defines the vertex set.

Each arrow
v_{{}_{i}}\rightarrow v_{{}_{j}}
corresponds to odd-degree Floer generator in
CF^{{}^{\bullet}}(L_{{}_{i}},L_{{}_{j}})
, namely an intersection point between
L_{{}_{i}}
and
L_{{}_{j}}
. If
i=j
, we get loops at
v_{{}_{i}}
corresponding t oodd-degree immersed generators of
L_{{}_{i}}
. From the construction above,
Q
corresponds to the endomorphism quiver of
\mathbb{L}
.

For a quiver
Q
with a given set of vertices,
Q_{{}_{0}}
, andd arrows,
Q_{{}_{1}}
, we have maps

h,t:\ Q_{{}_{1}}\ \longrightarrow\ Q_{{}_{0}}

(988)

assigning head and tail vertices to each arrow, respectively.

Each vertex
v
corresponds to a trivial path
\pi_{{}_{v}}
of length 0. A sequence of arrows

p\ =\ a_{{}_{k-1}}a_{{}_{k-2}}...a_{{}_{1}}a_{{}_{0}}

(989)

is said to be a path of length
k
if

h(a_{{}_{i}})\ =\ t(a_{{}_{i+1}})\ \ \forall i

(990)

Setting

h\ \overset{def.}{=}\ h(a_{{}_{k-1}})\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ t(p)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ t(a_{{}_{0}})

(991)

p
is said to be a cyclic path if

h(p)\ =\ t(p)

(992)

Two paths,
p,q
, can be concatenated if

t(q)\ =\ h(p)

(993)

to get the path
pq
. Otherwise

pq\ =\ 0

(994)

A path algebra
\Lambda_{{}_{0}}Q
over the ring
\Lambda_{{}_{0}}
for a quiver
Q
Q is a
\Lambda_{{}_{0}}
-module spanned by paths in
Q
. The product is defined by linear extension of the concatenation of path.

We can set

\Lambda\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \Lambda\ \underset{\Lambda_{{}_{0}}}{\otimes}\ \Lambda_{{}_{0}}Q

(995)

Each vertex,
v
, corresponds to a trivial path,
\pi_{{}_{v}}
, of 0-length.

For a vertex
v
,
\pi_{{}_{v}}
is an idempotent in
\Lambda Q^{{}^{\mathbb{L}}}
. It generates a semi-simple ring
\Lambda^{{}^{\oplus}}
defined as follows

\Lambda^{{}^{\oplus}}\ =\ \Lambda\pi_{{}_{1}}\ \oplus\ ...\ \oplus\ \Lambda\ \pi_{{}_{k}}

(996)

with

\pi_{{}_{i}}\cdot\ \pi_{{}_{i}}\ =\ \pi_{{}_{i}}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \pi_{{}_{i}}\cdot\ \pi_{{}_{j}}\ =\ 0\ \ \text{if}\ \ i\neq j

(997)

Since an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-category is an
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-algebra over
\Lambda^{{}^{\oplus}}
,

{\cal C}\ =\ {\cal F}(\mathbb{L,L})\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \bigoplus_{{}_{i,j}}{\cal F}(L_{{}_{i}},L_{{}_{j}})

(998)

where

{\cal F}(L_{{}_{i}},L_{{}_{j}})\ =\ \begin{cases}&CF^{{}^{\bullet}}(L_{{}_{i}}\underset{\iota}{\times}L_{{}_{i}})\ \ \ \text{if}\ \ \ i=j\\
&\text{Span}_{{}_{\Lambda}}(L_{{}_{i}}\cap L_{{}_{j}})\ \ \ \text{if}\ \ \ i\neq j\\
\end{cases}

(999)

and module structure given by

\pi_{{}_{i}}\ \cdot\ {\cal C}\ \cdot\ \pi_{{}_{j}}\ =\ {\cal F}(L_{{}_{i}},L_{{}_{j}})

(1000)

\Rightarrow\ \ \ {\cal C}^{{}^{\otimes r}}\ =\ \bigoplus_{{}_{i_{{}_{1}},...,i_{{}_{k}}}}{\cal F}\left(L_{{}_{i_{{}_{1}}}},L_{{}_{i_{{}_{2}}}}\right)\ \underset{\Lambda}{\otimes}...\underset{\Lambda}{\otimes}{\cal F}\left(L_{{}_{i_{{}_{k-1}}}},L_{{}_{i_{{}_{k}}}}\right)

(1001)

{\cal C}
is said to be unital if each
CF^{{}^{\bullet}}(L_{{}_{i}},L_{{}_{i}})
has a unit
\mathbb{1}_{{}_{L_{{}_{i}}}}
, and

\mathbb{1}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \bigoplus_{{}_{i}}\ \mathbb{1}_{{}_{L_{{}_{i}}}}

(1002)

is a unit of
{\cal C}
.

Mirror construction

Take the obstruction to be

b\ =\ \sum_{{}_{e}}x_{{}_{e}}X_{{}_{e}}\ \ \in\ \ \tilde{A}^{{}^{\mathbb{L}}}

(1003)

where

\tilde{A}^{{}^{\mathbb{L}}}\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \Lambda Q^{{}^{\mathbb{L}}}\ \underset{\Lambda^{{}^{\oplus}}}{\hat{\otimes}}\ CF(\mathbb{L,L})

(1004)

x_{{}_{e}}
is the formal variable in
\Lambda Q
associated to each arrow
e
of
Q
, whereas
X_{{}_{e}}
denotes the corresponding odd-degree immersed generators in
CF(\mathbb{L,L})
. Assuming

\text{deg}x_{{}_{e}}\ =\ 1-\ \text{deg}X_{{}_{e}}

(1005)

such that deg
b
=1. Then, the Maurer-Cartan relations read

\mu_{{}_{0}}^{{}^{b}}\ =\ \mu(e^{{}^{b}})\ =\ \sum_{{}_{i}}{\cal W}_{{}_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{{}_{L_{{}_{i}}}}+\sum_{{}_{f}}P_{{}_{f}}X_{{}_{f}}

(1006)

where the coefficients
P_{{}_{f}}
are associated to the even-degree generators
X_{{}_{f}}
of
CF(\mathbb{L,L})
.

The generalised mirror of
X
reads

\left({\cal A},{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}\right)

(1007)

.

Analogously to the case of the single Lagrangian immersion,
{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}
is central to
{\cal A}
.

Under suitable conditions,

{\cal A}\ =\ \text{Jac}(Q,\Phi)\ =\ \frac{\Lambda Q}{(\partial_{{}_{x_{{}_{e}}}}\Phi:\ e\in E)}

(1008)

with
\Phi
the spacetime superpotential.

The mirror functor reads

{\cal F}\ =\ {\cal F}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}:\ \text{Fuk}(X)\ \longrightarrow\ \text{MF}\left(\text{Jac}\left(Q^{{}^{\mathbb{L}}},\Phi_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}\right);{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}\right)

(1009)

The construction of such
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-functor follows in a very similar way to that of a single Lagrangian immersion, apart from the fact that now we work over the semi-simple ring
\Lambda^{{}^{\oplus}}
. Taking an unobstructed Lagrangian
U

{\cal F}(U)\ \overset{def.}{=}\ \left({\cal A}\underset{\Lambda^{{}^{\oplus}}}{\otimes}\ CF(\mathbb{L},U),\delta\right)\ \simeq\ \left(\bigoplus_{{}_{i}}\bigoplus_{{}_{v\in L_{{}_{i}}\cap U}}{\cal A}\cdot\pi_{{}_{i}},\delta\right)

(1010)

Proposition
[CHL]

{\cal F}(U)
as defined above is a MF of
({\cal A},{\cal W}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}})
, namely

\delta^{{}^{2}}\ =\ {\cal W}\ \cdot\ \text{Id}

(1011)

Theorem
[CHL]

If the
A_{{}_{\infty}}
-category is unital, then
{\cal F}_{{}_{\mathbb{L}}}
is injective on
H^{{}^{\bullet}}(\text{Hom}(\mathbb{L},U))\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall U
.
